



About the Contributors
Gianmario Borio earned his Ph.D. in musicology studying under Carl Dahlhaus at the Technische Universität Berlin and is now professor of musicology at the University of Pavia and director of the Institute of Music at the Fondazione Giorgio Cini in Venice. His numerous publications deal with several aspects of composition in the twentieth century, music theory, and aesthetics. He is currently working on a book project on German theory of musical form in the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.
Simone Caputo completed his Ph.D. in music history at the Sapienza University of Rome with his dissertation “The Requiem Mass in the Musical Intonations from the Sixteenth to the Nineteenth Centuries.” He collaborates with the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi (Quaderni dell’Archivio Scelsi) and Nuova Consonanza. Caputo is research fellow at the Centro di Musica Antica, Fondazione Pietà de’ Turchini, in Naples.
Friedrich Jaecker studied composition with György Ligeti at the Hamburg Conservatory of Music and Musicology, University of Hamburg. Author of several prizewinning compositions and numerous essays on contemporary music, and Scelsi in particular, Jaecker is professor of composition at the Hochschule für Muzik und Tanz Köln.
Michelle Biget-Mainfroy, pianist and musicologist, is author of La geste pianistique: Essai sur l’ écriture du piano entre 1800 et 1930 (1987) and Le rêve et la fureur: Les écritures musicales romantiques (2002). Biget-Mainfroy is professor of musicology at the Université François-Rebelais in Tours.
Sandro Marrocu graduated in piano performance from the Conservatory of Turin and in musicology from the University of Turin. In 2009 he obtained a scholarship to pursue a Ph.D. in musicology at the University of Rome Tor Vergata. His research project, under the direction of professors Mario Baroni and Giorgio Sanguinetti, focuses on the creative relationship between Giacinto Scelsi and Vieri Tosatti.
Luciano Martinis, artist, painter, and sculptor, has been Giacinto Scelsi’s friend and confidant for many years. In publishing Scelsi’s writings and drawings in Le parole gelate, Martinis became a pioneer in his field. He is currently senior vice president of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi and editor-in-chief of its periodical, i suoni, le onde.
Alessandra Montali obtained a diploma in piano performance and a Ph.D. in musicology from the University of Bologna with her thesis on “The Time Perception in Music.” Active as lecturer in composition techniques at the conservatories of Bari, Bergamo, and Pesaro, she has published various essays and the volume Ascoltare il tempo (2008).
Alessandra Carlotta Pellegrini, one of Italy’s foremost musicologists and twentieth-century-music specialists, has written on Casella, Nono, Petrassi, and Scelsi, among others. She has edited both Italian and French editions of Giacinto Scelsi’s autobiography Il sogno 101 (both published in 2010). Pellegrini is scientific director of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi in Rome.
Franco Sciannameo is professor and associate dean at the College of Fine Arts, Carnegie Mellon University, in Pittsburgh. He is author of Nino Rota, Federico Fellini and the Making of an Italian Cinematic Folk Opera: Amarcord (2005); Giuseppe Mazzini’s Philosophy of Music: Envisioning a Social Opera (1836) (2005); Nino Rota’s The Godfather Trilogy (2010), and Phil Trajetta (1777–1854), Patriot, Musician, Immigrant (2010).
Guido Zaccagnini is professor of music history at the Conservatorio di Musica in Perugia. He has translated into Italian Charles Rosen’s The Romantic Generation (1997) and Maynard Solomon’s Beethoven (1998), in addition to writing a monograph on Berlioz, Hector en Italie (2002). As a pianist he has performed and recorded the complete piano music of Friedrich Nietzsche.








Acknowledgments
We extend our heartfelt thanks to each of the chapter authors for their masterful work and to the individuals, institutions of higher learning, organizations, and places of business listed below.
First and foremost we express our most sincere thanks to Nicola Sani, president of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi, for his stalwart and enlightened advocacy on behalf of the music of our time and of Giacinto Scelsi in particular. We salute Luciano Martinis, senior vice president of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi, for his generous availability as an ironclad reference for scholars and followers of Scelsi; Mauro Tosti Croce, who as superintendent of archives for Italy’s Italian Ministero per i Beni e le Attività Culturali and coordinator of historical archives at the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi, has become the indispensable anchor for those embarking upon archival work; Nelly Quérol, directeur général of Editions Salabert, Paris, for fostering a propitious collaboration between scientific research and practical musical editions; Colette Jousse Wilkins and Donald Wilkins for dedicating their precious time to the revision of the translations from French to English; Alison D’Addieco for her invaluable assistance in formatting and editing; Nathan Hall for setting the numerous musical examples in chapters 2 and 6; Simone Caputo for compiling the index; the staff and colleagues of the College of Fine Arts at Carnegie Mellon University for their constant support; John Shepard, director of the Jean Gray Hargrove Music Library of the University of California–Berkeley, for granting permission to publish the Scelsi/Klein correspondence; and Editions Salabert, Paris, for permission to quote from Scelsi’s works.
Furthermore, we thank all those who in various capacities have worked at the newly created Archivio Scelsi. Their prismatic research methodologies and the opportunities they offered for discussion have constantly inspired and enriched our work.
Finally, we offer thanks to Bennett Graff, senior acquisitions editor at Scarecrow Press, for spearheading the realization of this volume and for the exemplary professionalism with which he has guided us throughout the publication process.








Discography
Simone Caputo
The discography of Giacinto Scelsi is divided in two sections: Dedicated Recordings and Collections. The former presents the music albums entirely dedicated to works by Scelsi, while the latter lists miscellaneous music collections containing just one or more works by Scelsi. The records are arranged following an ascending chronological order, according to the criteria formerly adopted for the essays in this book. The titles of the works are reported as indicated on each music album. The main sources used to compile the discography follow.

Archivio della Fondazione Isabella Scelsi
I suoni, le onde… : Rivista della Fondazione Scelsi
Giacinto Scelsi 1905–1988 (catalogue des éditions Salabert, 2005), discography edited by Sharon Kanach and Fabio Carboni
Scelsi: Discography, edited by Todd M. McComb, http://www.medieval.org/music/modern/scelsi/discs.html
online catalogs of record labels, discographic databases

1

Is Composing a Cooperative Effort?
A Historical Roundtable Discussion
Guido Zaccagnini
Imet Giacinto Scelsi some ten years ago in his splendid home overlooking the Palatine Hill. There I listened to his many stories about acquaintances in high places (Dalì, Éluard), about anthroposophy, about women, and about his way of composing music. Yes! “His way” of composing music. Our group, Spettro Sonoro, had in its repertoire some works by Scelsi—Pranam II, Pwill, Tre pezzi per trombone, Khoom. Therefore, consulting with the author, learning about his expressive intentions and his compositional process, represented a fundamental opportunity for me to tap into a powerful performance. But it was not to be: Scelsi spoke at length about the fact that, in reality, he was only a go-between in the earthly world, that a “Superior Compositional Instance” instructed him to sit at the keyboard (piano, ondioline, or organ) and, after a phase of concentration, play what he perceived internally, then tape record everything and transcribe it to sheet music. It was probably because of my inability to decode such messages that the result was the opposite of what I had hoped for. The discourse on or about Scelsi, as far as I was concerned, had come to an end.
On several occasions afterward, I participated in discussions involving Scelsi: for example, at the Festival Pontino of 1986, when in answer to Walter Zimmermann’s complaint about Scelsi’s absence someone said that such a void was essentially insignificant; or in 1987 when, in the course of a meeting of the association Nuova Consonanza, I objected to programming a full monographic evening to Scelsi, like those planned for Stravinski, Stockhausen, and Cage.
Today, after the revelation that a good portion of Scelsi’s music was, in reality, written by Vieri Tosatti—though how many in the Roman musical world ignored this?—the controversy has been reignited. However, rather than conduct a post-humous trial (Parce sepulto), Tosatti’s intervention ought to be useful in focusing on contemporary compositional methodologies. Therefore I have prepared some questions for a group of composers, organizers, and musicologists. I will pose three questions that, even if not directly related to “the Scelsi case,” touch somehow on the problem emanating from the recent querelle.
FIRST QUESTION
Could the practice of improvisation, that in our century reached its golden moment in the 1960s, still be considered (if it ever was) a valid compositional system?

JOHN CAGE: Yes.
MARIO BORTOLOTTO: It is a matter of fact that by improvising one is destined to reproduce formulas or manners already utilized. At most, such a summation could be valuable according to the interpreter’s skills, but above all in the New Music—where essentially one did what one wanted to do—no improvisation has ever risen to the level of a compositional system.
CAMILLO TOGNI: As far as I am concerned, few things are further from my thinking than improvisation, because in the process of conceiving a sound, a piece, I am slower than anybody. But I believe that for a musician like Stock-hausen such a method could become quite useful. It all depends on the character and poetics of the musician.
GIORGIO VIDUSSO [partially along the same lines, considering that within the tenets of contemporary music everything is legitimate]: Many among today’s pieces oscillate between the most absolute alea—an echo of the romantic Urschrei—and the most forceful calculation—left over from Pythagorean matrixes—which, at any rate, do not always offer the sense of a utilized parametration. It is always clear when music is the fruit of improvisation or calculation, while, on the other hand, there is no performer who would not be remotely tempted by improvisation. However, in Scelsi’s case it is a different story: he was in the lion’s position, according to a Plotinian concept, concerning the divine message. Thus for a laic like myself it is difficult to accept such a thesis.
FRANCO DONATONI: Vidusso is partly right about the comprehension in listening to certain compositions, but I think that when the writing is controlled, if one could not perhaps ascertain the codes, one could perhaps understand that a code exists and therefore could evaluate its functionality. Regarding improvisation, I don’t think it has anything to do with craft methodology, which, for me, is the best method to define a composition.
ALDO CLEMENTI: Improvisation, even in its most primitive manifestation, cannot fail to consider a minimal knowledge of music, and it must leave unex-pressed even a miniscule starting plot.
GINO MARINUZZI [Nino Rota’s frequent collaborator, takes a radical position]: I am as against considering improvisation as a composition system, as I am against any aleatoric form of music.
SYLVANO BUSSOTTI: A question like this posed to someone like me, who earns his daily bread by practicing improvisation—not only in musical matters—would sound much more provocative than the worst offense.
PAOLO CASTALDI [approves of improvisation only in jazz music, stretching it to live electronics]: In a way, to see a composer working live at an electronic instrument is like observing a bartender mixing drinks at random. On the other hand, writing seventy minutes of music in a seventy-minute period of time could no doubt be profitable for composers like Nono and Stockhausen, for the publishers, and for SIAE [Società Italiana degli Autori ed Editori, or the Italian Authors’ and Publishers’ Association], but certainly it will be detrimental to the work’s poetic value and to those who care about quality.
SALVATORE SCIARRINO: It makes sense to talk about improvisation if it is linked to precise cultural limits. Twenty years ago the use of such a term was euphemistic for a different aspiration, perhaps 1968-ish, about freedom from rules, constrictions, serialism… It was a terminological and conceptual error—more than a creative method, because one could not really speak of a system. It would have been better to define it as spring-like, spontaneous. In the end, such a praxis was borrowed from Eastern cultures, even if with a bit of difference: in India, for example, one must study for twenty or thirty years before improvising a phrase.
GUIDO TURCHI: In fact, the problem of improvisation, like that of serialism, is an old one. Who would have an interest nowadays in looking for skeletons in the closet?
HEINZ KLAUS METZGER [answers in a more articulate fashion]: I am both in favor of and against improvisation. In favor because the problem of the musical thought’s real time can be solved only through improvisation. The fact that one has to work three months to produce three minutes of music is, for a composer, an unbearable contradiction. On the other hand, I am against it because since the time of Guido d’Arezzo’s notational reform music must be written down: any type of regression must therefore be condemned. What counts is the score. Scelsi solved this question in a dialectic—even if perhaps he would not have liked this word—fashion. Beginning with improvisational data, he recovered notation by transcribing or letting others transcribe—which amounts to the same thing—on paper the sounds he had thought of. There is no doubt, though, that in order to perform his music one must use the written scores, not his recorded improvisations.
MICHELE DALL’Ongaro [his reply closer to Bortolotto’s]: Improvisation has a strong propaedeutic power but offers few chances for strategy and speculation, and for all we admire and utilize in the masterworks of Western tradition.

SECOND QUESTION
Aside from the Scelsi-Tosatti case, one cannot forget the legions of “slaves” employed by Carl Czerny or, in more recent times, the contribution of Gino Marinuzzi (as already mentioned) to the music of Rota, the collaborations of Ivan Vandor and Francesco Carluccio to the scores of Luciano Berio, and, in the case of Carré, the work Cornelius Cardew did for Karlheinz Stockhausen. Therefore, is it possible, just, and justifiable to speak of workshops, perhaps in the Renaissance sense of the word, when next to the master there is the apprentice? More concretely, is the hidden handwork within either serious music or commercial music an undisputed fact, plausible, if not efficient from a didactic standpoint? Is it a diehard practice even in its lowest artistic and economic degree?

VIERI TOSATTI [author of the revelations that generated this debate]: It is not a scandal that a rich man can afford a “slave.” The problem occurs when the times we live in offer too much space for mystification. [In this sense, Tosatti seems to agree with Vidusso concerning the ambiguity of certain recent scores.]
MARINUZZI: The problem of the “hidden handwork” doesn’t exist. I have worked in that fashion quite a bit with a very positive economic advantage.… My experience with Rota has been decisively positive because our personalities were too divergent to be confused. Whoever has sufficient personality sooner or later emerges.
TOSATTI: But such an argument does not stand. I, regarding Scelsi’s music, acted like a gynecologist who, once home, looks at his own wife in a different way.
VIDUSSO [who regarded the question as ill-conceived or, better yet, as generated from a romantic premise he does not approve of ]: I believe that one can confidently affirm that in the presence of linguistic conventions or in well-solidified structural projects, the work of coauthors, transcribers, and orchestrators is at times superior to the original. But the example of Stockhausen’s Carré is different; in Carré he had traced a path, thus defining the score, leaving to Cardew only the graphic realization.
METZGER [in full agreement with Vidusso]: Carré’s conception is absolutely Stockhausen’s. In fact, Stockhausen’s actual activity can be considered a workshop. [Metzger seems to believe more in a renaissance of the workshops than the survival of schools of composition.] The last real school of composition was Schönberg’s.We cannot speak of schools but rather of movements concerning those composers who, thirty years ago, had been fascinated by the masterpieces of Boulez and Stockhausen.
CAGE [with a less laconic answer, this time]: Working with a good composer, independently of the outcome, is always an opportunity to take advantage of.
DALL’ONGARO [substantially skeptical regarding Metzger’s hopes and opinions]: Undoubtedly, this is true. The real workshops are practically closed to those who wish to participate. What flourishes instead is a myriad of false mini workshops—summer courses through which, over the span of a couple of weeks and after paying a sum, one becomes a composer, with a suntan to boot, since it is the month of August. In terms of acquiring a “working practice,” young composers are on their own. For applied music the story is different. There is enough work for everybody. In the United States, though, a certain division of labor is an integral part of the production system. In fact, this is obvious in a film’s credits: music, theme, orchestration, musical supervision by so and so, etc. In Italy, often the first three categories employ “slaves.” But the situation worsens when one finds the names of four or five other people “miraculously” appearing in one’s own royalty statements.
SCIARRINO [whose experience should also be read following Vidusso’s and Metzger’s words]: In my case, I did employ assistants, young pupils of mine, whom I have entrusted with mechanical work—copying or refining the layout of graphic scores. I wish to add that in olden times the copyist’s work represented the initial phase of the apprenticeship. Now, instead, the “black-market work” is connected with revenues: when music generates revenues (like film music) the “slaves” are automatically created.
BUSSOTTI [in agreement with Sciarrino, enriches his intervention with an autobiographical note]: Whoever should venture through the wolf-guarded gates of my home could find in its many rooms some young amanuenses involved in some kind of work. Looking carefully at certain of my recent and not-so-recent publications, one may find written there, in very fine print, the name of some collaborator or other. I, for a mere plate of lentils, edited once, from beginning to end, the published score of Electre by Henry Pousser, and I had a hard time convincing the publisher, Universal, to print my name on the score in the smallest print characters available.
CASTALDI [defends the concept of the workshop, expanding it to painting]: Raphael learned everything from the school of Perugino. At one time the workshop represented a rite of passage meant as an artistic workshop in the form of mutual exchange. But even today something has still survived, like the architectural atelier of Philip Johnson.
DONATONI [in complete opposition to Castaldi]: It no longer makes sense to speak of masters and workshops. If anything one should speak of “launching a praxis.” The world of today does not allow an initiation to rigor, though. Let’s say that we are living in a modest, easy-going phase. For example, I consider myself a teacher, a docent, an assistant, a transmitter of my personal experience. If someone wishes to parade himself as a maestro, so be it. Furthermore, I don’t see the problem with the “slaves”: it is simply a matter of compromise, an economic relationship between two individuals.
CLEMENTI [whose answer is based on glossing over Donatoni’s]: We must distinguish the famous, very mundanely busy musician who uses the services of the unemployed colleague in need, from the analphabet who needs the professional’s help because of his total lack of musical knowledge. Furthermore, there are four categories of composers to deal with: the professional (Beethoven, Verdi, Armstrong, etc.), the dilettante (who writes badly but writes), the one who plays by ear (who does not write but plays), and the analphabet (who neither writes nor plays but who emits).
BORTOLOTTO [like Castaldi is of the opinion that the history of the visual arts, more than music, offers some examples of the evolution occurring between master and apprentice]: “From filling in a heavenly background to drawing a calf, thus concluding the apprenticeship period by painting the outline of a face.” And in literature we should not forget certain very glorious uses of “slaves,” like in the case of Dumas, who used Maquet in writing even The Three Musketeers. But aside from the master-pupil relationship, its implications and results—just think of Schönberg’s revisions and corrections to Alban Berg’s Sonata, op.1—the crux of the problem resides in the quantity of the work the “helping hand” is entrusted with. If his intervention is a decisive one, one cannot but speak of collaboration. Evidently things change if, because of one’s incompetence, the entire task of a work’s realization is entrusted to someone else. Out of the question is the case where such a realization becomes necessary by the will of destiny, as in the case of Mozart’s Requiem.

THIRD QUESTION
The argument has moved a little toward the implications between the hierarchic positions of master and pupil. The study period under a particular composer, even one with a strong personality, should not induce the pupil to emulate the master. However, are there instances during the past thirty years in which a pupil writes music in the name of the master or… for the master?

VIDUSSO: This is an absolutely subjective problem. As in every activity, there are imitating monkeys everywhere. In our musical circle just think of Vincenzo Vitale’s piano students: none of them can escape the stamp of the master, if in different measure. But, to return to Scelsi, let me say that the fact that he is considered one of the major composers of our time by a large portion of French and German musicians—notwithstanding the notion that the deciphering of his cryptic messages has been entrusted to other people’s writing skills—seems to be particularly salient fact. I think that the cult of Scelsi’s music should be transferred to Tosatti.
TURCHI [is also in agreement with the idea that the argument should include the interpreters]: There are many, too many, say, violinists who consider themselves to be the “executors of the master’s will.” But the problem of the pupil imitating the master is a very old one; after all, even Mozart stole here and there from everywhere but then wrote what he wrote. But, now, let’s leave these examples for now. Chronicles are full of epigones, and some of them do not deserve be discarded. In sum, I would say that if you lack autonomous capacity, then imitate, so long as you do it well.
DALL’ONGARO [offering completely different advice]: I could spend entire days cutting little pieces of canvas making Fontanas, perhaps producing lousy work, but still by Lucio Fontana. It always happens when dealing with objects endowed with too much personality. Try to make a tenor howl a high note; no matter the content, it will always sound like a haughty personage desperately crying out for a lost love (or enjoying the finding of a new one—your choice). It seems to me that a comparison is possible with the material utilized by a great composer like Sciarrino. How many works by Sciarrino circulate in our country? Certainly more than he wrote. One cannot contribute to the fame of an incompetent, one cannot divulgate someone else’s music, one cannot plagiarize his own students as if they were clones. I’d like to say, Down with gripping with claws, down with the “slaves,” down with those who use the “slaves.”
SCIARRINO: The responsibility of a compulsory imitation remains mostly within those masters who have a certain closed mind about musical conception. You cannot study with them without imitating them. Yet they are not to be blamed: “Wicked is the disciple who does not surpass the master.”
METZGER: No! The responsibility rests with the pupil. First of all, he could always rebel against the master. Secondly, there are cases in which the apprentices take the upper hand over the masters.
CASTALDI [seems to agree with Sciarrino, even if expressing his sentiments in sharper tones]: Bussotti, Donatoni, et similia do not teach techniques but rather their compositional tricks. On the other hand, some thirty years ago they themselves were influenced by musicians like Boulez and Stockhausen. It is sad to think how at the beginning of the century there was an array of composers, each one with his own personality: Stravinsky, Ravel, Strauss, Puccini, Hindemith, Ives, Bartok… What a different panorama! Therefore, if it is true that an artist has to have his own style, it is also true that a teacher must take into consideration multiple techniques. I like everything. Therefore I am against preclusion.
DONATONI: Let’s be clear about a few things. First of all, when speaking of imitators of Donatoni, please mention their full names; anonymity prevents me from answering correctly. Second, if a student mistakes as a point of arrival what I mean to be a point of departure, I don’t see why it ought to be my responsibility. Furthermore, students who come to me have already exercised a certain choice: I cannot teach the craftsmanship of Boulez or Clementi or Stockhausen or Bussotti. Finally, I want to mention the entity of my intervention: within the next two years, the Accademia di Santa Cecilia will promote some twenty meetings, while elsewhere the monthly lessons [I offer] don’t last more than a school year.
CAGE [prefering not to indulge in autobiography]: Now that Schönberg has been mentioned, I wish to remind you that I also was his pupil. And I don’t think, to be frank, that I absorbed his teaching in a passive fashion.
TOGNI: In fact, the importance, the conditio sine qua non, is that the maestro must have a full awareness of what he intends to teach. This way, the danger of plagiarism is avoided as well as, perhaps unconsciously, a propensity toward mimesis.
MARINUZZI [skeptic and pessimistic]: Even though my experience with Petrassi was marked by the absence of any coercion whatsoever, perhaps it is nonsensical to talk about schools of composition. We live in a time devoid of certainties characterized by the end of languages. If I were thirty years old today, I would be in a crisis with such a dark horizon.
TURCHI [partially in agreement with such a desolate panorama, even though, as a former director of Siena’s Accademia Chigiana, he tries to limit the scenario’s decline]: Speaking about teaching composition, what the state imposes on the conservatory curricula today is stupidity more than an anachronism. In this sense I no longer believe in schools of composition. I believe, instead, that what counts in present conditions is the individual experience. Let me be clear: Sciarrino is perfectly justified in organizing his own courses at Cittá di Castello.
TOGNI: I believe, instead, that it makes sense to talk about the schools of composition, also in consideration of the fact that the relationship between teacher and pupil could assume a profitable connotation of reciprocity. Certainly studying with a great composer could be fruitful as well as damaging, but the truth is that Berg’s Sonata, op.1, sounds absolutely Berghian with Schönberg’s corrections—or, should we say, because of them? Speaking of workshops makes sense only if a system of equality is established: let us not forget how much violin virtuoso [Joseph] Joachim collaborated with Brahms and [Rudolf ] Kolish with Schönberg in the composition of their respective violin concerti.
TURCHI: Anyone who decides to study composition nowadays chooses this or that teacher with all that choice implies. Certainly some musicians like Dona-toni express in their teaching a certain aggressiveness while remaining excellent teachers. But I should also add that it is not necessary to be a great composer in order to be a great teacher. Take, for example, the case of Albrechtsberger, author of rather boring music.The mere fact that he was Beethoven’s teacher could make an illuminating case.
BORTOLOTTO [very close to Turchi’s position]: An individual who approaches a teacher understands the risks involved. It is normal, though, that at some point the pupil chooses his own path notwithstanding the adumbration the teacher may take. Even if Schönberg is the first to prove me wrong, a mediocre composer is always the best teacher. But in the end what are we complaining about? As Adorno said, “He who complains about love is always wrong.”
METZGER: Not in your wildest dreams! The mediocre composer is of no use to anyone, especially the students. The mediocre composer has no rights, not even to exist.
ZACCAGNINI: What about concluding the debate after such a truckload of opinions?
BUSSOTTI [expressing his thoughts in a deserving, poetic form]: I’d like to evoke the friend who people said was a very rich man—Giacinto Scelsi, who never missed any of my musical productions, whose eyes filled with tears because I’d written at the dawn of my career as a publicist a few words in his praise some fifteen years before he’d become fashionable. From a very rich man one would expect very beautiful “slaves,” since he could have afforded them. However, let us be satisfied with having just good ones perhaps with a “fair skin,” and let us count the stones we throw: there are only a couple of them remaining in our pockets. It is wise to save them—you never know, a drift of wind could blow us away.
CLEMENTI [remaining anchored to concreteness; his is a lapidary synthesis]: Gentlemen mystics, a musician must know how to read, write, and play. It is rather humiliating that after centuries of music making we should even debate such obvious notions.
BORTOLOTTO: We must add that the “slave’s” price also includes his silence.
METZGER [rebelling]: But what “slave”? I do not wish to speak about a copy-ist who seizes somebody else’s spiritual paternity of a work. There is today no composer who can ignore Scelsi’s innovations. That is to say that progress is no longer between various sounds but in the internal structure of the sound itself.
ZACCAGNINI: The “happy end” is provided by a master from the other side of the Atlantic.
CAGE: Today we still do not know who William Shakespeare was; let us consider ourselves satisfied that we know who Scelsi was so soon after his demise.









2

Giacinto Scelsi: An Approach to Piano Writing
Michelle Biget-Mainfroy
Is a twentieth-century probe of the piano anachronistic subject matter today? Several young composers think so. It is not fair to ask oneself whether it is still possible to express something new with an instrument so linked with the values of European romanticism. Did the revolutions of which the piano was the presumable protagonist not conclude with some celebrated composers born around the 1880s? After Stravinsky, Bartok, or Cowell, what more can be expected from pianism? Integrated for two centuries with the sociology of European music, the piano has always satisfied the demands of interpreters and audiences alike. Part of our cultural mores, it should not be forgotten that this instrument showed the first signs of intolerance, the Empfindsamkeit, which challenged the timbral fragility of the harpsichord, thus urging the great masters of romanticism to develop a new discourse based on timbres and extended harmonies. Debussy’s piano was a laboratory for rethinking sound data as a substitute for tonality. The proliferation (after 1910) of modes of sound projection corresponded to the important insurrectional currents, which eliminated a unidirectional language in favor of a multilateral one.
Since 1827, the year of Beethoven’s death, various typologies of piano writing have been established, parallel to all nineteenth-century aesthetic currents. These typologies remained unchanged until Debussy stripped them of their primitive functions. For example, the impetuous, magniloquent keyboard arpeggios Chopin, Liszt, or Schumann had associated with tonal instability became, for Debussy, a mouvement sur place, while the thundering chord the romantics reserved for tonal stability became elliptic in works—such as La cathédral engloutie or Brouillards. The acceptance of unpleasant, brutal sounds as musical entities, once considered ugly, was justified after World War I with the emergence of new performance-practice techniques, particularly the use of the martellato and clusters. Scelsi’s compositions are revolutionary in their musical language and piano-performance practice; his piano works mix traditional and innovative elements. Like Debussy, Scelsi employs a nonidiomatic language: he is interested in anything serving the cause of timbral diversification, in line with the general trend of his time. The piano has, by the way, always favored an écriture des differences whose dynamic levels were refined by Debussy, Ravel, or Schönberg (in his op. 11), which Scelsi perfected still further, always according more autonomy to the minimal elements of his discourse—sometimes by employing only one isolated note.
Scelsi’s work adopts the frequent use of ostinato to effect differentiation. If Prokofiev, Honegger, or the Futurists used the constant repetition of groups of a few notes as a metaphor for modern life and mechanical industrialization, Scelsi uses the piano paradoxically to debate, among other things, the metaphor’s extratemporal problem: identity and alterity. In fact, at a certain point the repeated note already sounds different.
Scelsi’s terminology often refers to the grand eighteenth- and nineteenth-century traditions, such as suite and sonata, but also adopts some vaguer terms, like illustrazioni and incantesimi. Scelsi’s pieces often bear subtitles revealing his fascination with the Orient: Suite no. 8, subtitled Bot-Ba, evokes Tibetan clusters; Suite no. 10 (Ka) questions the Being and the Essence; and the first movement of Quattro illustrazioni alludes to the metamorphosis of Vishnu. Scelsi’s interest in Indian and Sufi mysticism goes beyond exotic curiosity: he has learned a new conception of time etirely. His predilection for minimal movements is, however, capital, and so are the durations’ elasticity, and the slow, barely audible endings. In the journal Musik-Konzepte (31:24), H. R. Zeller noted that, after Suite no. 2, Scelsi structures tempo through sudden agogic impulses, irregular passages, and measures of varying length, employing irrational values and free durations. Zeller also points out Scelsi’s rich dynamic palette, emphasizing that every note can possess a particular, expressive indication and that the rapid alternation between bass and treble parts—at the opposite extremities of the total area—emphasizes the uniqueness of the musical occasion. We can assume that in Scelsi’s music the combination of an Oriental conception of time and the legacy of the romantic piano literature—when actively applied—generate new pianistic idiosyncrasies. The écriture des differences can be applied to both long and short forms—for instance, the fifth movement of Suite no. 8, which recalls (perhaps) the monk’s chant and searches for alterity beginning with a G-flat, obsessively repeated throughout the movement. Scelsi’s predilection with the interval of a second (almost a unison) reveals his marked interest in micro-intervals, which, although common enough among certain other composers, shows his inclination toward the unusual while searching for more and better-diversified sound sensations.
THE OSTINATO
Although in isolated instances piano compositions—as we shall discuss presently—have constituted phenomena of a clearly different, sometimes opposite, nature, the majority can be traced back to some form of ostinato—a device suitable to the creation of a polyphony of sound frequencies disposed over various registers, concerted with various melodic lines or with another ostinato. But at the same time the ostinato is also an isolated process, because even if developed in combination, it can still claim its own autonomy.
The ostinato could also enhance timbral effects when projected in space, horizontally or in depth, in order to differentiate various masses of sound. It could also—as a monodic line—become an aggregate, either leading to the texture’s lowest pitch (total silence) or generating extreme violence.
The ostinato is therefore at the service of the tempo’s pulse and timbral coloration. In this regard, Scelsi distances himself from the time-honored classical-romantic practice of following the developing thematic materials during their changes in writing style. Rather, for Scelsi, such changes are continuous and generate dialogues of diverse motives as well as obsessive repetitions. The element’s emancipation of a motif or an ostinato, the sound’s isolation of groups of notes through large intervals, a little like Webern, and the presentation of the same sound sequentially or simultaneously are some of the solutions he adopted to enable the advancement, progress, and life of his ostinati and, in more general terms, his music. The third and ninth movements of Scelsi’s Suite no. 2 (1934), and the much-later fourth piece from Cinque incantesimi (1953), are, in this case, of major significance.
I shall try to demonstrate how every Scelsian pianistic gesture tends toward an “aesthetic of the obsession” that, as such, has no relation to the “style of the identical.” Let’s begin by illustrating Scelsi’s predilection for the mouvement sur place, whose trills and tremolos are their simplest manifestation: movement limited to adjacent notes, reiterations in tremolo, and continuous repetitions. Examples can be found in Scelsi’s Suites no. 2 and 8; his Sonatas no. 2, 3, and 4; Quattro illustrazioni; and Cinque incantesimi. The tremolo on various third intervals in Suite no. 2’s second movement fairly sustains the principal melody, marked legato mormorando, con pedale (see example 2.1), which does not remain obscured for too long; it captures first the interval of a fourth, then a sixth, to culminate in large left-hand arpeggios that, marked agitato, show their willingness to take over the space (see example 2.2a–c).




Example 2.3 shows the beginning of Suite no. 8, with the tremolo upon a tritone, soon associated to a pedal-held B, then an F natural, contributing to the realization of a tridimensional obsession. A similar phenomenon assumes more of a timbral aspect when associated with chords struck sforzando (see example 2.4). The rapid alternation of two-part chords in both hands produces the impression of a constant tremolo (see example 2.5), always similar but never identical: this simulated tremolo progresses faster and louder.
The same dynamic qualities are to be found in the third movement of Cinque incantesimi: here the tremolo leads to fortissimo, schiacciato chords, thus contributing to the final sweep of the precipitato ending (see example 2.6). In addition, the entire fifth and most of the seventh movements are similarly treated. Scelsi often lets a note—and it’s always the same note—resonate in the middle of a mass of sound, reminiscent of Ravel’s celebrated “Le gibet” from Gaspard de la nuit, in which the repeated note evokes the bleakness of a death knell. One example of such structure is the seventh movement of the same suite (see example 2.7). Fast moving sounds contrasting static sounds cleverly mix various registers of the piano. For example, consider a passage from the third movement of Suite no. 2, in which the right hand repeats tirelessly chords comprised of seconds, while the left hand presides over various octaves (see examples 2.8a–b).







The importance of this ostinato is that it underscores mobility in its very immobility. It could also evolve insidiously, gradually enlarging its melodic area, shifting its intervals up or down, like the F-sharp, which, in Suite no. 2’s eighth movement, appears in different registers, bearing various dynamic indications, such as crescendo, rinforzando, and marcatissimo, before disappearing into pianissimo misterioso. As previously noted, the beginning of Suite no. 8 systematizes the tremolo. It is interesting to note that continuous increases of amplitude in the left hand’s shiftings are reached here through the notes of the ostinato only, rapidly expanding over octaves and fifteenths (see example 2.9) before reaching a climax at the following fortissimo (see example 2.10).


The use of a chordal mass of sound as an ostinato is evidently very useful to the dynamic layout, as demonstrated by the compact chords at the beginning of the second movement (see example 2.11). But a fixed ostinato can also prove effective by emphasizing the timbre, as seen in the chromatic staccato embroideries in the third movement (see example 2.12).


In Sonata no. 3’s second movement, the ostinato is openly materialized by increase of intensity, starting from a monodic line, in combination with vibrating low pitches and a repeated median B before the chords reiterate ad infinitum (see example 2.13).
A similar situation is encountered in Sonata no. 4’s second movement, where the ostinato gradually involves the entire vibrating mass of sound, and in the third movement, where, starting from obstinated seconds, it reaches ampler agglomerations (see examples 2.14a–b).



Generalized immobility does not at all signify absence of dynamic development, and even miniatures like Quattro illustrazioni clearly exemplify this. At the beginning of this piece, the combination of two ostinati are based on the embroidery of minor seconds; then a melodic motion is projected, first within a restricted area, later given more space, accoring the left hand particularly some very impressive ascending leaps. In Quattro illustrazioni’s fourth movement, dynamic changes take the principal role: in a brief dedication to Krishna, Scelsi limits himself to a stock of chords comprised of seconds on two obsessive height levels, B and E, enriched by neighboring notes, using (sometimes up to three) dynamic indications in each measure, permitting variety even amid stasis (see example 2.15).

The first movement of Cinque incantesimi alternates almost systematically immo-bile and active sections, the inertia of seconds—percussively executed—contrasting with their agile fragmentations in sixteenth notes. A leap toward the upper register leads to a first climax, which is immediately neutralized by a return to those unrelenting seconds, before a new episode marked fortissimo e marcatissimo leads into a novel, dynamic segment, also reconnected to the ostinato using intervals of seconds (see examples 2.16a–b).


Cinque incantesimi’s fourth movement, on the other hand, departs from a stationary D, which, according to Scelsi’s indications, ought to provoke supernatural sensations: altered as a brodé D-flat/C-sharp, this D disappears, leaving room for an aggregation of notes nonchalantly tossed on the keyboard while the pedal prolongs their resonance. The D reappears at the center of the chord marked glissato in un tremolo, the triumphant protagonist of a variety of sound adventures. The D’s disappearance and reappearance creates tension and relaxation—like the old consonance and dissonance, this time, though, without any predictability. The concept of ostinato is also applied to masses of sound, jeu par accords frappés, which had so distinguished piano and harpsichord writings of the eighteenth century. Beethoven later increased the number of chordal components and made use of the aggregated shock effect by associating harmonic surprise with prolonged resonance. The romantics later devised a more refined method of composition, using more or less dense, aggregated sequences (from the parallel thirds in Chopin’s Barcarolle to the robust chordal declamations of Liszt’s “Mazeppa” or his Sonata in B minor). The impressionists used the chord for its timbral potential, while the pianists and composers—for whom the hammered touch would eventually become a topos—required a type of dry resonance unknown at that time, since the nineteenth century required the aggregation of a soft touch, isolated or in sequence.
In Scelsi’s piano music the chord is often used as strict homophony, as in Suite no. 2, preserving the declamatory character of the romantic aggregate sequence, like a sort of thick melody. See, for instance, the double octaves in the third movement of that same suite (see example 2.17). But Scelsi also uses the chord for shock value. Take a close look, for example, at the obsessively repeated combinations in the right hand at the beginning of the suite’s fifth movement, enclosed in a disquieting diminished octave (see example 2.18).
In the seventh movement, multilayered atonal masses of sound function as an ostinato (see example 2.19). The eleventh movement gets a jolt of energy from the unison occurring between the two hands, alternated with the raging percussiveness of the chord (see example 2.20). The twelfth movement restores the old operatic concitato to generate a feeling of agitation, which is exacerbated by the chord’s repeated timbral ostinato, before the rhythmic structure of the passage is expanded in longer values (see example 2.21).





This last episode of Suite no. 2 concludes with a repetition of certain aggregate elements, starting piano, moving to fortissimo and then forte pesante; soon the mass of sound produces a mouvement sur place, just as Scelsi accomplishes in Suite no. 8. In the second movement of Suite no. 2, chords of robust octaves remain practically unchanged, functioning only as nuances’ variables or—on the contrary—as improvised contrasts. Something analogous occurs in the fifth movement, where the homophonic aggregate mass of sound could be presented either as a striking fortissimo or as a mysterious piano and pianissimo, with minimal departures from the harmonic model. The emergence of a chordal ostinato could also constitute the repetitive element of a Scelsi piece: the first movement of Sonata no. 4, for example, begins with a tricinium, collects sporadic aggregations, reaches its dynamic apex with struck martellato chords—particularly in the high register—and finally falls into a progressive diminution of intensity.
The chord could also, thanks to the pedal, fulfill its function with a slight ritardando. In the second movement of Sonata no. 4, for example, the grand fortissimo chords are complemented by median D notes (creating an echo effect) and slopped into a series of fading dynamic markings: mezzopiano, poco forte, or pianissimo (see example 2.22).

Such obsessive repetition is also based on timbral extremism, for which the same phenomenon’s iteration—if necessary the same aggregate mass of sound—simultaneously assumes the function of an ostinato, which continues to develop inside the sound fabric because of its singularity.
Alternating chords and runs was key to romantic-era piano composition; it allowed for a depth of composition that was not reliant on orchestral and instrumental ensembles and was different from the baroque and preclassical polyphonies, concentrating only on melodic layouts. The latter influenced Scelsi to some degree. The beginning of Suite no. 2, for example, emphasizes the exchange between chordal punctuation with generous resonance and the isolated line that occupies a progressively larger area, followed by a restriction of the same space (see example 2.23). In that suite’s ninth movement, this chord alternation is also placed at the service of the ostinato, through the insertion of an F-sharp in both chord and melodic line (see example 2.24).


In the fourth movement of Suite no. 8, the obstinately repeated chords evoke a tam-tam—think about the use of bells by both the Russian Five and Stravinsky. This aggregate context is then sometimes alternated with a line of seconds placed above it and repeated until a whispered repetition of an E fades out, capturing some embroideries before conceding space to a last bell stroke and an isolated B in the lower register. Casual of systematic, rare of usual, the alternating of mass of sound and melodic line becomes an accomplice of the ostinato, thus reconciling in Scelsi’s ostinati the romantic principle of contrast with the concept of mobile immobility and active repetition.
Great shifts, necessary for contrast between registers, are relatively rare in Scelsi. They are mainly confined to the left hand, often as part of a repeated gesture, like the mechanical accompaniment in the second piece of Suite no. 2 (see example 2.25), which includes ample lines expanded over more than four octaves before landing on ostinati.
Sonata no. 3’s first movement assigns broken figures and large intervals to the left hand, played by leaps and ascending arpeggios; the third movement is similarly arranged. This procedure constitutes less of a supporting stereotype than the contrast between an itinerant line and one or more stagnant levels—thus the alternate and itinerant figures indicated by portato markings present in the second movement of Quattro illustrazioni (see example 2.26).


Quattro illustrazioni’s third movement, very particular and of great dynamic breadth, opens with directions to play con eleganza e seduzione. This is reminiscent of the romantic arabesque—only the isolated chord at the beginning and another one marked pianissimo at the end of the first section a tempo let one intuit the piano’s possibilities for massive writing. This endless agitation can be heard, in fact, either as an endless levitation or as an advancement toward an objective. The register’s alternation could also be very rapid, thanks to the distance between the lower notes of the left hand and the main melody, just as can be observed in the beginning of the third movement of Suite no. 2 (see example 2.27). This phenomenon could eventually play a sudden role between two “magic spells” in the same register as at the end of the ninth movement in the same suite (see example 2.28).


Through the combination of two ostinati situated at the opposite ends of the keyboard, Scelsi fuses his écriture des differences by alternating a repetitive process and a change of register, as shown in the end of the third movement in Suite no. 8 (see example 2.29). The end result is the isolation of the timbral areas similar to Sonata no. 2’s second movement (see example 2.30), or Sonata no. 3’s first movement, in which Scelsi alternates two melodic layouts prior to combining them.


The coercion of ostinati, mass aggregations, and contrasts between volume and register brings to the fore the question of pianistic timbre. As mentioned earlier, the principal problem in composing for the keyboard is the diversification of sound quality: Liszt, for example, expected the piano to imitate other instruments—quasi flauto, quasi cello, and so on. A certain literature of transcriptions has taken advantage of the modern piano’s resources: two pedals and double (or triple) escapement, important new levels of resonance. Did Debussy attempt to forget that the piano was a percussion instrument? What about Stravinsky’s Petrushka and Bartok’s Allegro barbaro? Composers after 1920 who came closer to the keyboard managed to extract something new from it. Scelsi, for instance, has deftly exploited the piano’s deep and dark registers. The ostinato in the first movement of his Suite no. 8 leans heavily on a bass line, almost crashing it (see example 2.31), while later, in the third movement, another line drags along slowly under the right hand (see example 2.32), struggling in vain to overtake the upper register.

A decrease of intensity in the concluding passage is derived from a sense of emptiness, noise-like disintegration, and silence; it can be observed at the end of the first, second, and third movements of both Sonata no. 3 and also the first movement of Quattro illustrazioni. Vladimir Jankelevic has pointed out a similar use of pianissimo in the piano music of Faurè, Debussy, and Ravel, and in certain passages of Bartók. It is more recently seen in the piano music of Scelsi, who did not neglect great moments of intensity, shock effect, cataclysmic crashes, and percussive ostinati á la Bartók. Strokes of tam-tam—as we’ve already noticed in the fourth movement of Suite no. 8—are followed by the gradual extinguishing of the sound, regular and irregular sforzandissimi on ostinato figure (Stravinsky’s influence?) or, better yet, on impetuous chords.
Quattro illustrazioni’s second movement offers an interesting array of sforzati, marcati, and staccati without pedal, only to stop at the frontier of silence on an ostinato in the lower register, where Scelsi allows for a con poco pedale: the “calm after the storm.” The crackling seconds (already present in Debussy’s “Feux d’artifice” and in Ravel’s major works) in the second movement of Sonata no. 2 have a clear timbral function (see example 2.33), as they tend toward disturbing noises—like the strident grace notes of the chords in the third and fourth movements of Cinque incantesimi (see example 2.34).



The fifth movement of Cinque incantesimi adopts noise-making effect through the dynamic indications pungente, strepitoso, and rudemente. Its stringendo leads then to a prestissimo through a tremolando of unleashed chords. Scelsi has not ignored the modern cluster in enriching his dynamic arsenal: chords of seconds executed at the extreme ends of the keyboard prove it. Neither, however, did Scelsi neglect intermediate solutions: consider the nuances between barely audible and extremely violent, or, better, tonal shades’ indications, like molto forte i La marcati and meno forte il resto, as marked in the third movement of Sonata no. 2. Scelsi has also attempted complex gestures: the famous tam-tam effect in Suite no. 8’s fourth movement is to be played fortissimo e percussivo, but the staccato marking gives way to the directive lasciar vibrare col pedale.
Let us now investigate the relationship between pianistic gestures and discursive tactic. Since the second half of the eighteenth century, the piano has been able to adapt its touch technique to formal developments, associating their main phases to existing typologies of writing. As the nineteenth century’s composers gradually divided the form’s globality into several episodes—favoring composite architectures—the piano struggled to cope with composition’s organism through a dynamic process born out of various approaches, espousing the general curve to its individual elements; the parceling out of the form, well perceived by the romantic composers, at the beginning of the twentieth century reached its apex in a so-called “aesthetics of the moment.” Excluding those pedagogical works aimed at overcoming technical difficulties—which perpetuate a certain type of composition—there are no piano works that systematize their own gestures. Instrumental technology, on the other hand, is able to diversify the dissonances of the ample acoustical potential, as it polyphonizes, deepens, and finally dramatizes the various gestures.
A brief analysis of the first movement in Scelsi’s Quattro illustrazioni reveals a sequential discourse conceived to valorize the art of micro-happening, which Scelsi has voided of any polyphonic ambition. Any short piece—from Chopin to Debussy’s Préludes—is always open to infinite evolutionary processes: it rejects the infiltration of motives, contrasting figures, and modulating patterns typical of the old sonata based on the process of interplay, conflicts, and, finally, reconciliation of the single components. The piano miniature stands in contrast to pieces of large breadth, like the classical-romantic sonatas. Among the miniatures we can enlist Schumann’s Carnaval and Kinderszenen, as well as the Gnossiennes and Gymnopédies, which Satie did not hesitate to publish separately despite of their ridiculously short duration. Romantic composers and their champions have familiarized us with the flexibility of a piece’s length. Interminable Mahler symphonies contrast with Wolf’s lieder, symphonic pages such as Saint-Saen’s “Danse macabre,” or Honegger’s “Pacific 231,” exceptions that last a few minutes. Gigantic cosmogonies involving numerous parameters of musical interventions (Wagnerian drama, Diaghilev’s ballets, Milhaud’s Christoph Colomb) reclaim temporal expansion. Nevertheless, the first half of the twentieth century broke all records of brachylogy, tending towards the miniaturization, particularly in the solo literature.
In a musical aphorism everything counts: in the first movement of Scelsi’s Quattro illustrazioni, portraying the metamorphoses of Vishnu, we find sound metaphors created with the smallest detail, refining differences, soliciting the proper resonances from the piano, always expressing tainted shades that sometimes reach monochromatic dynamic levels. That first movement begins with the superimposition of two ostinati placed a tritone apart—a little exotic touch and an allusion to a religion of the Great Beyond (see example 2.35). The Cs (sharp or otherwise), which form an embroidery with the ostinato left-hand D, played mezzopiano in the first three measures, allow us to intuit the presence of the third melodic layout. This third level of ostinato is then definitely stabilized by the presence of chords of seconds on the first mezzoforte, while the next measures lead to the appearance of a group of three sounds in which the note D occupies the median position (see example 2.36). The following two measures deal with the iteration of the same elements—subdued to their intensity’s amplification—which leads to a perfect C-sharp minor chord, whose interval of a fifth (C-sharp to G-sharp) substitutes the preceding D–A interval (example 2.37).
Here we have a first panel of the piece characterized by a composition method based on durations and nuances, combined with a positive dynamic evolution—from mezzopiano to quasi forte, in the course of three short connected periods. The second episode, marked poco piú mosso, is connected to the preceding one by the tuilage of the fifth C-sharp–G-sharp and by holding the note D as the center of gravity (see example 2.38). However, the sensation of ostinato persists, despite the interference of sparse Gs and Es, and the note D’s instability in holding its fundamental position. The sequential élan has now become wider as dragging notes give life to the first part of this episode (ten measures) with effects of both ritenuto and a tempo, through the recurrence of nuances étouffee from another damper tone that never exceeds mezzopiano. The last three beats of the last measure played mezzoforte are an exception (see example 2.39).




After that, the marking a tempo restores the initial ostinato, allowing them to progress dynamically to forte (see example 2.40). What follows is a dialogue between different registers of the piano, a secret to all but Scelsi: in the course of the measures, three ostinati, based on the simple repetition of the same notes expand over five octaves (see example 2.41). Once the dialogue abandons the extreme registers, it continues softly within the span of a little more than three octaves. The composer, though, quickly multiplies the dialogue’s level of high frequencies up to five times (see example 2.42). A last sequence of four measures in the middle register represents the note D through its neighboring grace notes—resolving the problem of identifying the polar note—and extinguishes itself in a triple piano, which allows a group of four sounds distributed within the interval of thirteenth to vanish (see example 2.43).



As a schema we have for part 1: 3 + 3 + 2 measures; for part 2: 10 + 11 measures; for part 3 (a variation on part 1): 4 + 3 + 2 + 4 measures—that is to say, a global curve formed by 8 + 21 + 13 measures. The longest section (half the total) is therefore the central one, which also has the distinction of having enlarged the sequences and the melodic span without losing its intensity. The third and last part, instead, shorter than the second but longer than the first (it is homologous), assigns through irregular symmetry a particular meaning to the return of the ostinato on a forte. It occupies, furthermore, the entire keyboard, even if sporadically, and activates a mobility, which the conclusive sequence of the second part did not anticipate. It is only in the last few notes of the piece that Scelsi uses the ostinato to introduce variation, a fact that confers to this finale a dynamic characteristic, recalling the ethics of the romantic tonal forms. It is noticeable how rapidly Scelsi reaches a dynamic evolution by using repetitions that logically ought to have been impeded. Scelsi’s responsibility for the pianistic timbre appears evident.


It goes without saying that the composition process adopted in Quattro illustrazioni’s first movement is not true but one of the possibilities for structuring musical time. This process provides an opportunity to probe the idiosyncrasies between instrumental style and formal concept without analyzing a long work. This piece—considering that the last panel has a function of opening and does not equal absolutely the reproduction of the first—is, in fact, retractable formally to the ancient (and proved) three-part scheme A-B-A. The sonata movements, instead, being more developed, will not be able to adopt such a simple course of action as the level of refinement of their individual elements. Scelsi’s sonatas (and suites)—structured in several movements—and the miniatures (Quattro illustrazioni and Cinque incantesimi) are therefore linked by a common ethic, meaning the atomization of the form on the part of the pianistic gesture—a constant of both modern and contemporary piano writing, retractable, for instance, in a prototype like Berio’s Sequenza. In the teleology of these works everything is possible: central climax by enlargement of the area of the more substantial resonances, linear advances toward dispersion—or, on the contrary, toward amplification of the final dynamic stage—to which the piano can offer many facets. Furthermore, a discourse born in the course of the piece—independently from the motivic reference (which does not exclude the use of the osti-nato)—symmetry or asymmetry of the instrumental formulations’ connection, free sequence of sections of tempi, or evident organization around the median axis. It is more important to be aware of the priority accorded to punctiform gestures in order to evaluate a structure, than the simple identification of the whole. Furthermore, Scelsi’s predilection for cycles of pieces that are frequently short seems to confirm a tendency toward a piano composition of moment musical.
An examination, albeit superficial, of Sonata no. 4 shows the signs of an inherited rhetoric—a piece of music cannot, in fact, be called a sonata without impunity—and a fervent aspiration toward a new pianistic dogma. The first movement could recall the old sonata form, through a vague reexposition effect, while the second movement begins like a devilish scherzo: in sum, it could satisfy our theoretical understanding of a sonata, but it is sufficient to investigate some purely instrumental behaviors to detect alternations in the original point of reference.
The opening statement of the first movement—poco movendo—shows a piano piece evolving by progressive enlargement of the left hand’s shifts, thickening the polyphonic fabric and gaining full control of the piece’s intensity. Although the initial cell is formed by two ostinati, we are far from the usual effects of invariability in the opposite poles of the sonata form; the touch indication marcato and various fortissimi give the central sequence a moment of eloquence up to the intervention of an ostinato embroderie on intervals of seconds, characteristic of one of the ostinati present in the exposition. Its placement in the low register of the models line prepares for an event definable as reexposition, not merely repetitive, though, since it rhythmically distorts the pseudohomologous portion, contradicts its duration (fifteen measures versus forty), employs the left hand with more agile fragmentary figures, and finally lands on a slow section whose agglomeration of notes drowns every sound in amorphous dyads.
The central lento is clearly composed in open form, thanks to its instrumental stance. It makes use of a stuttering ostinato of a few notes, and the moving section is placed at the bottom of the structure in order to contrast quasiprocessional sections to a more mobile one in which the occupation of the melodic space and the appearance of aggregate masses of sound define their depth. The alternation of this ostinato—one of an itinerant pianistic gesture and the other developed irregularly, sometimes precipitously—ultimately arrive to a fusion as shown by the ostinati Ds that complete and aggregate themselves in a fortissimo expanded over a more than three-octave area (see example 2.22, previously cited). Then a four-measure sostenuto, starting from the ostinato, enlarges the texture through crescendo and movendo. Therefore, we have the following dynamic sequence: percussivo, stridente first in the same notes, then in the same chords, until the ostinato’s transformation in timbral values concludes, in the last measures, with the reconciliation of the static and dynamic principles.
The third movement, rapid, belongs to the spate of grand fantastic tourbillons of the romantic era. The dilatation of intensity—virtuosity that increases in intensity as the piece proceeds—and the superimposition of various pianistic topoi—emphasis on triple forte and chords hammered with full force in a perorative episode—characterize the precipitato ending of this piece. In reality, such unleashed violence leads to a tranquil (quarter note = 63), subdued feeling, without pedal, also based once again on an ostinato, which reenters in the lower register and emigrates suddenly to the upper spheres before shrinking to a single appoggiato sound.
It seems plausible to explain the formal development of a Scelsi piano piece—and also those by many other composers—as “distribution on the keyboard of instrumental gestures following one another without logic.” The osmosis between musical concept and its materialization is perfect, thanks to the historical quality of an instrument capable of producing simultaneous sounds. Variety of each one’s timbre is brought to the fore through the magic of various touch techniques: in this sense Scelsi has only exploited an idiom others before him had already put to use—we must remember that what we have classified before as idiosyncratic, meaning the assumption of a jeu refined by composers since 1780, reclaims a responsibility in the structure of Scelsi’s works. This cult of the ostinato, many times mentioned, can very well be considered as a pianistic potential: Scelsi’s ostinato is somewhat of a wager, because it attempts to organize a moving process while maintaining static characteristics. Extended on the keyboard’s tonality or confined within limited space, tenuto line, or multiple aggregations, positioned in diverse sound production permitted by the softness of the pianistic mechanism, the ostinato is variable and, as such, could aspire to something that in fact could be produced, a condition without which no sound event, structure, or composition can take place.
With seven suites, four sonatas, and forty preludes composed in the 1930s and three more suites, Cinque incantesimi, and the Quattro illustrazioni written between 1952 and 1955, all this after a period in which the piano had been neglected, Scelsi took his time in order to elaborate a pianistic discourse that today certainly deserves to be heard. Scelsi’s discourse is not limited to the recovery of preexisting values; it is a discourse that reconciles sound and silence, reaching, thanks to its instrumental pertinence, a particular balance of elements fundamentally loaded with musical meaning.








3

Sound as Process: Scelsi and the
  Composers of Nuova Consonanza
Gianmario Borio
In a symposium about a composer whose creation of a valid exegesis poses anything but minor obstacles—quantitatively and qualitatively—a contribution about his cultural-historical environment could be viewed as merely peripheral, only indirectly concerning the substance of his work. In the case of Scelsi, the problem of historical localization is more serious than usual, since he has mostly been portrayed as a loner in the history of music in previous interpretations. His work is said to have such uniqueness that no convincing connections with history, either diachronic or synchronic, could be determined. It is specifically this disconnectedness that accounts for some of the fascination, which his music radiates onto today’s public. The following reflections should be taken as an attempt to revise this position and to transpose Scelsi’s artistic work of the 1950s and 1960s into the context of the Roman world of art and music of the time. To preempt the inevitable questions, I will first summarize the problems surrounding the current reception of Scelsi.
Scelsi’s music entered the international discussion about New Music when his most important works had already been written. The performances on the occasion of the Darmstadt Internationale Ferienkurse für Neue Musik in 1982 and 1984, as well as the retrospectives in Cologne and Royamont in 1987, acted as a refreshing impulse for a compositional situation that threatened to polarize itself in a structural mannerism removed from the reality of the sound and a stylistic pluralism devoid of orientation. In this context, Scelsi’s exposed works had a signaling character. They showed that the range of usable material is nowhere near exhausted, that a restriction of the point of view—to an interval or a sound—could reveal unimagined creative possibilities. Two complementary points of view entered the public conscience: that the single sound is not a uniform but a complex phenomenon and, inversely, that a complex composition can be presented as a single sound. But Scelsi’s work has also made clear an ideal often aspired to by the serial and informal avant-garde: the abolition of hierarchies among the parameters. The distinction between pitch and duration as structural parameters and timbre as well as dynamics as secondary parameters was overcome by Scelsi through a reversal process that relocated all “peripheral” dimensions to the center of the compositional work. His aesthetic postulate that we moved from the age of melody and construction to the age of rhythm and harmony1 can be understood beyond the literal wording: Harmony means fanning out a tone, while his conception of rhythm, intended in an amotivic and afigurative mode, gives rise to a rhythmic variation of a single tone or produces the pulsation of a large-format sound texture.
In sharp contrast to the catalytic effect of his poetics, Scelsi’s compositions have remained hidden within the unbreakable veil of mystery that surrounded their creation. Journalism generally focused on mostly negative features: anomaly, irreducibility, and unanalyzability. Placing his output in the context of the music of the twentieth century is still pending.2 After Scelsi’s death in 1988, the public divided itself into two factions: unconditional supporters, who took over his aesthetics and Weltanschauung together with the technical innovations, and irreconcilable opponents, who based their arguments on the controversial authorship of his scores without reflecting on the fact that the continuity between thought and notation does not constitute necessarily the premise for the validity of a composition. The only way out of this bottleneck appears to be a historic normalization of the Scelsi case. Its tasks include debunking the myth of Scelsi as a unique individual in world history and interpreting his music as an intersection of historic lines that run from the past, through his experiences, to the future. This “normalization”—for which Zoltán Peskó has been pleading recently3—initially implies philological research into the scores, handwritten sketches, and numerous sound documents stored in the Scelsi estate.4 Quite a few music-history questions remain open today:

the question of Scelsi’s reception of twelve-tone technique, whose answer requires a clarification of the real significance of the teachings of Walter Klein;
the influence of the Parisian culture milieu;
the importance of Michiko Hirayama, not only for Scelsi’s musical treatment of the sound but also for the formation of his sound aesthetics in general;
the mutual influence between Scelsi and the composers of Nuova Consonanza;
the question of usable criteria for the analysis of his music, which, in turn, could offer a connection to the music history problems; and
the question of the compositional reception of Scelsi’s music—a reception that has apparently been absent until today but for which the most recent works of Aldo Brizzi, Giulio Castagnoli, Federico Incardona, and Giorgio Tedde provide important pointers.

One of these points is discussed below in detail: the question of the existence—and importance—of the relationship between Scelsi’s production and the activity of the composers of Nuova Consonanza. This question will be discussed focusing on three areas: the critique of the musical notation, the problematic associated with compositional subject and authorship, and sound as process.
The notation developed in Western music culture is concentrated on so-called “primary” parameters. We have exact forms for the designation of a pitch at our disposal, as well as a very differentiated range of graphical signs that enables unmistakable representation of the most complex rhythmic relationships. For the “secondary” parameters—dynamics and timbre—we generally have periphrases that provide us with an approximate idea of what is to be realized. Based on a century-old performance tradition, musicians are capable of understanding the designations for dynamics and timbre in such a way that the meaning of the work is—mostly!—not being falsified. Things are different for a music that includes timbre and dynamics as the most important conduits of meaning. In light of these problems, one hypothesis is gaining momentum: the fact that Scelsi’s renunciation of the written fixation of his works is not due to an aristocratic arrogance but is based on problems inherent to musical notation. In light of this inadequacy, Scelsi’s compositional practice is articulated in three phases: (1) improvisation on keyboards, its preliminary recording on audiotape and possibly a graphical draft of the developing work; (2) written representation by an “assistant” to reach a certain level of objectivity and its implementation based on multiple verification (the important point is that the notation is intended for the performance); and (3) preparation of a performance with the respective performer. The latter makes evident the shift of meaning of the notation. In fact, if the musical text had supplied a sufficient guarantee for the correct rendering of the musical contents, the collaboration between the composer and the performers would not have been necessary to this extent.5
The question of authorship goes hand in hand with that of identity of the compositional subject. The idea of compositional work as solitary desk work of a subject that conducts an imaginary dialogue with the past and his or her contemporaries has become questionable at most after John Cage’s Music of Changes. Scelsi’s position in this context is hardly as obscure as some of his claims could suggest. He did not view himself as a composer but rather as a messenger mediating between a higher world and the earthly world of mortals. If we wanted to dissociate ourselves from Scelsi’s spiritualism, then this statement should be understood as a metaphor for a historically conditioned behavior. I suspect that Scelsi’s turning point in 1960 was highly influenced by Art Informel—particularly by abstract expressionism in America. A detail from Scelsi’s biography pointed out by Peskó can substantiate this suspicion: “In the 1960s, he was cofounder of a ‘Rome–New York Art Foundation’; not only did he know the music avant-garde like nobody else but also the avant-garde of literature and fine arts.”6 Now, I wish to offer a short summation of arguments that have been discussed elsewhere in detail.7 Art Informel is generally known as the result of a cross-influence of surrealism and expressionism. It intensifies a break from the traditional concept of the subject, which was traced out in the French and German avant-gardes of the 1920s and 1930s. If expressionism had taken a significant step toward resolving the rationally operating subject by separating reality and representing the visionary, then Art Informel eliminates representations altogether dissolving—so to speak—the subject in the self-movement of the color matter. André Breton’s requirement of a “psychic automatism” seems to show its real content only in the informal art: by accelerating the painting speed, action painting intends to eliminate rational thought processes and recourses to the familiar; the picture becomes a seismograph on which all subjective movements are entered directly. There are many indications pointing to the supposition that the Art Informel’s problematics influenced wide areas of the music scene around 1960. Scelsi and the composers of Nuova Consonanza drew different conclusions from it, although similar in nature.
The two problems mentioned—écriture and subject—converge at the compositional level. Here composing does not mean so much the building of structures as it does creating movement types of the sound matter that approach states and processes of nature. Sound, the result of the interaction of the parameters, is at the center of this work. It is a moving sound whose processes and tendencies have to be “composed.” In this context, Scelsi’s dissociation from parametrical thinking receives an entirely different meaning as the abstract negation of serialism through the postmodern restitution of traditional values. The infraparametric procedures of an informal composition are based on the same foundation on which the serial music pursued the elimination of hierarchies between parameters.
