One could speak of the second phase of one and the same mental process. If chord formation can no longer be distinguished from timbre or dynamic graduations from pitch fluctuations, if sustained notes are unimaginable without microrhythmic movements or sound attack cannot exist without clouding the spectrum, then the separate handling of the parameters loses its meaning. One starts to conceive the sound as a multiple force field containing certain developmental potentials. A reversal takes place in the relationship between structure and material. External structures are no longer being sought after in order to organize the material; the natural qualities of a material segment already provide an insight into the possible laws of structure and form.
Franco Evangelisti was aware of these problems, as seen in his compositions following the publication of his seminal work Ordini (1955) and his scattered writings, which were collected by Irmela Heimbächer and Heinz-Klaus Metzger and published under the title “Vom Schweigen zu einer neuen Klangwelt” in issue number 43/44 of Musik-Konzepte. Scelsi met Evangelisti in Rome in 1952 immediately after Evangelisti’s return. In the 1950s Evangelisti was known as mediator between the innovations that took place in the framework of the Darmstadt Ferienkurse (summer courses) and in the Cologne Studio for Electronic Music, and the young composers of Rome who had to do battle with the traditionalist music scene of their home country. After the attempts to modernize the Roman music scene undertaken by Goffredo Petrassi after the end of the war, it is Evangelisti that the city has to thank for the most significant advances. In 1959 he founded the journal Ordini, whose main objectives included examining the relationships between music and the other spheres of art and knowledge.8 Evangelisti soon became the catalyst of a circle of musicians—Domenico Guaccero, Mario Bertoncini, Daniele Paris, Egisto Macchi, Antonio De Blasio, Mauro Bortolotti—who had been working on the idea of a music society since 1960. The group’s goal was to spread New Music beyond schools and trends. Their alternative to the official music scene became reality in 1961 with the establishment of the Nuova Consonanza concert society. The term Nuova Consonanza was retrieved by the music critic Alberto Pironti in the foreword to the collection Le musiche di Iacopo Peri from the year 1600; it does not refer to any new law of harmony but to an aesthetic program: look beyond your own boundaries to constantly reach “new consonances.”
Due to financial difficulties, the concert series could not start until the following year (1962) and did so with an evening of electronic music and a piano recital of Frederic Rzewski.9 Beginning in 1963, Nuova Consonanza organized a yearly multiday festival, which included music by Scelsi: in 1964 Hô for female voice, in 1965 the second and in 1968 the third string quartet. At the beginning of the 1960s, a few members of Nuova Consonanza were involved in the conception of the Settimane Internazionali per la Nuova Musica of Palermo, whose six events offered an unparalleled representation of the central musical trends of the decade.10 In 1964 Evangelisti established the improvisation group Nuova Consonanza, which had its first performance in 1965 on the occasion of the third festival of the society: in addition to Evangelisti, Carmine Pepe, Larry Austin, John Eaton, John Heinemann, Roland Kayn, William O. Smith, and Ivan Vandor performed as well. After a few personnel changes, the group of players stabilized in 1967 with Mario Bertoncini, Walter Bran-chi, John Heinemann, Egisto Macchi, Ennio Morricone, and Franco Evangelisti.
The establishment of an improvisation group occurred at the time when Evangelisti decided to reveal his activity as a notation composer. These decisions were preceded by a decade in which Evangelisti tested strategies for the constitution of a radically New Music. The early formative experiences for his compositional work go back to the Darmstadt Summer Courses for New Music, which he’d attended regularly since 1952, during which time he had decisive encounters with Werner Meyer-Eppler and Karlheinz Stockhausen. During the 1950s Evangelisti developed the conviction that a “new sonic world” could not be implemented using conventional notation, common notions of form and instruments. Among the first countermeasures was the introduction of aleatoric practices, which were not to serve as an alleged “liberation of the performer” for him but, rather, as the creation of multiple force fields. The following passage is from the text Mehrdeutigkeit und Werk,11 which served as model of a broadcast by Radio Bremen in 1962:

The term aleatoric continues to be confused with the term chance.… By confusing these terms, the complete situation in relation to Cage is being falsified. Admittedly, Cage can neither be identified nor associated with these manifestations, even if one could refer to him in certain procedures. In reality, Cage uses a completely different approach. His work remains rooted in the accidental, whereas the aleatoric is a completely purposeful process.… If we go back to the origin of this term, we will find that its most basic meaning contains a fundamental prerequisite: the danger and the risk. Hence, to be in an aleatoric situation means the conscious assumption of the risk situation that is presented within a range of possibilities and within certain dimensions. Let us give an example that is closely connected with the term aleatoric and its Latin root, alea [in English: a dice]. It stands to reason that the range of possibilities for a person throwing dice is determined by the sides of the dice with their numeric symbols from one through six. Hence it is not at all possible to confuse aleatoric with chance, a situation whose occurrence cannot be predicted in any way.”12

This alea conception first manifested itself in 1959 in Evangelisti’s Aleatorio for string quartet. The work consists of four score pages whose performance duration exceeded three minutes only on rare occasions. The individual notation systems are equipped with selection options from time to time that apply to different dimensions of the movement. This is designed in such a way that a specific result is always reached—despite ambiguity. Each score page of Aleatorio can be interpreted as graphical representation of a specific sound field of which each performance realizes one manifestation. Listening to several performances in succession would allow us to determine that the identity and meaning of the work remain unchanged. The repeated listening experience conveys the impression that this composition is less about its temporal unfolding than about a sound object, which, so to speak, is rotated about itself.
The path to field composition and ambiguous form had already begun in Evangelisti’s first important composition, Ordini. This work, which carries the revealing subtitle “Strutture variate per sedici strumenti,” is divided into two equal parts, which, in many ways, behave as complements to each other. While the first part represents an uninterrupted sound process, sound textures of different lengths alternate with zones of silence in the second part. In the first half, Evangelisti uses predominantly determinate pitches and simple instrumental mixtures; the second half, on the other hand, distinguishes itself with dense and unstable sound masses, while pursuing varied forms of integration of sound and noise. The fact that the tone quality belongs to the central categories of this music is already shown by the attention with which Evangelisti implements a constant change of instrumental combinations in the first part (see table 3.1).
The sixteen instruments are divided into four groups of four segments each (which also specifies the spatial arrangement of the ensemble on the stage):

trumpet, French horn, trombone, contrabassoon
piano, celesta, xylophone, timpani
tenor drum, cymbals, bass drum, triangle
violin, viola, cello, double bass

Ordini begins with one instrumental group per measure in opening measures one through twelve; each instrument must play at least one tone. In the subsequent course, a regular change of measures with two or three groups, as well as measures with a single group, takes place. The selection of groups, as well as the number of instruments used in each case, depends on serial principles. An attempt is made to reach a proportional increase, which culminates in the concluding tutti of measure forty-five (see example 3.1). If we disregard this measure, every group appears sixteen times; on average, each instrument plays in thirty-two measures (although the overall number of entries per instrument is not constant).

These few observations show which decisive role serial procedures played for Ordini (the title already refers to rational treatment of the material). The additional dimensions will not be discussed here in any detail. Reference is only made at this point to the twelve-tone row built according to the Webern pattern: three four-tone groups referenced to each other with respect to original, inversion, and retrograde. Similar to Luigi Nono’s Incontri (also created in 1955), the tone series is repeated untransposed several times.13 However, constantly changing harmonies, clusters in the celesta and piano, glissandi, and undetermined pitches in the strings, as well as regular interventions of the percussionists, prevent us from perceiving the constancy of the tone sequence. The second half of Ordini consists of eleven episodes—one, two, three, four, five, six, five, four, three, two, and one measures long—that are separated from each other by general rests of random duration. Contrary to the first part, Evangelisti is developing sound textures here that are comprised of a multitude of detail movements. A profound modification of the musical text is taking place: new graphical characters are being introduced to determine timbre and forms of articulation more precisely, or to designate unconventional playing techniques. As illustrated by example 3.1, dynamic instructions are intended primarily to phase the instrumental layers in and out. Internal movement characterizes the individual layers. The strings change their playing technique from arco a vite (helical movement of the bow) to legno, normale, and legno battuto; while doing so, they perform slow glissandi of which only the direction is given. The wind instruments start with determined pitches, continue with frullato without generating tones, and finally dull controlled tones with trills, tremoli, and larger pitch variations. Such “denatured” sounds merge with the round movements of the brush on the tenor drum, glissandi on the triangle, cymbal rolls, and plucked piano strings.

Only if we bear these early experiences in mind is it possible to correctly assess Evangelisti’s establishment of the Gruppo d’improvvisazione Nuova Consonanza: as continuation—and not interruption—of his compositional activity. Between his concept of sound composition and aleatorics on the one hand and the conception of
the group on the other exists a strict consequence. Let us visualize Evangelisti’s path. First, he attempts to circumvent the cliché formation, which always threatens in traditional notation, using graphical tools and formal ambiguity. Finally, he refrains from the written representation of his musical thoughts as such; he starts to write a book about the physical properties of the sound and the modes of its perception. At the same time, he establishes an improvisation group whose principles dictate that all members must be instrumentalists and composers. If aleatorics were not employed as negation of the musical work, then improvisation is now considered to be the path to another concept of work.
Such a work has three features: it is instantaneous, collective, and impermanent. The term instantaneous composition is used since there is no standard-based behavior that should adapt the material to be processed after multiple interventions. It is a collective work because the compositional subject converges by itself into a general subjectivity. Each player is responsible for his or her own actions and forms the work being created in his or her own way; however, each individual’s contribution is meaningful only in conjunction with the work of the others. Each creation is finally transitional and nonrepeatable; if at all, a tape recording of the compositional action is allowed, but it will be considered more a document than the actual work. This change in perspectives does not lead to the loss of identity and meaning of the instantaneous composition. The limitations on one side correspond to openings on the other side, the side of the material. The respective action—the respective “piece,” if we want to label an improvisation as such—defines itself by the sounds applied, their production processes, and the special improvisation style of the group members. An entire series of new instruments and types of sound generation was introduced by Nuova Consonanza: moving the hair of a violin bow back and forth underneath the strings of a grand piano, breathing and talking into wind instruments, using the grand piano as a resonance body for other instruments, using bassoon mouthpieces for brass instruments, and introducing countless ad hoc constructed noise-producing devices. For all members, a catalog of prohibitions existed that was not that far removed from the principles of Scelsi’s work: do not allow any priority by an individual player, do not produce any sounds tied to the tonal system, do not create any rhythmic cycle, do not insert any memorable motifs, and do not provide any exact repetition of anything past. The “exercises” performed by the group in order to explore the limits of certain sound processes can be considered as the only positive standard; these were regularly performed practices about a pitch, a color group, a behavior, etc. If desired, response forms internalized in this way could be inserted in specific sound situations. The idea of sound as infinite process—without beginning, without extramaterial development, without controlled contrasts, transitions, and conclusions—was demonstrated almost rhetorically in the performances of Nuova Consonanza.
In conclusion, the discussion turns to another composer of Nuova Consonanza: Mario Bertoncini. From 1964 to 1967 Bertoncini composed Cifre, a work for any number of pianists who each are playing on two pianos prepared in different ways. Two aspects of Cifre are of interest in this context. First, the “score” appears as a mixture of traditional notation, action notation, and tablature (see example 3.2).

If we go into more detail, we discover a complex sign system whose purpose is not indeterminacy but exact identification of the musical events and their sequencing. The only freedom exists in the possibility of omitting one or several events, which will then have to be made up in a following pass. Hence, we can notice that the change of notation types is founded in the concrete nature of the single sonic phenomenon. The other relevant aspect of Cifre is the preference of long-sustained sound bands—which contradict the characteristic staccato sound of the piano but perfectly utilize its resonance possibilities. Sound bands are created by brushing cowbells, moving brushes, and paper rolls on piano strings and by moving the hair of a bow back and forth underneath the piano strings—methods already used in the improvisation group.
Bertoncini fell back on the fundamental ideas of Cifre—unstable sound bands, long resonance times, and a functional sound notation—when he started working on different “aeolian sound objects” (harps and gongs) at the beginning of the seventies. Objects of this type are made to vibrate through human breathing or air compressors: a dense network of copper wires creates unstable sound bands with complex overtone spectra. I call them aeolian sound objects and not instruments, since an instrument generally serves as a means for realizing different pieces, whereas Bertoncini plans and builds his objects for a special work in each case. The object constructed in such a way is already the work or a field of possibility that leads to several ways to operate a single musical thought. These are also not “sound sculptures,” because the visual aspect is not in the foreground here. It is important—as it was for Scelsi and Evangelisti—that the composer works directly with and on the subject matter of the sound.

Der Begriff aleatorisch wird immer wieder verwechselt mit dem des Zufalls.… Indem man diese Termini verwechselt, verfälscht man die ganze Situation im Verhältnis zur Sonderstellung von Cage. Cage kann freilich mit diesen Manifestationen weder identifiziert noch in Verbindung gebracht werden, selbst wenn man sich in gewissen Verfahrensweisen auf ihn beziehen kann.
