4

Conceptual Reflections and Literary Evolution in Giacinto Scelsi’s Works
Alessandra Montali
T he aim of this chapter is to provide a comprehensive view of Giacinto Scelsi’s theoretical and literary output.1 It is through such a broad approach we may begin to understand the categories of thought at the core of Scelsi’s entire artistic and, above all, musical oeuvre.
I’ve emphasized Scelsi’s ways of thinking as they evolved through time, starting from an initial phase reflecting the musical-language current between the end of the nineteenth and the beginning of the twentieth centuries, a time when it progressively morphed into a sort of abstract, expressive essentiality at the expense of any rational categorization, superseding the idea that the genesis of an artistic work resides in its intellectuality. Similarly, we will observe the consolidation of Scelsi’s increasingly synthetic poetic language, which reached fruition in his last works through the use of aphorisms and symbols.
In the following I establish premises indispensable to understanding Scelsi’s personal reflections, premises that not only guided his research but also resulted in both the compositions of his later years and his essential concentration on sound depth (infrasonic investigation). Finally, I hope to dispel the image of a musician too superficially classified in the musical panorama of the twentieth century as “isolated.” On the contrary, what emerges from the analysis of Scelsi’s writings is a lucid consciousness of sound’s emancipating movement, born over the course of two centuries and definitively liberated from the theoretical and intellectual structures of the tonal language. According to Adriano Cremonese, the discomfort Scelsi experienced visà-vis the dodecaphonic language—which he studied in Vienna from 1935 to 1936 under Schoenberg’s pupil, Walter Klein—should constitute a demonstration of the “rejection… of music’s raison d’être in which the mind… has regained control over musical matter.”2
I have identified three fundamental periods of reflection into which several of Scelsi’s writings fall:3

Conceptual reflections in “Sens de la musique” (written in 1942, 2nd version in 1944) and historical reflections in Évolution de l’harmonie and Évolution du rythme (written in 1942).
In Son et musique (written between 1953 and 1954) and Art et connaisance (written between 1953 and 1954) Scelsi described his own artistic and human identity by delineating his openness toward Eastern cultures and spirituality. Meanwhile, in the poetic collections Le poids net (in 1949), L’archipel nocturne (in 1954), and La conscience aiguë (in 1955), he experimented with a particular symbolic language, which was important to his later works.
In Il sogno 101 (written in 1980)—his most hermetic writing of this late period, Cercles (in 1986) and Octologo (in 1987)—Scelsi’s favorite mode of expression was the symbolic language, the only one apt to define, or, better suggest, the evocation of his spiritual experience as he wished it to be intuited.

THE 1940s
Analytical Theory
In this initial phase we notice a tentative conceptual elaboration articulated over two fronts: the proposition and application of an analytical methodology for the study and development of music composed between the end of the nineteenth and the first three decades of the twentieth centuries. In fact in “Sens de la musique”4 we observe the elaboration of a complex analytical theory based on the premise that music is for one reason or another a projection of images and state of consciousness. This cannot be described solely by analyses of form and structure of a particular work, its diatonic or chromatic character, discussion of chords, harmonic aggregations and superimpositions, polyphony, atonality, or polytonality, all notions whose meaning may amount to next to nothing.5
The value of Scelsi’s proposal lay in its capacity to clearly define an analytical approach no longer based on the description of a composition’s technical data but, rather, an analytical approach prompted by an investigation of the “correspondences existing between the elements that constitute music and the categories of images projected by the composer in the sound matter.”6 According to Scelsi these categories of images emerged directly from the individual’s fundamental faculties—that is, those elements strictly connected to the essence of one’s being, which Scelsi distinguished as vital/rhythmic, emotive/affective, and intellectual/psychic. Scelsi was adamant about defining the nature of such faculties in terms of the power and energy manifested in individuals through uneven vibrations and intensity. Individuals’ unique sensitivities, he emphasized, determined their abilities to perceive and identify them in the guise of images, sensations, emotions, or psychic states.
Conceptual Reflections and Literary Evolution in Giacinto Scelsi’s Works 55
In such a vision, artistic language came to life as a translation of fundamental faculties, as expression through matter, and, particularly, as the projection of matter in the musical language that conditioned time, melody, formal construction, and, ultimately, harmony. Table 4.1 illustrates the scheme related to such correspondences in synthesis.

In human beings the vital element is a manifestation of the primal movement’s pulsation, which is at the origin of any form of life. From a musical viewpoint Scelsi identified and separated two main expressions of rhythm. The first revealed the quality of its pulsation through the alternation of sound and silence. In this instance, the expression of rhythm seemed, at times, capable of realizing a sort of autonomy when compared to other compositional parameters, like melody, form, and rhythmic patterns. Sometimes it was transformed through the exclusive use of percussion instruments,7 thus obtaining a most complete independence.
Scelsi equated the second expression of rhythm to the concept of duration, emphasizing its function of connecting two poles, the artist’s personal and relative time and absolute time.

The rhythmic language is the expression of the profound rhythms emerging from vital dynamism. But, on the other hand, rhythm is also the expression and the manifestation of duration. Therefore, as it is partly the first condition of existence for man or for the work of art, it also unites and fuses the personal and relative time of the creative artist—through its essence and through the images it creates—to cosmic duration to absolute time. It is apparent that an intellectual structure, an arbitrary series of rhythmic cells, has little to do with the real sense of rhythm, which is, above all, a primordial physical force.8

This idea is fundamental, as it establishes the theoretical background of the conception of time that Scelsi later developed in his infrasonic research. In this instance time was no longer calculated through sequencing, the horizontal flow of rhythmic and melodic cells, but at another level, that of the depth of a single sound.9 Therefore in this type of music one may lose the consciousness of time intended as the chronological and consequential element of the personal and relative time. Instead time unfolds into a new development that represents the material projection of the inner time experienced by the artist in his or her innermost being. This experience coincides with space and sound depth and becomes symbolic of the infinite spaces that the individual can discover in his or her inner being, connecting to the concepts of cosmic duration or absolute time. But if the artist’s inner time can be related and assimilated into cosmic duration and/or absolute time, we find that, as a consequence, the inner rhythm, now turned into free expression of the primal physical propulsion, could become a manifestation, within the human microcosm, of the primal movement or original vibration at the beginning of every living form and the thrust toward transcendence. The primal sound, the Word, and also rhythm are vibrations that pervade all vital activities, including the body, mind, social life, and spiritual life. For this very reason every form of art affects the spiritual life of the artist.10 Besides expressing and recreating the artist’s inner rhythm, modeled after the primal rhythm, the absolute rhythm becomes a quest for the meaning of the Creator’s impetus.
In July 1938, four years before Scelsi penned the first version of “Sens de la musique,” Pierre Jean Jouve, poet, music lover, and Scelsi’s friend, published the essay “La musique et l’état mystique.”11 Based on the analysis of the temporal dimension, Jouve confirmed music’s capacity to project the subjective instant in the eternal. His conclusion was a revelation, reaching the deepest recesses of the human soul: “Music lets us dive into the eternal with the most intimate part of our being, with our most intense pleasure, thus transforming one instant into a state of fervid expectation of the eternal. Through music the less capable individual sees his/her capabilities extended to a larger potential. Through music time is absorbed into religious expectation until the instant is immediately open to eternity.”12 This convergence of thinking helps us place Scelsi’s writings, especially “Sens de la musique,” in a hypothetical context of interests in line with the reflections and cultural exchanges of other intellectuals of the period.
In his writings of the 1950s13 Scelsi elaborated further on the concept of depth: the sonic third dimension. In so doing, he arrived at a radical choice for concentrating and organizing the same musical form in relation to the continuous movement and fluctuation of the Klang.14 In Quattro pezzi per orchestra (ciascuno su una nota), composed in 1960,15 the sound entity is modified continuously through movements of spectral dilation and contraction, thus assuming a spherelike dimension, vital in its intrinsic capability for expansion, movement, and pulsation. In such a sonic pulsation process, no abrupt fragmentation or changes can be produced; even modifications “are artfully placed so the differences are not perceived as intervals—that is, as proportional relationships between distinct elements—but as ever significant modifications of the state of sole entity.”16
In this case, we could align Scelsi’s temporal dimension with Kramer’s category of “vertical time: a temporal continuum of the unchangeable, in which there are no separate events and in which all seems to be part of an eternal present.”17 I deem it interesting to point out the relationship between this new temporal direction and the sense of continuity thereafter produced: “When the instant becomes the whole composition, the discontinuity disappears, giving way to a total temporal differentiation, an ‘hour’ whose duration seems infinite however feels as an instant. Time flows not longitudinally, but it appears to flow vertically.”18
Scelsi’s theory of fundamental elements dictates that one must always try to personalize the categories of images that belong to the composer in order to identify the projection of their artistic matter. We could hypothesize that behind Scelsi’s concept of duration—thereafter morphed into concept of sound as unity—the instant is always present and unchangeable, albeit pulsating. One could anticipate an aptitude for overcoming the essential ambivalence of time,19 making possible every personal projection into the future—be it a project, progress, expectation, or success—that comes closer to the inevitable: death! In fact, music that affirms the rejection of such ambivalence would express itself through “incessant, unpredictable ruptures,… themes barely sketched out and quickly forgotten,… rhythms started and suddenly reversed. The lack of unity equals a negation of the very sense of duration, a defense against anxiety caused by destruction or death. No reprise, no return to the past, is possible… because this would presuppose some unity of time [that], at the end, would coincide with the beginning, death with birth.”20
Instead Scelsi’s idea of duration, expressed in his music through the exploration and valorization of sound depth, becomes the projection of death’s serene acceptance in the assimilation of personal time. The eternal loses its meaning when speaking of a before or an after, of birth or death, because everything unfolds in an eternal present that reconciles the extremes. This hypothesis appears to be validated by Scelsi’s later musings, found as complete expressions in Il sogno 101. In that work, under the section aptly titled “The Return,” Scelsi describes the experience of remembering past lives and reincarnation:

I always feel falling
    Now I have three sides
    They move away
    What shape have I?
I no longer have a shape
    Yes
    Now I am an egg.
But all is dark in here!
I feel a certain warmth
    I did not have this sensation
    Before.
What is happening to me
    I can no longer think
    All is dark
    And I hear a faint tapping
    As from a hearth
    What shall it be?
Once again
    I must wait for
    The light.21

Ultimately, we observe that the relevance attributed to the artist/creator’s personal time seems to anticipate (as Scelsi mentioned in 1944) some arguments properly pertaining to modern semantics and psychologically oriented musical research. In fact, the latter shows that “the states of integration or disintegration of the interior life and the ego represent the base of the musical time’s lived experience. Thus, these states, or, rather, these counteropposed dynamic structures,… offer the musical discourse its most profound meaning, beyond the cultural filters that condition the listener, albeit partially.”22
This definition of the emotional element undoubtedly is less articulate than the preceding one. Scelsi explained how the whole emotional realm is projected in music through melody. Here the individual recognizes, with immediate ease, the self-evident symbolic representation of the personal, emotional, and affective past, whose awareness he immediately acquires once having gained consciousness of his own living being. Conforming to the theory of the fundamental elements’ holistic approach, Scelsi distinguished the relationship between the two realms under analysis, notwithstanding the fact that melody can, at times, distance itself from the rhythmic element until, in some cases, it detaches itself altogether in order to reach independence. This can occur only when an emotion is abstracted from the vital physical element as it approaches closely the realm of the intellectual and the psychic. In fact one could attribute to the intellectual element the function of a speculative instrument capable of logical organization and of realizing architectures by inserting in a rational form all lived experiences at the emotional, spiritual (psychic), and rhythmic levels.
In the second version of “Sens de la musique,” Scelsi hinted at a peculiar quality of the intellect, the “faculty of identification,” referring to the capacity for establishing relationships between precise elements, which constitute, thereafter, the base of their inherent rational construction.23 Scelsi’s research revealed the complex role of the intellect as an intermediary between the rational and the psychic, able to recognize and make sense of experiences perceived at a high level of consciousness.

Yes, the faculty of identification of perception also appears to vary in intensity and in clarity according to its own laws, conditioned by the character of the perceptions—principally by those of the psychic order. For, as we saw at the time, the faculty of identification involves both the physical and the nonphysical, and through the nonphysical aspect it is one of the faculties of man that, more than others, links him to the highest cosmic forces.
Finally it is this faculty that joins the artist, as with the mystic, to the domain that we call supernatural. [This faculty] does not become conscious, does not appear to our consciousness, when it is clarified and known by the proportions or limits of what is possible.24

This passage brings us to the last of the fundamental elements, the psychic. To it belong the less-evident and -recognizable images rather than those reflecting emotional or vital order, even if at times the latter—after moving to the subconscious—could reemerge at this level in a disrupting manner. In addition, there are images of a purely psychic order. Scelsi pondered this faculty only in the first version of “Sens de la musique,” thus realizing in it the convergence of parameters such as those that characterize “spiritualism, magic, metaphysics, universal consciousness, dream, blood, and ancestral memory.”25
By attributing a fundamental role to the psychic or transcendent dimension in the very structure of the human personality, Scelsi anticipated some ideas concerning transpersonal psychology that were developed toward the end of the 1960s.26 This too confirmed that

normal consciousness, as defined by rationality, comprehends only what may be perceived by the senses and excludes the intuitive. Therefore, this state includes the concrete and objective dimension but not life’s transcendent dimension. Rational consciousness is thus selective, dividing, and dualistic. Beyond this type of consciousness [one must examine] the superior levels of consciousness and their relationships to knowledge and creativity. When corresponding to diverse identifications of reality, the levels of consciousness evolve from sensory to spiritual, according to a hierarchic structure spanning the particular and the universal, until reaching the cosmic consciousness, which comprises the whole universe.… This ultimate consciousness is devoid of barriers and includes the imminent as the transcendental, the relative as the absolute, all encompassed into a supreme unity.27

Furthermore, the psychic element, by virtue of its quality of modified status visà-vis rational consciousness, allows the individual to evade the perception of his own inner time, thus arriving at the intuition of an eternal, absolute time,28 that “duration” we have just considered in the dual aspect of the rhythmic element.
The psychic element is self-projected in harmony. In musical terms, says Scelsi, we could associate the concept of harmony to the basic definition of the Klang within the parameters of functional theory:29 In such a case “this term must be intended in its most accepted significance, not as ‘sound’ or ‘chord’ but as a complex sonic entity resulting from the concomitant superior or inferior frequencies—that is, the harmonic sounds (superior or inferior).”30 Ultimately it is the same spectral movement of the sound, the concentration and dilation through the harmonics that constitute it, that determines its roundness, its spherical shape and depth, and which ultimately reveals its solid harmonic consistency.
Historical Investigation
The theory of the fundamental elements enabled Scelsi to explain why diverse elements, reflected in the artistic language, emerged from time to time not only in the works of a single composer but also in the course of various periods. Therefore the second line of investigation consists in applying this theory to historical and musicological inquiries. Such was the catalytic premise of both Évolution du rythme and Évolution de l’harmonie, penned by Scelsi in 1942. In them he highlighted the emergence of rhythmic and psychic elements during the nineteenth and the twentieth centuries.
For the greater part of the nineteenth century the preeminence of the intellect and the doctrine of affections corresponded to the formal construction and melody in the musical language’s architecture. However, the century’s end brought with it a different orientation:

The relationship between the four elements turned in favor of the psychic and rhythmic elements until it reached an anti-intellectual and antiemotive reaction, which presented itself under various guises, from neospiritualism to theosophy to metaphysics and to the vulgarization and popularization of Oriental philosophies and doctrines. Furthermore, we see such an evolution in the reevaluation of intuition, as well as the exploration of the unconscious, the rediscovery of African art… and the fashionable black music, from the revival of the cult [of ] the human body to the exaltation of the collective values, from the discovery of relativity to non-Euclidean geometry. In every field we see the affirmation of the new and diverse conception of the matter, life, the becoming, which, by abolishing all old oppositions and dualisms, gave birth to the idea of the matter’s identity and spirit under the form of energy and duration propelled toward the concept of a universal, transcendental unity. In art as well as in science, in philosophy and in politics, the orientation became psychic and rhythmic. All domains, tendencies, and [research] were united in the struggle toward the discovery and the expression of a new dimension. Under all forms one could detect a conscious or unconscious search for the time, space, and duration.31

Already, from the analysis of the fundamental elements, we notice the emergence of the particular attention Scelsi devoted to the rhythmic element; in fact he dedicated an entire essay to this purposes, Évolution du rythme. In it Scelsi detailed the emancipation of rhythm with the premise that the beginning of such a process must be credited to Liszt32 and, above all, to the music of the Russians.33 He reiterated the well-grounded opinion that the development of harmony was to be found in the roots of the last manifestation of Wagnerian chromaticism, which remained connected to a lyrical and formal conception of the composition and thus to the nineteenth-century predominance of rational and affective elements.
Debussy was also part of this process, albeit indirectly, through fragmentation and rhythmic dissolution that, according to Scelsi, reached independence in some passages of his String Quartet. But it was with Stravinsky that rhythm finally came to the foreground, superseding other elements. For instance, if in Firebird the affective element was not completely emancipated, Scelsi singled out Petrushka and its second tableau, in which rhythm showed the harmonic disarticulation that allows listeners to perceive an already extreme autonomy: “One clearly has the impression of a vital element [that] progressively assaults and destroys the bar lines, striking mightily with one arm and then the other, its inclination to attack and upset the established order. Rhythmic eruption of Prometheus chained primal motion, dynamic impulse, a veritable giant pulling on its chains—to humans it would appear stripped, terrifying, immense. And it would be the Rite. Emotion and intellect were to be swept away. Men would live the cosmic primordial breath, the élan vital.”34
At this point in his disquisition Scelsi mentions jazz, observing how its basic rhythmic matrix at this point in compositional history no longer has the vital support of the melodic elements and no longer serves as the backbone of the formal construction but instead realizes a language after its own impulse. In it we observe an absolute balance of sound and silence, distinguished only by the fact that they belong to differently planned strata.
Moving further toward rhythm’s total autonomy, and adopting the means that permit the individual to connect to the absolute duration and universal vibration, Scelsi refocused his attention on the African and Eastern peoples. They did reach a true understanding of rhythm, unaltered by intellectual mediations. For this reason the rhythm they employed as musical expression was a vital component of the cosmic rhythm. Take, for instance, the use of the tam-tam in African practices. Scelsi averred that “in all its varieties (ritual, musical, and instrumental) this must be interpreted and understood as a means of achieving a state of collective consciousness by the deregulation of the individual physiological rhythms and their synchronization with the tellurian and universal rhythms. The pauses in this music are really a portion of space measured by time. Ecstasy or euphoria is produced by the sensation of the disruption of personal rhythm—that is, of individual and relative time—and by of the fusion of the being with a current of cosmic rhythm, in time and duration.”35
The rhythmic dimension is even more developed among Asian musicians, who are capable of creating parallel strata consisting of diverse rhythmic plans, a true counterpoint. Such major articulation was appreciated and made relevant by Scelsi, not as much for their capacity to produce a more complex intellectual game but “for the harnessing by the consciousness of several rhythmic elements and their subsequent projection [involving] a more powerful and precise absorption of creative forces.… It would explain why, if black music produces a sensation of wild euphoria, Asian music by contrast seems not to have terrestrial characteristics or limits. With its continuous and ethereal dynamism, its irresistible force, where it skirts and crosses infinite trajectories, man’s personal time not only seems to melt into that of nature, of the earth, but seems to lose and confound itself with the vast breathing and vital cycles of cosmic absolute time. With this, we are approaching the fourth dimension.”36
The reference to the fourth dimension is revelatory at the present stage of our discussion. In it we recognize proof of Scelsi’s vivid presence in the intellectual Parisian milieu of his time, a period instrumental in promoting the passage from mannered nineteenth-century exoticism. Scelsi considered certain degenerative aspects of these superficial works in the second part of his essay, quoting Zolla in order to comprehend the authentic influence of primitive art and its metaphysics. “An epidemic of descriptive music, both white and black, accompanied by rituals or warrior dances,” he says, “has taken over concerts, not to mention Indian tribal celebrations and the full gamut of exotic flavors.”37
In fact during the 1930s Artaud and Daumal, the organizers of the surrealist movement’s secession, discovered that “tribal arts are not feral but deal with alternative states of consciousness. They are not manifestations of existential anguish but pivotal points for activating the knowledge of distinct degrees of the being.”38 Zolla confirms that such a passage was stimulated indeed by the study of the fourth dimension, an inquiry advanced for the first time by James Hinton in 1880 and then absorbed and discussed by Ouspenki, Gurdjieff, and Apollinaire. The thematics proposed by Hinton, although not in a direct fashion, were surely known to Scelsi through theosophical doctrines; he studied in great depth many themes related to theosophy in reading the texts of Blavatsky, Alice A. Bailey, and Arthur E. Powell, which to this day can be found in his library.
In the fourth dimension the temporal perception is self-modifying when compared to the ordinary experience; music aids the individual in detaching from his or her chronological and physiological rhythms and in realizing an enlargement of the parameters of the consciousness necessary to harmonize his or her inner time with that of nature and, through a further expansion, with the cosmic time.39 Scelsi’s original text, typed and bearing numerous erasures and handwritten emendations, stops abruptly after mentioning the fourth dimension, a theme reserved, Scelsi noted, for future investigation.
Scelsi’s narrative, reorganized in a publication edited by Adriano Cremonese, resumed with Stravinsky. Scelsi wrote that Chinese music influenced Stravinsky’s Rossignol from a harmonic viewpoint, but simultaneous superimposition of rhythmic strata was only achieved in the masterpiece Les noces. However, Stravinsky acquired such a capacity not through his knowledge of Eastern music but, rather, through the Bach model: “Of all the European musicians,” Scelsi insisted, “Bach is the only one who possessed a rhythmic dynamism [that], though not based on syncopated progressions, miraculously approaches an independent, truly rhythmic language.… He created separation among the levels, which in another sense created relationships of space and time (these would perhaps not result otherwise, with one rhythmic line taken in isolation).”40
Stravinsky’s subsequent neoclassical evolution was explained in relation to the reemergence in his music of the affective and intellectual elements. But according to Scelsi, without the irrefutable validity of works like Oedipus rex and Symphony of Psalms, Stravinsky would have hardly attained—in these elements—the expressive heights reached in the rhythmic and harmonic elements, unless one considered a total mutation of the proper balance between the fundamental elements.
Thereafter Scelsi equated the positive use of rhythmic language in those compositions enhanced by “affective images”41 to the power of the rhythmic language in the experimentations promoted by the Futurists, which revealed “the imperious desire for rhythmic manifestation above everything and for itself.”42 Scelsi himself took part in the Futurists’ exploits with “Rotative” (1929), whose first version for three pianos, brass, and percussion was conducted in Paris by Pierre Monteaux in 1931.43 At that time Scelsi was a member of the Société Musicale Indépendante, which assumed a pivotal role in the promotion of avant-garde music between 1910 and 1935.44
In Évolution de l’harmonie Scelsi investigated the relationship between the psychic element and the development of harmony, beginning again with Wagner. In fact in Wagner such a relationship revealed a perfect correspondence. “On the one hand,” Scelsi pointed out, Wagner’s “works and writings provide evidence of the extent to which race, ancestral blood, and his particular brand of mysticism have primordial importance. However, the impulses and psychic images appear to be sufficiently close to emotion and subordinate to the intellectual element.”45 This relationship is reflected in a musical language by the fact that, on the one hand, it moves away from tonality—thanks to the predominance of chromaticism and to the use of the eleventh chord46—but, on the other, by the fact that it clearly manifests a tendency toward lyricism and a will to realize architectural and formal structures.
Russian music, as well as Wagner’s, conditioned the evolution of harmony geared toward the psychic and rhythmic elements. Furthermore, the knowledge of Eastern scales and modes had a definite influence over the most important linguistic achievements for both Debussy and Stravinsky. Scelsi particularly devoted the second part of Évolution de l’harmonie to Debussy, who had always been averse to rationalism and formalism and, consequently, distanced himself from the intellectual and affective elements characteristic of Wagner’s music. In Debussy one notices a close correspondence between musical language (harmonic technique) and the inner evolution of the fundamental elements. An analysis of Debussy’s harmony shows two principal poles: double chordal harmonies built on superimposed thirds and the whole-tone scale. The former’s harmonic ambiguity and the ensuing limitation imposed on the dominant and subdominant produced by the interval of second—as well as the frequent reliance on Eastern and chromatic scales or the clustering of separate chords in distant tonalities—lead to atonality. Scelsi observed how in Debussy the predominance of images of affective character that often tended toward the assimilation of psychic characters (Scelsi cited La mer as an example) corresponded to a still-rational harmony even if already oriented toward the affirmation of its own autonomy.
In addition to Debussy, two other musicians contribute to this second phase in harmony’s evolution. The first was Richard Strauss. From his early works Strauss had adhered to Wagnerian stylistic characteristics, achieving autonomy with Elektra, an opera in which “the Wagnerian concepts are brought to their final consequences and complexity.… His work—its dominant elements manifested with incredible intensity—reflected the revelations of an experienced forty-five-year-old man. Strauss could go no further; after his masterpiece there were only increasingly light and superficial, even banal, expressions from Rosenkavalier to Schlagobers to Arabella.”47
The second composer contributing to harmony’s second phase was Alexander Scriabin, whose music, according to Scelsi, revealed an extraordinary evolution that brought the composer in a short period of time—between his op. 36 (1903) and op. 74 (1914)—to the elaboration of a language that foreshadowed Alban Berg’s.48 Scelsi felt this trajectory was influenced by either Wagnerian chromaticism or Debussy’s harmonic language. Another possible influence was Scriabin’s own research in the area of extreme harmonics, prompted by his—and Debussy’s—psychic, religious, and esoteric experiences, which in turn prompted the creation of Prometheus, the last preludes, and the last sonatas.49
In these works the reference to Debussy’s experimentation with harmonics is of particular interest, as it constitutes a testimonial of the primary sources regarding the sound depth Scelsi continued to research in the mature phase of his artistic output. Scelsi’s essay on the evolution of harmony is incomplete, therefore, even if judged by the shape he gave it; an inevitable analysis of the atonal phase could be dedicated to “Schoenberg and his pupils. Furthermore, perhaps as a reflection, the discourse could center around the relationship between atonality and the twelve-tone system. More than Schoenberg’s and Webern’s, the major figure here seems to be that of Alban Berg, the ideal Master of the 1930s.”50
To conclude, Scelsi demonstrated in both Évolution du rhythme and Évolution de l’harmonie the possibility of laying a concrete basis for a new type of historical analysis that, grounded in the theory of the fundamental elements, would consider and adopt formal, harmonic, and stylistic analyses as a means to bringing into evidence those correspondences, human experiences, and profound relations that determine and condition the artistic expression. Scelsi emerges from these writings as a profound connoisseur of the intellectual and musical orientation of his time, willing to institute a system of musicological criticism based on new analytical categories. It is important to mention both Scelsi’s involvement with the international musical panorama, as demonstrated in those two essays of 1942, as well as his generosity in organizing a series of contemporary music concerts held in the late 1930s at Rome’s Sala Capizucchi with Nicola Costarella and Goffredo Petrassi.51 Scelsi’s involvement in contemporary music is noteworthy, as his personal reflection on it, in addition to his interests in yoga and spirituality, ultimately unleashed the great revolution of his mature language.
This first period, characterized by Scelsi’s urge to define his personal artistic identity within the musical panorama of those years, could not have come to a close absent a profound human and artistic crisis. The theoretical considerations he’d matured during those years inspired his search for a new and personal artistic language, which he considered the true expression of fundamental elements emerging in both his conscience and, more generally, the twentieth century’s evolution of the musical language. His much-labored 1948 composition La naissance du verbe for mixed chorus and orchestra52 was a vivid manifestation of this crisis; evident in it is Scelsi’s emerging desire to research, which foreshadowed “the radical Scelsi of the 1950s mixed with something else that [would] no longer occur in his music: contrapuntal passages in a bleak atmosphere.”53 These elements remain the last vestiges of a crisis that resulted in four years of compositional silence, years from which Scelsi emerged cured and restored, thanks to the recuperating therapy that resulted from a new way of listening to sound. Scelsi articulated the experience thus:

I have experienced something like this by myself without knowing the story: I was sick in a clinic. In clinics there is always a piano hidden somewhere. Having found one, I began to play the same notes over and over until I heard someone saying, “That one is crazier than us!” By repeating the same note for a long time, the sound becomes bigger, so much, in fact, that one hears harmonies around it and around oneself while being enveloped by the sound. I can assure you that it is an extraordinary experience; the sound contains an entire universe, with harmonies never heard otherwise. The sound fills the space around you; you can swim in it. The sound is both a creator and destroyer agent. It is therapeutic: it can cure or kill. Ultimately, once you penetrate the sound, you become part of it, swallowed little by little until there is no need for another sound. Music today [has] become an intellectual game, combining sound with another, etc.… Everything is at the center of the sound, the entire universe fills the space, and all possible sounds are contained in it.54

THE 1950S: HUMAN AND ARTISTIC IDENTITY
The literary works belonging to this period are subdivided in two groups: (1) a series of conversations between Scelsi and friends taped in 1953 and 1954 and later transcribed in the volumes Son et musique and Art et connaissance and (2) three collections of poems, Le poids net, L’archipel nocturne, and La conscience aiguë, written between 1949 and 1955 and published by Guy Levis Mano in Paris.
Art as a Vehicle of Ascendency: The Yoga of Sound
The balance between the fundamental elements determines and conditions of a person’s artistic creative works, a process that Scelsi exemplified in Art et connaissance.55 He argued that myriad boxes or drawers “hidden” in one’s self come to the fore at a determined historical moment and reveal their content. These boxes or drawers represent a person’s infinite potential: the diverse and ever-changing balance between the fundamental elements, seen through various historical epochs and individuals. From time to time their emergence alights one’s consciousness. Such a phenomenon does not occur without trauma, though. In fact, when it does occur, the individual often remains in a state of confusion, unable to recognize his or her newly acquired capabilities and will need further intellectual challenges in order to define and integrate them consciously in the delicate relationships developed by his or her own mental schemes. In other words, the experiments individuals can conduct now at ordinary and common levels of consciousness—as it has happened in the twentieth century with the emergence of the psychic experience—can morph into artistic language.
As far as the psychic element’s early manifestations in the twentieth century are concerned, Scelsi references the exploits of the Dadaists and surrealists. Jean Cocteau was the possible link with the Parisian avant-garde movements, and he no doubt fascinated a young Scelsi so much that Scelsi kept notes of aphorisms and fragments mostly uttered or penned by Cocteau. Furthermore, based on Cocteau’s text, Scelsi composed “L’oracle” for voice and piano, performed for the first time in 1938 at Rome’s Sala Capizucchi.56 One also detects a clear surrealist influence in Scelsi’s four youthful short stories collected in Extraits de son journal (1928).57 The same can be said of the title of Scelsi’s first composition, “Chemin du Coeur” (1929) for violin and piano, which brings to mind Indicateur des chemins de coeur, a book by Tristan Tzara, published in 1928.58
Scelsi appreciated these avant-garde movements not so much for their polemics and their attack on society but for the new conception of artistic creation their irrational approach brought to life. The former was fully reflected in the importance attributed to automatic writing,59 a technique that aimed to “free the forces of our unconscious ego even when in a state of alertness… [in order] to find ways to let the hidden voice of our spirit intervene in the dissolute life of the present time. That voice that the most brutal of conventions tries to push back.”60
Scelsi concluded in Art et connaissance that if the Dadaists departed from the renunciation of logical discourse in order to reach and postulate the ephemerality of the gesture as aesthetic canon—acting in a provoking, often self-destructive fashion—in reality they were manifesting a need to conduct experiments on other, deeper, levels than those commonly known.61 These avant-garde movements were constrained by their inability to move past the exploration of the unconscious capacity and the oneiric experience. Even Salvador Dalì entered the polemical fray by supporting the concept of surrealist automatic improvisation, claiming that the use of images was authentically irrational, that they “overcome the dominion of spirits and representations that are subject of psychoanalysis.”62
At this stage Scelsi specified that these movements “lacked a deeper psychological technique necessary to reach some states of consciousness in a different time-space order.”63 What he deducted from these experiences, at least from the 1950s onward, was the creative process—which, by extending to extreme consequences the practice of surrealist automatism, resulted in a modified state of consciousness. Consider, for instance, Scelsi’s improvisation at the piano or ondioline.
On this point one might notice an interesting convergence with the thinking of Vladimir Jankélévitch, another important intellectual active in Paris at the time, whose 1955 essay defined improvisation as “the mystery of mental gestation… like the courage and virtue of the outposts, the virtue of the will first and foremost and in immediate contact with danger, so that improvisation is the first step of the creative virtue that starts from nothing, the white page. It is the beginning of the beginning. … The form emerges out of a primordial mystery” and “takes shape incomprehensibly in the limbo of the unformed and deformed. The order vacillates extensively in the nebula of this great maternal disorder, which is the beginning of everything.”64
In reality Scelsi’s improvisations surpassed the surrealists’ experiences, as he managed to move toward deeper states of consciousness, real and true trances witnessed by individuals close to him.65 Therefore the improvisation then assumed the character of profound meditation, a state in which individual consciousness is lost, enabling the meditator to become a conduit whose power leads to intuitions relating to universal consciousness and transcendental realities. Cellist Frances-Marie Uitti recalld that Scelsi wanted “to compose in a pure state, a state of contact with another human being, another spirit, with other intuitions. He wanted to use the logical portion of the brain and wanted to be fully liberated of the body, to improvise and to be in the sound at that very moment… it was like a dialogue with God, a solitary dialogue that proceeds vertically, a profound dialogue, not a chat with a friend.”66
As we continue to place Scelsi’s writings and interests in a historical and intellectual context, let us note an important finding in Scelsi’s still-uncataloged private library: a text on yoga, printed in Paris in 1953.67 Exceptionally, this copy of Yoga: Science de l’homme integral bears Scelsi’s signature and many handwritten annotations. It is comprised of a very interesting collection of essays on yoga by P. M. Oursel, M. Eliade, F. Shuon, and R. Guenon; and on Tantras by A. Danielou. In addition, J. Evola and A. Migot elaborated on the relationship between yoga and Christian hesychasm, A. Bloom and S. Siddheswarananda discussed alchemy, M. Aniane pointed out the relationship between yoga and artistic creation, J. Bruno elaborated on the relationship between yoga and psychoanalysis, and the volume concluded with an essay by S. Aurobindo. Bruno’s essay in particular has strong affinities with Scelsi’s assertions, and the numerous penciled underlining show what a close reading Scelsi gave it. Yoga: Science de l’homme integral discusses distinguishing between the persistent, labored tension that precedes inspiration and cerebral—almost paroxysmal—hyperactivity,68 based on the stimulation of inner images, and the apparently opposite aptitude that leads instead to an enriched psychic potential.69 Here we refer to meditative yoga techniques that prompt immobility, inner silence, and emptiness, creating a mystic state that transforms the human body into an instrument of superior perception. The same distinction was emphasized by Scelsi in Art et connaissance when he wrote about the purpose of the faculty of artistic creation. He defined such a state as “man’s ability to project out of himself and to crystallize, as I said, in a sonorous, verbal, or plastic medium.”70 Shortly after he refers to Eastern creationist methodologies based instead on the “purpose of the artist, which is to crystallize a moment of duration and articulate it through a medium.”71
In this way Scelsi repositioned artistic inspirations as a powerful means for allowing the composer access to a mystic, extraordinary state of consciousness. About such a state we find a precise description in another text dealing with spirituality, a book also belonging to and signed by Scelsi.

There is a vast subliminal zone beyond our mind, our life, our physical consciousness. It consists of the most sophisticated vital and physical interior sustained by a psychic substance.… While we find ourselves on the surface cut off from the world surrounding us, except the insufficient contacts with the exterior mind and senses, in these inner expanses, the barrier between us and the rest of the existence is so thin that [they] can be easily breached. In these expanses we sense the action of the world, of our mind, and of life—secret forces that constitute universal and individual existence.72

Only in these terms may the artist be exposed to—and receptive to—levels of consciousness that would allow the artist to establish contact with those superior psychic forces that Scelsi, echoing the Indian tradition, called deva.73 In Son et musique Scelsi specified that the deva have a beneficial influence on humanity and adopt sounds to inculcate new vibrations into the world, which in turn accounts for the individual’s psychic positive, moral, and spiritual modifications. This way, the artist becomes an intermediary, a messenger, and a channel through which the deva act to take full advantage of humanity’s spiritual evolution.74
From this viewpoint the artistic experience reaches a clear delineation in Art et connaissance, as a true and proper ascetic pathway to research at the service of the creative forces. Therefore if, on one hand, research propels a thrust toward transcendence, then, on the other hand, the artist’s task concentrates on the power that projects in the work of art of this type of psychic experience—contact—illumination. This capacity differentiates and characterizes the artist when compared to other individuals and, above all, makes his or her work a special event whose final scope transcends pure technical ability.
In particular Scelsi indicates the upward journey that the musician must follow while developing the capacity to listen to sound: “It is on the cognitions’ and vibrations’ octaves that one must exercise his own faculties of perception and identification in order to operate humbly to the service of the very faculties that have so conditioned him for the most marvelous gift ever bestowed upon any living being to work on this earth.”75 The perception of the harmonics that Scelsi wishes one to experience through intense sound listening is very similar to another meditative technique—Tibetan harmonic chant.76 In fact both aim to reach “a mental state of emptiness, yet iridescent and resonant, that… in reality is the perception of our being at the most profound state as sound vibration.”77
The adepts, according to Scelsi, first manage the hearing of their own inner sound, the bearer of equilibrium amid the fundamental elements’ levels that allows them to perceive the devas’ new sound vibrations. The value of the mantra factor is clear here.78 Scelsi attributes it to sound strong enough to solicit the awakening of functions not commonly utilized. The perception of sound as vibration or energy, coming in contact with and acting on the individual’s physical and psychological structures (a common thought to many religious traditions), is endorsed nowadays by the most advanced scientific researchers like Alfred Tomatis, who wrote that “it is an error to think that the sound is perceived only by the ear.… The sound we produce, like our language, transmits a series of small touches to our corporeal image and to our peripheral nervous system.… We transform the structure of our body by speaking—by speaking to it, to be exact—since the body is the first to be interested in the sound just produced.”79
In synthesis we can confirm that Scelsi’s conception of the “path” to spiritual knowledge was developed along the following phases: From the experience of intense sound listening, through the perception and distinction of the harmonics’ series, to the ability to internalize it and find one’s own mantra sound. This process includes tireless repetition, which, while contributing to the rebalance of man’s fundamental elements, also becomes vital in accessing a modified state of consciousness through which new psychic perceptions and contacts may be experienced. Finally, the projection of such contacts in the artwork completes the last leg of this knowledge journey.
Although we have unearthed no fanciful and eccentric theory to examine, the consequences of the journey Scelsi embarked upon in his own life were reflected in his work. Remember, for instance, his discovery upon listening for an extended period of time to a sound played repeatedly on the piano while a patient in that psychiatric institution. The exercise not only offered the ear a new depth but could also have had a therapeutic and rehabilitating effect as well.80 Furthermore, the in-depth experiences of irrational faculties such as automatic writing or improvisation were projected in the works of this mature period.
Knowing and comprehending Scelsi’s writings is of fundamental importance, because it is in these writings that he provided conscious expression of his human and artistic identity. Hence through their examination one can define not only the background of his spiritual conception but also, and most importantly, the justification for his musical language, according to the direction taken by the infrasonic investigation. Sound acquires a new value. It assumes independence and autonomous significance when compared to the other sounds forming the scale or with which it connects itself dialectically through the intervallic structures. Sound becomes cosmic sound, sonic energy, and primal sound as creator of all things through its infinite expansion. Therefore, the capacities to overcome barriers led Scelsi to the transcendental experience, which consisted, once again, of sound now expressed in the total realization of its potentialities. Music acquires meaning in the rediscovery of the prime element, the primordial sound, a profound sense that does not warrant a precise reference to any cultural or linguistic system.
Naturally in this new conception of sound, traditional categories of frequency and duration are no longer sufficient—the very reason Scelsi, in Son et musique, adds depth as a better conduit to the idea of a dynamic sound in continuous expansion. Such is the sound’s third dimension, even if—as Scelsi reiterated in the writing mentioned above—evidence of it has not been found in the musical language (as it has been in painting, through the use of perspective). Its reality is based on intuition, and it is scientifically supported by the existence of harmonic sounds, which amplify the single sound’s perspective, thus revealing its structural complexity, amplitude, and pluridimensionality.
The capacity to perceive sound’s spherical dimension has been regarded as Giacinto Scelsi’s secret,81 despite the fact that he predicted that it would become common knowledge to many by the end of the century “through the acquisition of a more refined faculty of perception or through a state of consciousness that allows a broader approximation of reality.”82 A 1920s text by Rudolf Steiner detailed how popular the idea of teaching new levels of perceived reality had become. In Scelsi’s personal copy of this text we find an illuminating reference to sound depth determined by harmonic sounds:

I would think that nowadays, in the musical experience, it would be possible to form a conviction on the fact that diverse individuals experience in different ways (let us stay away from the so called “real” music) a single sound… [and that] such diverse experience acts somehow on their comprehension of music. In particular, one could clearly perceive, I believe, that today a tendency exists in the very individual who experiences music to penetrate deeply into sound in a certain way. Thus it is clear that by listening to a sound one can remain either on its surface or penetrate into it more deeply.83

Referencing Goethe’s theory of colors, Scelsi points out the fact that the type of sound under discussion is preexistent in its sensitive and physical manifestations. Vibrations are not the origins but, rather, the means, the conduit through which sound manifests itself to our limited hearing ability. The amplified opportunity of our capability to hear, what Scelsi called the ground of sound’s manifestation, could lead to the direct knowledge of sound depth through the acquisition of a new state of consciousness. “In other words,” Scelsi explained, “everything seems to exist in a latent state. Everything in the cosmos seems to preexist in all possible forms, and the manifestations seem to be produced—whether materially or mentally and no matter to what degree—when the ‘terrain’ is favorable. Man can expand and enlarge the spectrum of ‘terrain.’ Certainly other sounds, other colors will be discovered and demonstrated. Just think of it!”84
Moving on from these theoretical premises, Scelsi elaborated on a revolutionary creative method85 that was based on the decomposition of sound in the spectral parameters of the harmonic formants, “the dissolution, therefore, of the presumed ultimate element of any imaginable music instead of its composition of such elements.”86
THE COLLECTION OF POEMS: TOWARD SILENCE
Following the composition of La nascita del verbo in 1948, Scelsi’s musical production fell victim to an inevitable block caused by, as we have already hinted, a human and creative crisis. During those years of reflection Scelsi acquired the mature consciousness of his identity, his works, and the very significance of his art. In this very delicate period Scelsi elected to express himself through poetic language. The publication of three short collections of poems—Le poids net (1949), L’archipel nocturne (1954), and La conscience aiguë (1955)—was endorsed by Pierre Jean Jouve87 in Paris for the publishing house of Guy Levis Mano.88 A few emendations and hasty handwriting reveal the impulsive genesis of these poetic writings, which, when compared to the theoretical essays, crowded with changes, notes, afterthoughts—all proof of Scelsi’s struggle toward the realization of a coherent synthesis of his thinking89—evidence the fragmented character, illumination of conscience, and essential psychic experiences that, for this very reason, do not reach immediate fruition. On the contrary, Scelsi seems not to have cared much about the role of the reader, believing that accessibility ought to be reserved to only a few initiated. Such characteristic thought was common among French poets of the period who were interested in the function of art as access to comprehending everything. These artists often experimented with a true separation from the general public.90
Their common denominator was the exploration of altered states of consciousness, even if accomplished through different means: From Pierre Jean Jouve’s fusion of Roman Catholicism and Freudian probing of the psyche91 to the hallucinatory prose of Henri Michaux, which was indeed conceived under the influence of hallucinogens,92 to the transcendental dimension of Scelsi’s inner void.

Immense
La triste opacité des choses
    déchire en nous l’espace
    brusquement révélé
    tandis que naît
    au fond du vide
    et de la nuit
    la pensée
    unique
    sans
    fin93

It is interesting to note how this poetic itinerary is closer to a language of aphorisms, myths, and symbols than to a true poetic language. Its extreme conciseness, reservoir of essentiality, and expressive depth typical of the tradition offered by the “Paths to Teaching”94 reveals an inner elaboration of the revolution in the very musical language that Scelsi pursued in those years. In addition, it also revealed the central and intermediate passage between the phase of theoretical and speculative elaboration and the poetics of maturity made evident through symbolic, oneiric images, “to the graphic forms and the empty breath, until total draining.”95
With its evocative power, the poetic language becomes the key to access the symbolism of Scelsi’s mature writing, like Cercles or Il sogno 101. Let us think about the importance assumed by the circle and the line, combined symbols that Scelsi acknowledged to be his sole, real image,96 as expressed in the following poem. Here we can intuit the full depth of the symbols revealing the existence of a passage, a path, a door between the world to come (Earth) and the world of the being (Sun). Only those who may be called “children of eternity” could access it:

Entre le soleil et la terre
    J’ai croisé ton regard
    Traverse l’archipel nocturne
    Vers l’immobile futur
    La porte est grande ouverte
    Aux enfants de l’eternité97

Surely we can acknowledge Dostoyevsky’s idiot as the hero capable of completing this journey. Scelsi was quite fond of this character, admitting on many occasions that he identified with Prince Myshkin.98 His is “a challenge to the world that knows material values: its contact with spiritual values is direct and pure, and in order to reach it others must follow a very difficult path.”99
The image of a child’s stupor when gazing upon the door to eternity that is opening before his very eyes is also a metaphor for the achievement of a state of annihilation of the ego, the dimension of transparency, the purity of the soul that reflects knowledge. It was “the mystery of childhood [that] is closer to unity than an adult’s wake: the ego and the world, in its interiority and exteriority, reach conjunction and permeate themselves reciprocally in an inextricable manner.”100 And the same image appears again in the successive collection of poems, La conscience aiguë, this time enriched by the representation of a self-perpetuating time aimed at conjoining immeasurable infinity and human dimension of memory. Furthermore, the latter is in synchrony with the Bergsonian representation, the developing dimension of the same conscience “that distinguishes in a more radical way the duration of specialized time. The past emerges through memory in every instant of our life. This explains why the duration, as lived time, is irreversible.”101

Le temps s’allonge
    entre deux mondes
    depuis toujours
de l’espace sans nombre
    soufflé un monster
    au centre de son ombre
lourde mémoire
    dont la stature immense
    n’étonne que l’herbe
    et les enfants102

The desire to free oneself from any subjectivity or egocentrism established at the emergence of a liberated conscience is confirmed by the fact that, in the three collections of poems, even the use of the pronoun I was constantly avoided.103 Rather, the essentiality of the word conceded more space to the depth of contemplative silence:

Le silence aura un jour
    Une âme neuve
    Et le savoir du sommeil
    Sera plus profond
    Que les raciness des arbres centenaires104

Jankélévitch also spoke of the silence as a necessary means for penetrating the “message” past the tumult of experience. “The research for silence,” he said, “is a research for a meta-empirical and hypersensitive elsewhere that is more essential than a clamorous and vulgar existence; therefore it prepares us to receive the truth if not acquiring its knowledge.”105
But at the end of the journey even the revelation remains mute one more time.
SYMBOLISM: THE LANGUAGE OF INTUITION
Three poetic works from Scelsi’s mature period point to a choice of expressive essentiality that emerged in the course of the present excursus; if Octologo is a collection of aphorisms and Il sogno 101 a verbal translation of the psychic experience, in Cercles paradigmatic essentiality is brought to extreme consequences, resolving into pure symbolic representation.106 These works encompass all three expressions, which, in my opinion, also constitute the key to accessing apparently hermetic meanings: the recuperation of the intuitive consciousness’s value.
Patanjali—a writer familiar to Scelsi, who knew the author well enough to recommend his work to others107—wrote that “knowledge of all things is obtained in the vivid light of intuition. In fact, if theoretical knowledge permits the formulation of hypotheses, possibilities, explanations, and the discriminating ability to distinguish opposites and dualities, intuitive knowledge is that in which intelligence emancipates itself, thus becoming a precursor of the discriminating knowledge like dawn, the rise of the sun. With the knowledge of intuition one can have knowledge of everything.”108
Intuition is also key in accessing symbolism, a language that is, more than any, adept at metaphysical representation.109 Symbols lead to contemplation, meditation, and the inner motions of the conscience. In order to understand them, there is no need for rational explanation, but active participation. Symbols are, first and foremost, the representation of a total experience that attracts the individual who focuses on his or her spiritual centrality.110
It will be the extent of an individual’s spiritual evolution, as he or she reads the symbols, that determines his or her capacity to penetrate the meaning. In fact, “to transmit the symbols’ significance to an individual who does not already possess their comprehension would be impossible. This may appear as a paradox, but the meaning of a symbol and the revelation of its essence can be revealed only to an individual in a position to understand them, because he already knows the inner meanings of the symbol. Thus the symbol becomes for him a synthesis of his knowledge and an instrument for expressing and transmitting it.”111
In the rising sun’s image we immediately identify Scelsi’s elective symbols—the circle and the line (see example 4.1). Beginning with a graphic analysis, this unified symbol probably derives from the Chinese ideogram dàn. It is utilized mostly in poetry and symbolizes “light that shines above water” or, more precisely, “the sun that rises above the earth at dawn,” the transition between night (lunar) and day (solar). But in the term light, one also identifies intuition’s probing power, and therefore Scelsi’s message could be interpreted as an invitation to a form of knowledge that searches beyond the image, even beyond language, rationality, and cognitive capacity, thus aiming toward the essence: depth.

This premise becomes essential to understanding Scelsi’s inner journey in reference to man and his music. Ultimately Scelsi himself clearly suggested such a concept in the Octologo’s second aphorism: “Do not think; let think those who have the need for thinking.”112 This is the reason why a rational explanation of Scelsi’s late poetic works is absolutely useless, if even possible. Rather, the need to allow room for thoughts and their content—so it can resound internally and let emerge otherwise inexpressible intuitions—is essential to penetrating the understanding of such works and is little similar to the sound that, even when ceasing to be perceived in a sensible way by the auditory system, continues to exist in the form of inner vibrations capable of fusing and shaping our most profound being.
Having arrived at the conclusion of our stated purpose, let us ponder the affinity connecting Scelsi’s two most hermetical works, Il sogno 101 and Cercles. Both works, in fact, develop through different languages a similar process of transformation and passage through a well-defined series of geometrical figures. In Cercles the representation is graphic, letting one intuit a transformation from triangle to pentagon to hexagon and to octagon until it reaches a circular shape. We could read it as a process of sequential expansion and harmonization of its various sides, representing the human being, until we reach the identity, the fusion of the opposites, the equilibrium that makes such ideas equidistant from the center of one’s being. Ultimately we see the symbolic representation as a process of spiritual perfectionism, alchemical trans-mutation that leads to the material condition of the spiritual state: the transformation of metal into gold means the transformation of human into pure spirit.113 The identification of the circle in reaching harmony and unity is an element found in Zen Buddhism in which a concentric series of circles points to the ultimate level of one’s spiritual journey, the achievement of inner harmony.114
This method seems to be confirmed by the transcendental experience Scelsi describes in Il sogno 101. The soul, having transcended its corporeal entity, finds itself in a new dimension where, from time to time, it assumes diverse shapes: first a triangle, then pentagon, then a hexagon, and ultimately an octagon. During these transformations the soul acquires the precise vision of all incarnations that have occurred in time, but it does not reach the circle because that level of evolution is yet to be completed:

Ti è stato concesso
    di vedere
    ma non puoi andare
    oltre
    né restare
    Ora torna sulla terra
    sono passati duecento anni
    da quell tempo
    …
    altre volte
    procederai.
    Sarai tetraedo
    e vedrai
    che come tu pensi
    che Dio sia
    così Egli è.
    …
    Ma Egli non è questo
    né quello.
    È il tutto.
    Poi
    Dodecaedro
    sarai in Lui.
    Poiché nulla è
    fuori di Lui.
    Infine cerchio,
    riconoscerai
    la tua divinità.
Ora torna
    sei atteso.115

It has been opined that these same geometrical figures served as schemes, traces, and bone structures for musical compositions.116 Even so, an opportunity for future study of Scelsi’s compositions as they relate to symbolism is evident.117
We can observe that even in this phase the poetic evolution follows an itinerary parallel to the musical experience. The genesis of the last text here examined was determined by “a method very similar to that of the musical improvisations,” where “Scelsi entered into some sort of trance and let his ‘voice’ run free.”118 It was a well-known fact that the works belonging to the composer’s mature period were born out of improvisations at the piano or ondioline. Therefore Scelsi was able to free himself of any possible rational constrain.
We must ponder the priority that the mature Scelsi gave his relationships with the interpreters of his works (say, the collaborations with Michiko Hirayama or the testimonial accounts of Stefano Scodanibbio, Irvine Arditti, Marianne Schroeder, and Frances-Marie Uitti),119 with the intention of guiding them to a consciousness of sound depth, expression of the physical, sensitivity of an eminent inner profundity. First and foremost Scelsi selected those individuals particularly prone to such experiences and, in a certain sense, put them to the test before initiating them into the search for sound depth. It has been said that he asked them to play just one note in the whole gamut of nuances they were able to imagine;120 then he evaluated not their technical prowess but their disposition toward expressive research. He then insisted on practicing improvisation, emphasizing the important fundamental concept that “one must search for himself the reason of being without imitating, copying, or learning something from others. In fact, one should keep distance from all this.”121
There is no room for exteriority, for prolixity, and, above all, for any form of dialectic display here. On the contrary, what prevails is the conscience of both composers and interpreters who are only transmitting instruments “of something given that is grander than them. They must respect such a gift without taking anything that could become a personal game. Place and conditions must be in agreement to a rite because such is music, even if not religious.”122
Thus Scelsi’s journey through his mature period was charted on the basis of the symbolism in his poetic works and his move toward the ritualization of the sound event in music. In fact when the profound involvement of composer and interpreter is reached it involves the listener as well. We could speak about a ritualized sound event and the sacredness of the event itself. Rituality is not determined by its context or superstructure but by the fusion of performer and listener in the same sound,123
obtained thanks to the extension of that intuitive faculty that is a key factor for accessing depth. “I have played the vina until my heart became the instrument itself; thereof I offered this instrument to the Divine musician, the only existing Musician. Since then I have become like his flute, and, upon His wishes, He can play His music.”124

In such a conception we find an echo of the theme about the research of correspondences, of harmony between music, man, nature, and the universe proper to the Western tradition of music of the spheres. If in the Apollonian vision of music the divinity indulges it in order to remind humans about the harmony present in the universe, Pythagoras realized a translation of the cosmos’s mathematical order in musical proportions. In The Republic Plato pairs music with astronomy because it is capable of expressing the universal order. Finally the topic, discussed again in Boetius’s classical three-part division (musica instrumentalis, humana, mundana), was developed in Renaissance thought that realized the image of the musician as the individual capable of connecting man to the harmony of the universe and, therefore, in the position to rediscover the “marvelous effects of music.” See Paolo Gozza, La musica nella rivoluzione scientifica del Seicento (Bologna: Il Mulino, 1989).
