5

Who Is Walt(h)er Klein?
Luciano Martinis
I have always been curious about the master/pupil dichotomy, particularly the strange role a master plays in the development of someone who, by ability or pure instinct, passes the test of time and enters the cultural patrimony of humanity, thus obscuring the identity of the master. Even in these instances something about these masters remains in the form of sporadic biographical information and scattered notes of merit—or demerit, as the case may be.
In the process of reexamining the sketchy biographical information surrounding the figure of Giacinto Scelsi, two names seem to appear with regular frequency: Giacinto Sallustio (1879–1938) and Walter Klein (1882–1961). These two men might, therefore, be considered Giacinto Scelsi’s “official teachers.”
As for Sallustio, limited personal details have emerged from biographical musical encyclopedias, including names of his teachers and titles of his principal works.1 As for Klein, though, our task is a bit more daunting, since in his case time has been inexorable, indeed!
Years ago, in a visual biography of Giacinto Scelsi,2 I exhibited the facsimile of a typed sheet of paper containing a detailed curriculum vitae for Walter Klein, probably compiled by Klein himself. It listed vital data along with his educational background, special interests, and a catalogue of his works. To me, a nonmusicologist, most of the information appeared superfluous, but I nonetheless decided to publish the document, imagining it to be interesting to someone. It was not, and, to make the matter worse, errors and false hypotheses about Klein have continued to circulate ever since. However, what impressed me particularly in that document written in English was its heading:

Professor Dr. Walther Klein
69, Pieristengasse, Vienna VIII, Germany.
    Composer, teacher of theory, author

The meaning was clear: when the document was written, Austria had already become a German province!

I traveled to Vienna to study the twelve-tone system with Walter Klein, who was a pupil of Schoenberg. Shortly after my arrival, though, I became ill. It was a normal consequence.
—Giacinto Scelsi3

In reviewing the facts many years later, it has became apparent that upon arriving in Vienna Scelsi’s “physiological” predicament assumed an importance greater than anticipated, but further information revealed that his health made a turn for the better and was almost completely restored by his further musical developments. That said, any encounter between Scelsi and Klein, even one limited to a mere twenty days, was sufficient for history to deem the Austrian composter Scelsi’s teacher. In fact, hereafter, his name began to appear in all of Scelsi’s published biographical notes.
But what happened to Walter Klein? Or, shall I say, Walther Klein? Could it be that all traces of his whereabouts had been eradicated? Probably! In the years between 1938 and 1945, millions of lives had been wiped out of existence with the greatest of indifference! But why, though, did Klein’s name remain in Scelsi’s sporadic biographies? Though I considered it puzzling, Klein’s name began to gradually emerge, taking shape like debris from a shipwreck, documenting a tragic story, one fragment at a time. Thus I was able to reconstruct, if not a story, at least the chronicles of a human relationship. My present task is to document the chronology of encounters and nonencounters; others, perhaps, will evaluate the consequences and implications from a musical viewpoint. For my part, I would be satisfied with having brought to light an aspect—perhaps not new, but certainly little known—of Giacinto Scelsi: the deep human side of his extraordinary personality!
1934
On July 26, Giacinto Scelsi sailed from Naples aboard the British vessel Otranto. His final destination was England, with ports of call in Toulon, Palma de Majorca, and Gibraltar. Scelsi preferred to remain onboard to avoid unnecessary fatigue until the arrival at the coast, where he traveled to London by rail in order to circumvent a four-hour custom wait. In London he joined Dorothy Kate Ramsden, the woman he was to wed.4
On Saturday, August 4, Scelsi wrote to his mother, Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, to inform her of the voyage’s outcome—a rather monotonous affair—and to express to her his hope that diplomatic difficulty at the government level was not going to hamper his sojourn in England. He also mentioned his planned society engagements and concluded the letter to his mother saying, “I hope that Klein’s letter does not announce his departure.”5
Scelsi appeared determined to meet with Walter Klein during his trip to England. In fact, on August 8 he moved to Northiam, Sussex, where some of Dorothy’s friends were. He initially found accommodation at the Six Bells Hotel, which turned out to be a less than ideal situation. But as the middle of August approached, it was no easy task to find anything more suitable. The same day he wrote his mother of his impressions and included the following information:

I have seen Klein for a moment and he seems likeable… the College where Klein teaches is closed, thus, he resides in a private home belonging to one of his students.6

It is evident, then, that Scelsi had not personally met with Klein prior to his trip to England. The next letter to his mother, written on Six Bells stationery, reports that

The weather is horrible, the hotel is depressing, and the food is below par. I must see if I can improve the whole situation. After all, Klein is the guest of a family, and he appears to be rather fearful. I don’t know why, but he seems to feel too obliged to that family, as if he has to account for his time. It must be like Sallustio’s situation in Geneva, and I have the impression that he regrets having invited me here. Today I saw him at midday, and we had our first lesson on rhythms. The method is more or less the usual, but certainly he has a great deal of experience… I am sorry that I cannot even have a piano here because he [Klein] does not teach scales, so for now I practice rhythms by clapping hands.7

Naturally Scelsi did not just clap his hands; he busied himself by securing more congenial accommodations. In fact, he wrote to his mother

I have been searching for another solution, which has finally arrived. Mrs. Heath, the proprietress of the Castle of Brickwall, home of the College, possesses other houses and, by way of forced diplomacy as well as through Klein’s influence, I was able to rent one (that is to say, some rooms in a house used as storage) very near to the famous Oak of Queen Elizabeth and Klein as well, just 300–400 meters. Klein comes to my place, because where he lives is some kind of Babylon.8

Once established at Oak Side, Scelsi let Dorothy join him, accompanied by her cook and a rented piano, which had to be hauled from Hastings. Scelsi’s only regret was that Klein had to depart in about ten days’ time.9 His next letter, written on August 26, was jotted down in pencil because the weather outdoors was too nice to abandon even long enough to fetch a pen from inside the house, so he informed his mother! The letter also contains some accounting:

What is really expensive, I am afraid, is Klein, because due to the rate of exchange it comes to fifty lire per lesson. I have already taken two per day, however, not at the moment. To learn how to notate all rhythms one would need at least six whole months. I learned about other things, though, like atonality.10

More interesting still was the letter Scelsi sent from Oak Side on August 31, consisting of a summation of his entire sojourn in that location. He described various excursions and spoke about some new acquaintances he’d made:

Last Wednesday, we had lunch with Mrs. Heath, an exceptional and very interesting lady. She was in Central Africa for eight years as a missionary’s wife, and then she married an alienist doctor and moved to India, Australia, for several years in Peru, and lived among the Redskins in Mexico, etc.
She has a daughter who studies dramatic art in New York and another who studies composition in Vienna with Klein. Mrs. Heath directs this school in Brickwall, but she can leave at the spur of the moment for the other end of the world. Klein departed yesterday, thus, during the last few days we did not have lessons because he was too busy. All said, he did not cost as much as I feared; He was reasonable and considered many lessons as conversations or just lowered the price, I am not sure. Certainly, time was too short, just 15 days, some of which were lost due to outings, or because he had to be in London on business. He would be glad to come to Italy, therefore, we could invite him like we did with Sallustio, in some summer place for his holidays. Here, he lodged with the Heaths, but I understand that he could not work profitably because the house was always full of people and confusion. Klein’s wife is a painter, and it seems that she will remain here at Brickwall to teach drawing and sculpting. Some evenings ago we went to the Heaths’ to play music. Klein played some of his pieces and I the famous “Rotativa” that always makes a great impression when I play it on a Steinway, which I had the chance to prepare beforehand. With Klein we discussed “Rotativa”’s orchestration, and he agrees with me that with three or four pianos, brass, and percussion it should sound much better than the current orchestration.
Naturally, there was no time even to make a sketch, because his time was very limited: he arrived always late, stayed more or less one hour, and often had to run off. He was always very busy. He wrote dozens of letters per day, worked with Mrs. Heath’s daughter, and then his wife had to be operated in London, etc.… He, too, like Sallustio, is much taken with Dorothy and dedicated a song to her that is incomprehensible, because it is in German and a little hard for the voice, but which seems very beautiful musically. Now, I’ll try to compose something during the last week here. Will see.11

Scelsi and Dorothy spent more time in the area around Northiam; in a letter dated September 5 he wrote to his mother something interesting:

I have composed in these days a little atonal melody, but I am not sure about its quality. Perhaps it could be played on violin, if not on the flute.12

Scelsi and Dorothy spent the second half of September in Sussex at the sea resorts of St. Leonards-on-Sea and Bexhill-on-Sea. Again, Scelsi wrote about the composition above in a letter to his mother sent on the 24th:

While in Northiam I have composed in the last fifteen days a short melody of the genre of “Chemin du coeur,” but atonal. Then, I began working on the rhapsody for piano when we needed to depart. I said all this because I wanted you to know that I felt well.13

Dorothy and Scelsi were back in London on September 30. Their visit to the British capital was filled with high-society events and entertainment before their return to Rome on October 31. In all, this was a good period.
1935
During this year Scelsi’s correspondence made no mention of Klein except the briefest mention of “a German,” but he may well have meant someone else. However, for thoroughness’s sake, here is the passage in question, as it appeared in a letter sent to his mother from Monte Carlo on October 23:

Regarding Sallustio I say: should he ask about who our guests were this summer, just say “a German,” appearing as vague as you can. Tell him that I had met him last year and change the subject.14

1936
In the following year, 1936, Klein is at first only casually referenced in a letter Scelsi sent his mother on August 19 to Aix-les-Bains from Monte Carlo: “with Klein things proceed ever so slowly.”15 Then, on August 24, Klein was mentioned twice: “Klein always the same, I am pushing to finish the piece.”16 And on the 30th of the same month, “Klein has left this morning. We have finished the piece!”17
1937
This year was particularly intense for Giacinto Scelsi. In the spring he organized a series of concerts of contemporary music in Rome. Then, that summer, a particularly revelatory postcard he mailed to his mother from Salzburg on July 2 provided the first indication of his Austrian sojourn. We learn that he traveled with another person whose identity cannot be ascertained at this time. The postcard in question lacks the whole lower left corner but reads as follows:

Dear Muzzi,
The trip has gone very well and very comfortably. Now we spend the night here and tomorrow morning perhaps will proceed by train because there are 120 kilometers and not 70 to Altaussee, and I believe that it would be more tiring to travel by automobile. Temperature and weather are good. Tomorrow we will send you… legram. Do not… and go out. Greetings from… and many hugs… Gtimo, together at…18

From the tone of the text, one could opine that Scelsi’s traveling companion was none other than Germano, the family chauffeur.
Unfortunately only the envelopes of two of the following three letters Scelsi mailed his mother have survived. The first two, written on the Hotel Am See’s stationery, were posted at Altaussee on July 5 and 8, while the third, from the Hotel Imperial in Vienna, was mailed on July 13.19 Fortunately, a fourth letter, mailed on July 16, the day before his departure from Vienna, remains intact. It reads as follows:

Friday—
Dear Muzzi,
I have received three letters from you but not the one that included a message from Isabella. Tomorrow evening I depart for Venice where (I hope) Papa was able to obtain the room that was available the day before yesterday. I did not have to wait for Professor Friedlander’s reply to a letter by Klein. It has been a game I needed to play, and I hope that in the end I’ll be able to satisfy everybody by taking the cure, but not up there. Dorothy has written letters and telegrams, she will be here on the 26th and wishes to know were we would go, however, thus far I cannot assure her about anything. From Venice, perhaps, I will visit Abbazia by ship but not from here because no sleeping car is available. Enough! Now I am thinking of resting for a few days in Venice. I have spent the last 20 days of July rather poorly. At any rate, stay calm knowing that I manage myself at best. When are you thinking of leaving? Do not get tired and do not worry.
Hugs, your Gtino.20

As one can see from Scelsi’s projects, the study of the twelve-tone system was not the sole scope of his trip to Vienna; first and foremost he wished to contact Dr. Friedlander with whom he had the intention of beginning an intensive therapy for a knee ailment.
The hastily jotted-down translation, in Scelsi’s handwriting, of a letter addressed to Walter Klein could unveil the mystery of the “game he intended to play.” It was about a tentative plan to make Dr. Friedlander’s schedule coincide with Professor Klein’s, in order to convene at an as-yet-undetermined summer spa. Such an arrangement would have enabled Scelsi to take advantage of the necessary therapy and continue the study of the twelve-tone system already started in 1934. A passage from this letter is particularly revealing:

Because you know Sig. Scelsi better than I do, it is superfluous to mention that the treatment is only “bodily” and physical, but it would be beneficial to the inner psycho spiritual domain as well.21

This letter reveals how Dr. Friedlander had intuited his future patient’s most profound particularities.
In the fall of the same year, Dr. Friedlander and Scelsi met at on the Italian Riviera where Scelsi began a strict regimen of massages and physical exercises. His only diversion was a series of boring chess games he played against the doctor. As for Klein, no mention was made of him during this period.
1938
Over the course of the succeeding months in late 1937 and early 1938, the precarious international political situation intruded unexpectedly on Austria in a rather dramatic fashion. In fact, the Anschluss occurred on March 12, 1938. That same month Klein’s name reappeared in Scelsi’s correspondence:

I have received a desperate letter from Klein. If we still had [the castle at] Valva, I could have offered him hospitality for the rest of his life, but now, what can we do? Despite the low cost of supporting him, still 1,000 liras a month would be needed which I don’t have now at my disposal.22

This brief note, sent from Ventimiglia on March 22, reveals that Klein had not underestimated the force of the events afflicting his country and that he had, in a certain sense, foreseen the tragic consequences brewing on the horizon. Scelsi regretted his inability to help Klein with offers of hospitality at the castle at Valva, the old family manor house now belonging to his uncle Peppino. Furthermore, Scelsi had no funds available to support Klein. Meanwhile—to complicate matters—Italy had instituted and begun enforcing its Racial Laws against the Jews. At any rate, Scelsi had not abandoned his friend, as we can see from the following letter Klein wrote Scelsi’s sister, Isabella, Countess of Zogheb:

Vienna, March 31, 1938
Madame la Comtesse
Mr. Giacinto writes to me that you were kind enough to speak in my behalf to Mr. Brunshwig. It is superfluous to tell you about my emotion regarding a friendship such as yours and his. Furthermore, what I wish to express cannot be committed to written words.
What I wish is a position, whatever available, that takes advantage of my qualities and experiences as I have outlined in the curriculum (poorly rendered in English), for example as a teacher in a music school or as an editor with a music publishing house.
I have a great deal of experience with the German version of libretti. Mine is, for instance, that of Honegger’s “Amphion” represented in Zurich, and composer Florent Schmith was very happy with my work. Furthermore, I translated Franco Alfano’s “Madonna Imperia” represented in Vienna.
Please, Madame la Comtesse, accept my respects and the most profound expression of my gratitude!
Walther Klein23

Donna Isabella wasted no time sending Walter Klein’s curriculum vitae to Emile Brunschwig, accompanied by a letter whose autographed copy is now in our possession. It reads as follows:

Dear Sir,
It is with pleasure that I send to you the curriculum of Professor Klein about whom we conversed on the telephone the other day.
As you have had the opportunity to see, he is a very interesting and capable person and deserves your attention and help in placing him properly.
Professor Klein is very experienced in the German translation of opera libretti. He was the one who translated “Amphion” by Honegger for the Zurich representation to great acclaim. At the same time, he translated Alfano’s “Madonna Imperia” for the Vienna representation. From this point of view, I think that Professor Klein would be a valuable collaborator at a music publishing firm or as a professor at a conservatory school of music. Hoping that you will give sincere consideration to this affair, please accept the best expressions of my sentiments.24

Here is Emile Brunschwig’s answer to the Countess of Zogheb:

Emile Brunschwig
Les Hauts-Crets
    Cologny-Geneve
Genéve, April 9, 1938
Dear Madame,
I received in due time your letter of April the 3rd which accompanied Professor Klein’s curriculum.
I wish to inform you that to date I have sent six letters to personalities in the music world who could be of help in securing an eventual position for Professor Klein.
Please accept, Dear Madame, my distinguished greetings.
E. Brunschwig25

How Klein managed to escape Austria and what route he took to the United States we do not know at this time, but we can make a few assumptions based Scelsi’s correspondence: Probably distrusting the postal system of the time, Walter Klein gave Lucette Cochard, his Swiss pupil, a copy of a letter dated March 31, 1938, with the task of mailing it from Switzerland. In fact, it was sent from Chernex, Vaud, to

Madame la Comtesse de Zogheb,
    Gèneve, Hôtel Chernex, le 10 IV 1938
Madame La Comtesse,
Dr. Klein has asked me to deliver to you upon my return to Switzerland the enclosed letter and curriculum vitae. I do this with a great deal of pleasure because Dr. Klein has been my professor during the past two years at the school Hellerau-Laxemburg. His very interesting courses were much appreciated, and all the students considered themselves greatly privileged to work and learn under his direction. I would say that he counts on your benevolence and, on my part, I would hope from the bottom of my heart that you will help him find a solution to his situation.
Madame la Comptesse, please accept my most distinguished greetings,
    Lucette Cochard26

By October Scelsi had taken up residence at the Hotel Ambassador in Paris, where he wrote his mother the following message:

I am very, very sorry about Sallustio’s death. I sent a telegram to his wife. Did he have a stroke? Regarding Klein, if he is not dead, we can also no longer count on him. I may lose the three of them at the same time.27

This brief passage reveals two important facts: First, Scelsi knew nothing about Klein’s whereabouts; in fact, he thought that something tragic had happened to him. Therefore, one may dismiss the notion that Klein traveled through Italy on his way to America. Second, Scelsi wrote about the loss of three individuals whose professional services he was counting on. Clearly, the allusion here is to a third musician. Who was he? To what type of collaboration did Scelsi refer? What happened to this person? I shall discuss some possibilities in a bit.
Walter Klein’s name appeared once again in the following letter Scelsi sent his mother from Taranto on December 2:

I received Klein’s letter you forwarded to me this morning. He, too, wanted me to pay for the trip to America for him and his wife. He said that he lost everything, house, furniture, clothes, manuscripts, everything, and that his only possession was the suit he wore. I feel his pain, but how can one disburse five or six thousand lira? I must tell him that I cannot, and I am truly sorry.28

From the tenor of this letter, one could deduce that Scelsi had helped someone else escape to America, a reason why he was so familiar with the costs and practices of such an operation. This might be a good avenue to pursue the identity of the mysterious third person mentioned before. But where was Klein at that particular moment? Was he, perhaps, in Switzerland, since he was in a position to send and receive mail out of Austria?
1939
A brief letter in English signed by Walter Klein was mailed from the United States in 1939 to Isabella Scelsi, Countess of Zogheb. It reads:

Cambridge, February 13, 1939
Dear Countess,
I thank you ever so much for your kind letter of January 29th and for all the trouble you have taken.
I am very obliged for the advice given by the Geneva committee, and will follow it as soon as possible.
With kind regards
Yours sincerely
    Walter Klein
    18 Gibson Street, Cambridge, Mass
    U.S.A.

NOTES
1. Italian composer Giacinto Sallustio acquired notoriety in the early 1900s for his transcriptions of the works of old masters, such as Antonio Lotti. Friend to Ottorino Respighi and Riccardo Zandonai, Sallustio was a composer attracted to the French School—Debussy especially—thus making himself the antithesis to the stylistic trends in vogue in Italy during the 1930s. Among his compositions one should remember “La solitudine” for voice and piano with text by Franz Toussaint and a dedication to “Marchesa Dorothy Imperiali,” who later married Giacinto Scelsi (published in 1930 by Ricordi); “Spleen” for voice and piano with text by Paul Verlaine and a dedication to “Contessa Isabella di Zogheb,” Scelsi’s sister (published in 1933 by Ricordi); and the symphonic triptych Trasfigurazioni, performed at the Teatro Adriano in Rome in 1937.
2. Luciano Martinis, Traces laissés dans les fissures (Rome: Le parole gelate, 1995).
3. From Frank Mallet, Marie-Cécile Mazzoni, and Marc Texier, Conversazioni con Giacinto Scelsi, audiotape, Radio-France/France-Musique (February 1987).
4. Dorothy Kate Ramsden Imperiali and Giacinto Scelsi were married in Geneva on June 4, 1940. The English-born Miss Ramsden had been married to Demetrio Imperiali, the marquis of Francavilla, since 1926. She eventually divorced Scelsi in the late 1940s and went on to remarry, this time to Major Patrick Reginald Boyle, thirteenth earl of the County of Cork, on January 28, 1952. As a result of this marriage, Dorothy Kate Ramsden was styled the countess of Cork on April 19, 1967. She died in 1978.
5. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, August 4, 1934. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. 7.934, A-B 16 b.
6. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, August 8, 1934. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. 7.934, A-B 17 a/b.
7. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, 1934. Archive Le parole ge-late, doc. 7.934, A-B 18 a/b/d.
8. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, 1934. Archive Le parole ge-late, doc. 7.934, A-B 20 a/b.
9. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, 1934. Archive Le parole ge-late, doc. 7.934, A-B 20 b.
10. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, August 26, 1934. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. 7.934, A-B 21 a.
11. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, August 31, 1934. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. 7.934, A-B c/d/e. From this letter we can deduce a detail that may be of importance: Scelsi prepared the piano before playing “Rotativa.” Such practice could go back to 1932; in fact, it would become standard performance practice for Scelsi.
12. Giacinto Scelsi to Donna Giovanna Scelsi d’Ayala Valva, September 5, 1934. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. 7.934, A-B 23 b. The remark in this letter referring the “atonal” melody Scelsi was working on is extremely important. This occurrence should be investigated in depth because it could demonstrate, if supported by further documentation, that Scelsi was the first Italian composer to come close to atonality and dodecaphony.

6

Expressive Atonality
Franco Sciannameo
These four surviving letters Scelsi wrote to Klein in 1941, published here in English for the first time, integrate Luciano Martinis’s previous narrative, thus filling an important chronological gap in the Scelsi/Klein relationship. The letters, written in French, are now preserved at the Jean Gray Hargrove Music Library of the University of California–Berkeley as part of the Walther Klein Papers bequeathed to the library by Gerhard Klein, the composer’s son, in 1987.
1940
From the following four letters it appears that an epistolary exchange took place in 1940 between Scelsi and Klein (in the United States since 1939). However, it seems that some letters written by Scelsi never reached Klein, or vice versa.
1941
The first letter, written in May, was delivered to Klein in California on July 5.

Hôtel de la Paix—Lausanne—Switzerland
Dear Dr. Klein
It is evident that you never received my letter in which I asked you whether your parents were still living at the same address because my sister wanted to send them some provisions. Also, [in that letter] I announced to you the news of my [forthcoming] marriage to Dorothy Imperiali [Geneva, Switzerland June 4, 1940].
My health continues to be very poor, and I plan to remain in Switzerland as long as I can.
Although you may not be able to work on my piece of music, I ask that you please return to me the theme [melody] and the several sketches that I sent you last year [1940]. It is possible that some day I may be able to turn them into something. I am very sorry, because I was counting on your help, which in the present situation would have greatly improved my morale. I hope that this finds you in good health and that your work progresses expeditiously in this insane and desperate world.
Your devoted
    G. Scelsi

The second letter was registered and mailed on October 21 to a New York address, where it arrived on November 2 and was forwarded to California five days later.

My dear Friend
You cannot imagine my joy upon unexpectedly receiving a few days ago the music that I thought had been lost [emphasis original]. You said that it was sent in the same post as an earlier letter that I thought had been lost at the bottom of the sea. The day before yesterday, I thought that you would receive my earlier letter, and you had the same idea. Then I sent you a telegram with the hope that you would receive it before the letter. The first thing I did was to give the last part [of the piece] to the copyist. While in a highly emotional state, I examined the first page for a few minutes several times during the day. Now I have the copy of the other [portion of the piece], as well. I am going to send them back to you tomorrow so that we do not waste any time, since I will keep the new copy here. I will examine it in depth, and I will do all I can to have it played. When you send me the rest, I beg you to try to keep a copy, because the thought that it could get lost is too agonizing for me. I must tell you right away that my first impression is one of admiration for your talent and your skills. You integrated the emotion and wit that were in my mind and in my sketch perfectly—the second part especially is [written] not only fully in my own style, but it reflects my thought as well. The first [part] is not finished, and it seems to me that it is only the beginning, because although it corresponds harmonically to my third measure, it does not have yet the crescendo that is already in this measure. My writing is very dense and thick, very concentrated. You render it light, fluid, and brighter. But to obtain the crescendo that in my version takes seven measures, one would need nearly seven pages. The majesty of the form must take the place of the concentration and, in this case, I agree it is much better. Then there is the descent of the five last chords. I am wildly interested in comparing your finished version with my sketch. I will write you soon about my other impressions. For the moment I am sending back the music with all my thanks from the depths of my heart. You have done me so much good. I will never forget it.
Your G. Scelsi

The third letter, written on November 21, reads as follows:

Dear Dr. Klein,
I hope you have received my last letter in which I updated you about my sister’s success in placing your parents on the list of people to whom a Spanish subsidiary of the Red Cross regularly sends food and supplies. In addition, she has inquired with a woman who heads a similar organization in Hungary, thus I hope that something good will come from this angle, as well. I hope that the fruits and medications that she sent directly will reach them, but this depends on the circumstances. From Italy, I cannot do anything. I looked into the possibilities when I was in Rome.
Now, I would like to propose something to you. I am so depressed for many reasons, largely because of my eyes. I cannot read at all, and you know what that means, and it is impossible for me to read any music.
I have seen no progress during the past seven months. On the other hand, I have nobody here who can help me. I am sending you these arpeggios, they are [representative] of what I call “expressive atonality.” With the other notes that remain available they always form all twelve tones. If you play them fast they form arabesques and a toccata movement. Played slowly, they become very expressive and take on a lovely, dark quality. Played as chords, they produce interesting progressions and, in addition, there remain single isolated notes with which I can construct a kind of melody. Do you think that you would be able to make them into a beautiful “Toccata?” I am sure that you can. You say that your style has come closer to mine. As for me, I think that the true modern formula is this “expressive atonality” that I seek, with form added to it. You can do it. Today I need to have a piece of this type, because I feel it [emphasis original], which is at the same time in a form that is playable. I have a few pieces that I love as they are, a bit like those of Schönberg in a fragmentary state, without much form. They are very interesting, but no one plays them. I am thinking of Frescobaldi’s Toccate, which are great architectures of sound, and not of those of the nineteenth century, which are rather mechanical preludes. I do not care for purely “pianistic” pieces. Above all, there must be music. I had found a series of chords that I find very beautiful, and which also form groups of twelve notes, without repetitions and the arpeggios that I mention above, and this is also how I had started my piece, before the operation [serial inversions], now it’s over… If this form of composition for piano does not tempt you, and you prefer the sonata form, I have no objections, but I believe that in this style it’s even more difficult. Perhaps in this case [it would be] less difficult [to write a piece] for violin and piano than for solo piano. I must have a beautiful piece in this style (expressive atonality) that I really feel, without superfluous notes for harmony or in passing [emphasis original], but that is sufficiently imposing in its form to be played in concert. I need this for my morale, which is very bad. You must understand this… Now I no longer have the possibility of even “escaping” through travel. Everything around me is horrible and depressing. I don’t know what I can lean on to resist it. Only music can save me, but my eyes do not allow me to work. If you accept [this proposal] my sister could get the sum of 20 English pounds to you (if you judge that to be sufficient), which should make around 100 dollars. I really envy you being in California and that you can work in peace. I await your answer with impatience and hope, in the meantime I send you my best wishes and my friendship.
G.S.
I named a figure [monetary] without thinking. Naturally it is up to your discretion to decide what it should be.




The fourth letter was mailed from Lausanne, Switzerland, on December 3.

Dear Dr. Klein,
Our most recent letters crossed. I answer yours while awaiting your reply to mine. I am very saddened by what you say about your parents, I assure you that I would be glad to do everything in my power for them, unfortunately though, I cannot do everything that you ask. If they are not Jews, they will not need an invitation to come to Italy, but I do not know if at present anyone will give them a visa, because permission is only given for a short, transitory time.
You have no idea about the most recent arrangements here. It is impossible for me at present to send a notarized invitation to foreigners to come and live in Italy. Aside from regulations that make it impossible to execute, I would also not be able to do so for political reasons (everything related to foreign countries is complex and suspicious) as well as fiscal ones. The situation in which I find my family and myself is already very difficult, and such an invitation could have consequences that I must avoid. Nor can I furnish them with tickets to go to America, since all that is carefully monitored, and many things are forbidden. Thus I cannot buy them tickets, even if their cost would amount to less than seven hundred dollars which in Italy makes 14,000 liras, truly a great sum which I do not have available. This sum might not appear large in America, but here it is truly beyond my means. In any case, the figure is at least three times the normal price for the journey for two people. Now that I have told you what I cannot do, I will tell you what I can do: write a letter privately to your father telling him that I would be happy to see him in Italy, when he is passing through [emphasis original] and that he can count on me, if he needs anything during his stay in Italy. In addition, I can make available to him, either for his stay or for the tickets, a maximum [emphasis original] sum of 5,000 liras. This is really the maximum that I can do and is even a sacrifice for me now, because things have really changed. Naturally, included in this sum is the money that I offered you in my last letter. Believe me, dear Doctor Klein, that if I could have, I would have already done everything that you asked, but it is not my fault if everything is forbidden and heavy with consequence. If your parents can get out, I advise them to do it as soon as possible and to get on the boat immediately, because it is possible that even the Italian boats may suspend their service later on. The boat trip is not tiring, and it is better than spending a winter in the cold, especially since they will find the summertime in California. If your brother can send the rest, I can contribute the sum of five thousand liras to your father for his transit and for the purchase of tickets, and this is not an offer that comes easily for me. But if they are forced to pay in dollars, I cannot do anything. I also must tell you that they should not think about staying in Italy for a long time; foreigners now receive visas that are for a very short stay. I think I answered all your questions. You know me well, my dear friend, and you can understand how much all this saddens and disgusts me. I do not need to say anything more on the subject. I impatiently await your answer to my last letter, which I hope will be favorable.
Your very devoted G.S.

Thus Klein satisfied Scelsi’s request by composing a piece for violin and piano based on material that Scelsi had provided him (see example 6.2).














7

In the New World
Luciano Martinis
We return now to my chronicles of the Scelsi/Klein relationship, which conclude with a small cache of letters Klein wrote to Scelsi in English in the year 1948.
1948
From Scelsi’s little address book:

Klein Walter (professeur de musique)
    62, Piaristengasse, Wien VIII
c/o Mrs. and Mr. Otten
    160 Claremont Avenue
    New York City, U.S.A.

Both addresses were then crossed out and substituted with the following update:

Klein Walter c/o Catherine Branson School
    (Ross, California, U.S.A.)
    114—10th Avenue—San Francisco
    California, U.S.A.

One would assume that the last corrections were probably made in the years after the war. However, in the following letters Klein addressed to Scelsi, his address was already different:

January 91
My dear Mr. Scelsi,
As I have been greatly worried about your health and general state of affairs, your letter is a real relief for me. You will understand my long silence: I had to move twice in one year, and moving means looking for a place in a time of a desperate housing shortage. I further learned that practically all my European relatives, at least nine persons, and many friends perished under the Nazis. I also had an intestinal hemorrhage and never completely recovered from it. There were other tribulations, too. There were absorbing professional obligations all the time and there were those numberless packages for Europe, the packing and mailing of each taking many hours and lots of money and energy.
Under these circumstances I was not and am not able to resume the dropped thread of those musical ideas I had developed when I was working on your sketches. I was afraid to tell you such bad news and so I postponed writing you from day to day.
Even now I must resign myself to some kind of telegram style. So many of the things I badly want to say will remain unsaid. My desk is flooded with unanswered letters and no copied manuscripts of compositions of mine. I just have not the time to attend to all that properly, and that although I have dropped a third of my activity (and income, of course).
Now regarding your wishes:

Americans hardly ever consent to do or write or promise anything they do not mean seriously and literally. Nevertheless I will try to get the requested invitation letter from the Music Department of the Presbyterian Divinity College to whose Faculty I belong. Your religion will probably not be considered an obstacle, since I myself am not a Presbyterian but a Catholic. But even apart from that the result is doubtful. As soon as I have their decision I will report to you again.
If you have some good published chamber music (anything except string quartets), you may send it to the Music Lovers’ Society c/o Miss Margaret Tilly, 2208 Steiner Street, San Francisco, Calif. U.S.A. They are first class artists, very progressive, and do only first class things. I cannot make them perform something they do not like, but I will call their attention to your work and ask them to give it careful consideration. However, even so, your chances are not great. This country is one where all the spiritual and musical giants of the planet seem to have a rendezvous, and it is difficult to compete with them. So, to avoid possible and even probable disappointment, you must be prepared to have patience and ready to accept a refusal without depression. Miss Tilly is a very good friend of ours. She has seen to it that some of my operas were done—and I never had a better performance in my life.

You say nothing about your parents, your wife, your mother, and the result of the cure in Switzerland, Mrs. Scelsi’s planned or rather finished novel! Please, inform me about everything!
Thank you for the address of your sister. I will apply to her when there will be any occasion. But right now there is not.
Please, give my regards to your mother and Mrs. Scelsi junior.
With my best wishes, from my wife too,
    Yours faithfully and gratefully,
    Walter Klein
[On second thoughts I find it would be better for you not to send any material to Miss Tilly ’til I have talked to her.] (Write to me in French. I still read it currently despite my awful orthography.)2
January 143
My dear Mr. Scelsi,
In my last letter I told you that I would try to procure such a letter of invitation as you wanted to have. In the meantime I had the opportunity of talking to Professor Krueger about the matter and he consented to have the invitation issued. The letter will be mailed directly from the Music Department. Kindly let me know when it arrives!
Miss Tilly prepares a concert and I have to wait ’til that is over.
With my best wishes and regards to you and your family
    Yours always,
    Walter Klein
January 154
Dear Mr. Scelsi:
Dr. Walter Klein, Faculty member of our School of Sacred Music, whose head I am, has told me of your interesting special studies in the field of music and that you intend to come to this country for a visit.
We would like to use this opportunity and hear your views on contemporary Italian music, especially, also on its relation to Sacred Music. Would you be inclined to give us a series of lectures on this subject? If so, kindly get in touch with me as soon as you arrive in the United States!
Yours very sincerely,
George F. Krueger
GFK:mo
Professor George F. Krueger
    Director of the School of Sacred Music
    130 Bolinas Avenue
    San Anselmo, California U.S.A.
February 185
66 Winship Ave.
    San Anselmo, California
Dear Mr. Scelsi,
I hope you got my letter in order, but I am somewhat concerned about your long silence. The invitation Professor Krueger sent you upon my suggestions also remained unconfirmed. Please, let me know whether it arrived and what your plans are! In the meantime, I had an opportunity of speaking to Miss Tilly. She said she and her co-artists will be glad to have some of your chamber music. They will read it and see whether it fits into their program, and you may also send things that are not published nor printed.
I have misplaced your letter. Could I have Countess Zogheb’s address again? Just in case. My wife and I are suffering from a bad case of protracted bronchitis and we have to work very hard, things are not quite easy. But all this is of minor importance, in view of the general tragedy all over the world.
I often think with profound gratitude of yours and your mother’s charming hospitality in 1936. Tempi felici passati per jammai! [Bygone happy times!].
Please give my regards to your family! Much love and good wishes to you!
Yours very friendly,
Walter Klein

Thus far, this is all we have of the Scelsi-Klein correspondence, which requires some explanatory notes. Scelsi’s sister, Donna Isabella Scelsi de Zogheb, traveled to the United States at the invitation of certain military officials she had known in Rome during the liberation period shortly after the war ended. Concerned about the difficult social situation in Italy at that time, Isabella urged her brother to visit the United States. Scelsi began making contacts with various personalities in the artistic/ musical world in America, reconnected with relations forcibly interrupted by the war events, and obtained an invitation to lecture on sacred music, therefore initiating the procedures necessary to obtaining the proper entrance visa. For unknown reasons, the visa was denied. From the tone of the correspondence with his sister, the reasons for the denials seem to have been as follows: officially, Scelsi was unemployed and penniless, and thus he needed an American citizen willing to vouch for him—someone in good graces with the current administration, which was not easy to do at that time. The beginning of the Cold War had considerably tightened controls limiting the influx of foreigners into the country.
The truth of it is that the United States denied a mere tourist visa to Giacinto Scelsi, last descendant in the line of the Marquis d’Ayala Valva, a dynasty that was already five hundred years old at the time of America’s discovery!
This all happened during one of the most tenuous periods of Giacinto Scelsi’s burdened existence. And it certainly contributed to the worsening of his already precarious health condition.
CONCLUSION
In 1972, when Giacinto Scelsi dictated his memoirs into a tape recorder, the particulars of his relationship with Walter Klein were quite muddled. There were fundamental errors, which have since been reconstructed, but at least he provided broad strokes of the chronological facts. That said, the following excerpt is extremely important in understanding Scelsi’s relationship with Klein:

Later on, I decided to get acquainted with and deepen the study of the twelve-tone system, about which I knew very little although I heard about it and had listened to some compositions by Schoenberg in some concert. Thus I went to Vienna where I met with Walter Klein—a pupil of Schoenberg—who taught there and in the summer at Laxemburg, a sort of castle-college that could accommodate all of his pupils, a very beautiful place.
I remained in Vienna for a very short time, for various reasons, but I arranged with Klein to see him at Laxemburg the following summer.
Klein was a Jew, a man revealing a fragile look, completely intellectual and typically “Mittel-European”—a characteristic too well-known to indulge here in further explanations. One thing was sure, though—he had complexes, like Kafka, Mahler, and others, because of their race, religion, or the particular climate against them in that country at that moment or long before then.
It happened though that Klein intuited that a persecution against the Jews would have taken place in Austria at the hands of the Nazi Germans, so, before the arrival of summer he wrote to me begging to help him get out of Austria. Already at that time it was not easy, so I did my best to call for him from Italy from where he moved to England where he had friends who helped him find a teaching post in a music college not far from London. After a while, I was able to join him and spent various months there where he taught—a very beautiful place like so many in England, places that possess a certain Romanticism from the point of view of landscape and atmosphere.
It was there where I studied the twelve-tone system, despite the fact that it did not interest me particularly. But I did learn the system in all its developments, not those adopted later by the post-Webern school, which at that time were not even imagined, but Schoenberg’s classical dodecaphonic system.
On the other hand, Klein’s own music, despite his adoption of the dodecaphonic system, was rather in the style of Mahler. I remember a few things of his: some piano pieces, nice and sensitive, a string trio, and a cantata for voice and orchestra. From him I received some letters from America, then nothing since!
This is all for what the teaching of music is concerned and for what I have learned from others. If I know something more, I have learned it from myself.6

It is my hope that the information in this chapter will inspire further research into Walther Klein, Austrian composer and/or Walter Klein, Californian composer.
NOTES
1. Walther Klein to Giacinto Scelsi, January 9, 1948. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. WK. 11.09. The following typed letters were written in English. The annotation on the first letter was handwritten, and the last line was written in French.
2. The last four lines are handwritten, and the bracketed sentence is in French.
3. Walther Klein to Giacinto Scelsi, January 14, 1948. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. WK. 11.09. This letter is typed in English.
4. George F. Krueger to Giacinto Scelsi, Jaunary 15, 1948. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. WK. 11.11. This letter is typed in English.
5. Walter Klein to Giacinto Scelsi, January 1948. Archive Le parole gelate, doc. WK. 11.12. This letter is a manuscript in English.
6. Giacinto Scelsi, Il Sogno 101, unpublished manuscript, 1972, 19–20. This text is faithfully reproduced from the original typescript.








8

A Forgotten Personage: Richard Falk
Luciano Martinis
Ahint dropped in a letter Giacinto Scelsi sent to his mother may be the key to identifying the individual who joined Giacinto Sallustio and Walther Klein as Scelsi’s early musical collaborators.
In tracking such a feeble lead we may be able to reconstruct certain aspects of Scelsi’s life during the immediate postwar years, perhaps the most critical time of his existence. Despite the successes realized by his then-most-recent works—Quartetto per archi and La nascita del verbo—this period was marked by a profound psychophysical crisis that brought Scelsi to the brink of ceasing to compose altogether, a situation that persisted for a number of years.
Perhaps the idea of reconnecting relationships with two former collaborators now transplanted in the United States represented a way out of this crisis for Scelsi; sadly though, it was not to be. Taking advantage of his sister Isabella’s extended sojourn in the New World and of her unconditioned support, Scelsi decided to join her and applied for the necessary visa documents. As mentioned previously, he was denied entry to the United States.
And so Scelsi began thinking about an alternate destination: the Orient…

I am very, very sorry about Sallustio’s death. I sent a telegram to his wife. Did he have a stroke? Regarding Klein if he is not dead also we can no longer count on him, so I may lose the three of them at the same time.1

In this brief excerpt of a letter Scelsi sent from Paris in October of 1938, we learn three very significant pieces of information regarding his musical collaborators: (1) that his first teacher and friend, Giacinto Sallustio, had died, (2) that Scelsi feared the worst about Walther Klein, and (3) that, with the ambiguous phrase “I may loose the three of them at the same time,” a third collaborator existed. Clearly Scelsi referenced a third musician. Who, though? Who was this third personage? To what type of collaboration did Scelsi allude? And what happened to that person?
It’s plausible that the mysterious third was Egon Köler (1886–1938), who had just passed away in Switzerland and has subsequently been considered by some to be Scelsi’s third teacher. However, there is no evidence that Köler, a theorist/aesthetician and an expert on exoticism, ever collaborated with or advised Scelsi on musical compositions. And so I would opt to exclude this hypothesis.
Fortunately some two months after Scelsi wrote that first letter to his mother we learn from another letter he sent her from Taranto, dated December 2, 1938, that Walther Klein has resurfaced:

I received Klein’s letter you forwarded to me this morning. He too wanted me to pay for the trip to America for him and his wife. He said that he lost everything, house, furniture, clothes, manuscripts, everything and that his only possession was the suit he wore. I feel his pains but how can one disburse five or six thousand liras… ?2

The letter’s wording makes it clear that Scelsi had already helped someone else in a similar situation, as he was already fully knowledgeable about the necessary costs. Therefore, for reasons I will elaborate on shortly, I can say that the mysterious “third man” was Richard Falk (1878–1949).
Richard Falk had been working in Italy since the early 1930s, mostly as a musicologist involved in the revival of the music of Giovan Battista Pergolesi (1710–1736). In 1936 the town of Jesi, Pergolesi’s place of birth, had organized an elaborate commemoration to mark the occasion of the second centennial of the composer’s birth. Duke Filippo Caffarelli (1891–1975), president of the Società Romana per la Musica da Camera and the curator of a disputed catalog of Pergolesi’s works, had been put in charge of organizing the festivities.
The Comitato per il Bicentenario Pergolesiano entrusted Richard Falk with conducting the “world premiere” of Pergolesi’s intermezzo, “Il maestro di musica,” on April 14, 1936, at Jesi’s Teatro Comunale. Falk had transcribed the piece for the occasion, which was at that time considered to be Pergolesi’s authentic composition; however only recently it has been attributed to the Neapolitan composer Pietro Auletta (1698–1771).
The following year, on March 17, 1937, Falk conducted the opera Olimpiade, which he transcribed from the manuscript preserved at the Biblioteca di Bergamo; he also reconstructed the choral parts—omitted in the original—from the libretto by Pietro Metastasio. The undertaking must have been memorable, since American critic Raymond Hall reported in the New York Times that “this new edition has brought to light for the present generation an illustrious milestone in Italian music.”3
Notwithstanding the success of these events, Falk, a German Jew, was compelled to leave Italy in 1938 as soon as the Racial Laws were implemented. We learn this from a letter Falk wrote Scelsi on November 29, 1940, from New York, in which he expressed his frustration with the lack of recognition on the part of the Italian authorities for the work he had done on behalf of Italian music and culture. He wrote:

This morning I must visit the [Italian] Consul again. Being from the Marche Region, he has an interest in the representation of Pergolesi’s opera Il Maestro di Cappella. [Should this project not come to fruition] it would be a shame since I alone, in the name of Italian art, have celebrated the memory of this composer here in the States. Please, when you see Caffarelli in Rome tell him about the situation: “They pushed me out of the Country to promote Italian culture in a major way and should the Consul fail, I will do it by myself in a very public way.”4

And so thus far the Scelsi-Falk relationship can be reconstructed by examining certain important documents. In 1934 Scelsi suffered from certain life-threatening pulmonary ailments that prompted him on November 9 to pen a holographic will, in which he directed a sum of money to be disposed of as follows:

Ten thousand liras in favor of Maestro G. Sallustio, five thousands to M Professor Riccardo Falk and five thousand to Signora Ornella Puliti Santoliquido as compensation for the time she invested in working on my Concerto.5

It is interesting that Scelsi crossed out the M[aestro] for Falk, substituting it with Professor, as he wanted to establish a categorical difference between Sallustio and Falk. However, the relationship between the two men was far from being superficial. For instance, on October 10, 1936, Scelsi wrote his mother that “Falk introduced me to a violinist and a cellist.”6 Then, on October 14 he cited Falk again, saying that “Sallustio comes every afternoon while Falk visits twice a week in the morning.”7 Furthermore, on the 18th we read:

The newspaper has announced the concert season at the “Augusteo” and it seems that Sallustio’s piece has been included. With him I am working on that little piano piece taken from the Danseuse while with Falk I am working on fixing the Trio for Piano, Violin, and Cello, those kinds of burlesque variations he had.8

In 1937, in another letter to Scelsi’s mother, dated April 3, we read:

Save for me all the reviews of the concerts presented at the “Mostra.” I have heard on the radio the one from Sunday, which was not remarkable at all. I have written to Falk and Costarelli to go and listen to them.9

Scelsi’s interest in musical life in Rome was not entirely serendipitous; in fact in 1937 he financed and organized the Musica Contemporanea, a series of concerts.
The Italian Racial Laws were enforced beginning in September 1937, and therefore Falk, being a Jew, was forced to leave the country; since he was of German nationality, it would have been out of the question for him to return home. He went unmentioned regarding musical matters in Scelsi’s correspondence with his mother for the whole of 1938 except for the following reference:

Bring me Falk’s Trio10 and the copy of the little piano piece you have already given to Ornella, that piece is still without a title.11

This is all we have! But in 1939 we find a peculiar business receipt dated December 19, 1939, for the amount of 2,500 liras that Scelsi evidently paid a moving company to deliver Falk’s furniture to New York.12 It might seem odd that under the circumstances Scelsi and Falk were involved in such a trivial transaction. However, the mention of a Steinway piano is relevant, since it appears in later correspondence as well.
And so we learn that Richard Falk had a very particular role in composing Scelsi’s Trio for Piano, Violin, and Cello and its revised version, Trio no. 2. According to the printed program for a concert held in Lausanne in 1942, the trio was composed in 1939; however, we have evidence that it was revised later, as confirmed by letters Falk wrote in 1940. Let us read them:

I am very busy with the andante, unfortunately though I cannot forge ahead with its elaboration as I would wish… there are too many problems, but half is completed and I think that its development is beautiful and interesting.13

And later:

I am very sorry about your poor health. I hope though that your staying at Cortina [d’Ampezzo] will heal the body… so you can resume working [on your music]! In the Trio (Andante) I have noted down the following: this movement must mature well before I send you the copy in the coming days when I think I can complete the work.14

Ultimately, Falk added this note:

Be assured that all is proceeding well with the Andante—also, the sketch which I wrote in a half hour—is long enough—but patience is needed for its elaboration.15

Also, enclosed with the letter were three brief music sketches Scelsi had penned, along with instructions concerning their development, which were probably those referred to in the letter Falk returned to Scelsi. An interesting detail though is a musical annotation, probably in Falk’s hand, on the back of the envelope (see example 8.1). This incipit is identical to that of the Andante movement of the Trio for Violin, Violoncello, and Piano that Scelsi published in 1947 in Rome for Edizioni de Santis.
The Trio no. 2 was first performed in Lausanne on June 11, 1942, by Edmond Appia on the piano, Paul Bourger on the violin, and Charles Lassueur on the cello. An interesting side note is the fact that Scelsi delivered a lecture before the concert at the Conservatoire de Lausanne where he had the opportunity to discuss his views on music. The transcription of this talk, revised and published some years later in the magazine Suisse contemporanée under the title “Sens de la musique,” constituted Giacinto Scelsi’s very first published writing.
On April 2, 1947, Isabella Scelsi, Countess of Zogheb, boarded a TWA Constellation flight—the Star of Madrid—from Rome to New York for her first extended stay in the United States. There she lectured extensively on the explosive political situation in Italy at the time and granted many interviews to magazines and newspapers, returning to Europe on May 25, 1949. She was primarily in New York, with occasional excursions to other states, Canada, and a protracted visit to the Bahamas from January 7 to March 4, 1949.

Notwithstanding her busy societal calender, Isabella Scelsi found the time to promote her brother’s music, seeking out publishers, organizers, performers, and contacts lost during the terrible war years. Her hope was for Giacinto to visit the United States at a time when in Italy civil war was in the air. Furthermore, she was seriously worried about her brother’s health. Isabella thought that escaping his daily worries and his wife Dorothy, whom he was trying to divorce at the time, would help him regain a measure of creative peace of mind. With this goal in mind, on several occasions Isabella met with Richard Falk, who offered to put his New York apartment and Steinway piano at Scelsi’s disposal. But all her efforts were for naught, as Scelsi was denied an entry visa to the United States. We can appreciate the countess’s disappointment, seen here in an excerpt of a letter she sent Falk on October 31, 1948:

Dear Maestro,
You were correct, indeed! Mr. Heinsleimer was barely courteous and referred us to other people. I am beginning to understand that everybody is afraid to make commitments because there are too many Europeans who wish to emigrate to America, thus nobody believes that Giacinto wants to sojourn only for a few months and should he wished to stay longer, the person signing the affidavit does not wish to assume further responsibilities. But, I have a better way that I want to follow and I am expecting a visit from someone in this regard.
All the best,
Isabella16

In August of 1948 Isabella traveled to California, where Walter Klein had sought refuge. It is possible that they met, since Klein had begun the process for procuring Scelsi an invitation to lecture. Also, while in the three extant letters Richard Falk sent to Scelsi during the postwar period there is no mention of musical collaboration between the two, there is information concerning Falk’s professional activities in New York, particularly in the field of Pergolesi studies and a “musical play” he was working on.17 Falk insisted on asking about Duke Caffarelli and asked Scelsi to intervene with the Italian aristocrat about mobilizing his influential New York acquaintances. Furthermore Falk informed Scelsi that Jesi’s Società Pergolesi had named him honorary president, but he wrote that “Now they ask me for money because they are in debt! What can one do?”18
And, in fact, while there is no reference to any type of collaboration between the two, a shade of an indirect stylistic repositioning is perceptible:

I have terminated the “Musical Play” whose title is Honeymoon Aflyng[?].19 Here, they love my music, it is very melodic and tonal, naturally.20

And a few months later:

I have just listened to the radio broadcast of Honegger’s Jean d’Arc au Boucher. It is very interesting, very modern but not atonal, I think that one can write this way very well, without sliding continuously into atonality—by finding a melodic pathway. In this oratorio the spoken dialog is too preeminent at the expense of music… we have many things to discuss.21

It seems obvious enough that Scelsi had no intention of continuing long-distance collaborations, even if he left open the possibility in case he somehow managed an American visit. On the other hand, in those years Scelsi was suffering from a profound psychophysical crisis, all while trying to complete two complex compositions, Quartetto per archi and La nascita del verbo, works he mostly completed in Switzerland before returning to Italy. But by that time he had already found other collaborators to complete the task.
