9

About and Around Rotativa: A Look at Young Scelsi
Alessandra Carlotta Pellegrini
For a long time, Giacinto Scelsi was known mainly for the compositions of his mature years, a period that began in the second half of the 1950s. However, recent musicological research has been focusing on his earlier works, composed before the mid-1940s. Is there an explanation for such a change of direction? Perhaps we have come to the realization that Scelsi grew musically and spiritually, confronting himself with the most diverse cultural and musical experiences available in his time. It was only once he had thoroughly experimented with them and found that they did not correspond to his creative needs that he proceeded to build his own personal dimension.
A more exhaustive investigation on certain issues could shine a light on the growth of an artistic personality and unique stylistic hallmark.
This chapter has been structured in four parts: (1) the study of the stimuli associated with the historical avant-garde, the musical vanguard in particular; (2) Scelsi’s encounters during his travels in his youth, when he journeyed to Egypt, Paris, and Geneva, between the late 1920s and the mid-1930s; (3) a focus on his relationship with his teacher and mentor Giacinto Sallustio; and, finally, (4) an analysis of “Rotativa,” whose debut led Scelsi onto the international music stage, focusing on the five different versions of this composition, the last of which was only recently discovered among his papers.
To understand where Giacinto Scelsi stood on the international music scene during his first phase of creativity (1929–1949), we must examine some of his early writings, in particular Sens de la musique, Évolution de l’harmonie, and Évolution du rythme.1 These provide a clear, personal account of the main factors influencing music history’s development in the first half of the twentieth century (especially within the Parisian context), from which we can easily infer Scelsi’s particular interests and the artistic experiences and trends that most especially caught his attention during this period. Information about his education, studies, and musical training are scarce, so relying on this body of writings becomes essential, providing a sort of a posteriori summary of what he experienced and assimilated.2
It is important to point out that the writings in question were published between the end of the 1930s and the mid-1940s, by which time Giacinto Scelsi had completed his education and was coming to the end of his first creative phase. This was during a period he spent in Switzerland, a time often considered to be a period of isolation and crisis but that was of crucial significance. For it was here that his first theoretical and aesthetic ideas took shape, to be collected into a body of writings that was enlarged and embellished over the following years. In this regard, the dense autobiographical account Il sogno 101 has particular significance; for although the main focus of the text is on aesthetic considerations, long passages also deal with the cultural and artistic experiences of Scelsi’s formative years.3
From his autobiography and from certain important letters—in particular to his mother, Donna Giovanna d’Ayala Valva4—we learn that Giacinto Scelsi spent long periods in Paris during the 1930s, where equally significant and innovative movements were succeeding the historic avant-garde. As a result of his experiences in Paris, Scelsi received and assimilated three very important “messages.”5 The first related to the special harmony of Debussy, which had the effect, among others, of stimulating a new interest in oriental music, and which “encouraged in particular a study of the scales, methods, rhythms of Eastern music, since many people—and not just musicians—realized that this art offered a particular meaning and a different perspective and led us on in an unknown direction.”6
The second important lesson Scelsi learned came via Russian music and the rhythm in Stravinsky’s three Russian ballets (The Firebird, Petrushka, and The Rite of Spring), as well as Les noces. In Évolution du rythme, Scelsi summed up the “lesson in rhythm” of this great twentieth-century composer:

But it was Russian music that first demonstrated this essentially rhythmic form of expression; it possessed an elemental violence that was labeled barbarous, but its power was shattering.… It is with Stravinsky that rhythm manifests itself in its most violent form for the first time in European music. In effect, although harmony is both essential and meaningful in Stravinsky, rhythm is the decisive factor.7

In the same text, we note the third element that inspired the Parisian scene during the 1910s and 1920s. This was macchinismo (mechanization), an idea rooted in Futurism and the Manifesto tecnico della musica futurista (Technical Manifesto of Futurist Music), published by Balilla Pratella in 1911 and in keeping with the more general Manifesto del Futurismo (Futurist Manifesto) issued by Filippo Tommaso Marinetti in 1909. Scelsi declared that

a further demonstration of the influence rhythm had over artists was the worship of the machine and mechanics: a phenomenon that manifested itself in all the arts. Futurism was a precursor of this since already, in 1909, Marinetti was glorifying the locomotive, the singing of the pistons and the ball bearings in a rhythmic form of poetic mimicry. Futurism was no less than an imperious desire to advocate rhythm above all else and for its own sake.8

He continued:

We therefore have to distinguish two groups in relation to this glorification of mechanization and dynamism. The first of these uses the machine or mechanical object either in opposition to, or as a substitute for, a landscape subject, a still life, etc. Works in this category are based on rhythms and the imitation of harmonies and stem as much from a reactive impulse or the desire for an antisentimental, antiemotional form of aesthetic as from the necessity to express pure rhythm. This is shown by the fact that rhythmic language, which is essentially imitative, like harmony, cannot be expressed according to its own laws but is subject to the descriptive and external conception of the particular model.… In compensation, and thanks to their genuine talent and virtuosity, certain artists have managed to create some excellent paintings, a number of which are truly exceptional. Among the best known are Honegger’s Pacific 231 and Fonderies d’accier by Mosolov.9

Finally, he tells us that

the second category is that in which the external rhythm of a machine, a game, or a human operation is expressed according to its own law and is seen as the subject of the work. Here the artists are not in the least concerned by this process where the primary origin or material of the rhythm is made to disappear completely.10

The references to Arthur Honegger and Alexander Mosolov are important within the scope of our discussion. They did indeed compose the two works mentioned by Scelsi (in 1923 and from 1926 to 1928, respectively), both of them inspired by mechanization, dynamism, and the excitement of the machine.11
We may certainly assume that the three “messages” Scelsi absorbed (the harmony of Debussy, the rhythm of Stravinsky, and the mechanization of the Futurists) reached him by means of his various teachers, in particular Alfredo Casella, who had spent nearly thirty years in Paris from the late 1920s onward. Nevertheless, in regard to Casella, Scelsi wrote that

meanwhile, it would be as well to make it clear immediately that I was never a student either of Respighi or of Casella—as some have said and even written. I knew both of them, but I never took any lessons from them.… I often visited Respighi’s house, a beautiful villa at the Camilluccia, sometimes after a concert at the Accademia di Santa Cecilia when these concerts were performed at the Augusteo.12

In the next passage, he reaffirmed this, saying that

Casella was totally different: he looked like an elderly gentleman—even though he wasn’t old—a distinguished gentleman, who was sometimes quite sharp, but polite, and even a little cold, or at least he appeared to be. He would have had Petrassi and others as his pupils. His was a neoclassical school that, I have to say, had little attraction for me, just as the music of Respighi also held little to interest me. So I only visited them as a friend.13

Scelsi always considered his first teacher to be the Italian composer Giacinto Sallustio.14 Writing more than thirty years later in his autobiography, Scelsi expressed himself in these terms:

I will tell you about my teachers—music teachers, that is to say… the first one was Giacinto Sallustio, who was actually a good friend of Respighi. He was a good man—I would definitely say that his best quality was his kindness. He was fat, with a round face and a round belly.… he couldn’t hurt a fly.… This almost feminine sensitivity manifested itself in a certain gentleness and shyness, and also maybe in a lack of drive that hindered his progress in the world of music.… His idol was Debussy, and in fact his music had certain impressionist features; it was always beautiful, but a bit… lightweight. However, some of his compositions—published by Casa Ricordi—have been quite unjustly neglected.15

His second teacher was Egon Köhler, and again Scelsi speaks about him in his autobiography:

The second of my teachers was Egon Köhler, a Russian—from the Baltic to be precise—who went into exile in Switzerland at a certain time.… He had studied music and was a composer.… He was a great admirer of Scriabin, just as Sallustio idolized Debussy. And he admired Scriabin not just as a musician but also for his ideas about the world, philosophy, and, in particular, theosophy. It hardly seemed real to him, therefore, to find a client like me in Geneva, especially as I too was a great admirer of Scriabin.16

And again of Scriabin Scelsi writes that he “really represented the opposite of the extroverted and rather provincial aesthetic approach of Respighi and the neoclassical stance of Casella.”17
Apart from his declared admiration for Scriabin, these passages make it clear that Scelsi assigned a wholly relative importance to the Italian context during this period. Nevertheless, among Scelsi’s early compositions one can find clear indications of a cultural climate advocated by Alfredo Casella and Gianfrancesco Malipiero, strongly linked to the rediscovery and reclamation of the Italian musical tradition of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. This was aimed at restoring an awareness of instrumental music in the Italian context after more than a century under lyric opera’s “dictatorship.”
A handful of Scelsi pieces for voice and piano date from this period: Tre canti (with verses from “Poema paradisiaco” by Gabriele D’Annunzio, 1932), Tre canti di primavera (with verses by Sibilla Aleramo, 1933), and L’amour et le crâne (with verses by Charles Baudelaire, 1933). Scelsi also experimented with form during this period, like his contemporaries Goffredo Petrassi, Luigi Dallapiccola, and Giovanni Salviucci, to mention just the better known.
During the same period—1932 to 1936—Scelsi produced a number of other works, including Sinfonietta, Concertino, Ouverture, Preludio, Arioso e Fuga, and Toccata. Even from the beginning his work emerged with a distinctive stamp, which, to a greater or lesser degree, had characterized Scelsi’s productive work during these years. The scores have been preserved—perhaps Scelsi found something noteworthy in them—but were never published. Maybe he considered them too far removed from the aesthetic criteria that, in the passing of the years, came to define his composition in a radically different way.
There are few documents and sources on Scelsi’s musical training that one might described as unusual, private, cosmopolitan, nonacademic, and autodidactic. As mentioned previously, and from what we can infer from various documents in the Scelsi archive,18 written in what was in all probability the teacher’s own handwriting, we know that Scelsi was first given private lessons in composition by Giacinto Sallustio.
Regular correspondence with his mother, Donna Giovanna d’Ayala Valva, provides us with almost daily news of Scelsi’s work at the time. It is clear from this correspondence that Scelsi also worked with his teacher on the score for Rotativa during a stay at Vallombrosa (near Florence) in the summer of 1930.
Scelsi wrote in early August, “I would like a piano to be sent on from Florence, but I do not know if I would be granted one for just a month.”19 Then, on the 9th of August, he wrote, “I have heard from Sallustio that he will arrive in Florence tomorrow. I would prefer to go [to Vallombrosa] earlier because I need to arrange a room for him and then choose a piano.”20 The next day: “I am now at Vallombrosa. The small place where I have a room for the piano affords a beautiful view. Sallustio is staying at a small hotel directly opposite.”21 The 15th of August: “I spent practically the entire day [with] Sallustio in my studio.”22 The 19th of August: “The music is going reasonably well, not exactly how I had imagined it, but then one cannot always realize one’s thoughts. I now need good inspiration for the ending, which is not quite clear in my mind, but hopefully it will come to me.”23 The 23rd of August: “I found the ending to my composition this morning. I would like it to be slightly different, and I might yet change it. But at least I have a draft. It is very difficult to play, and even I will have to practise a lot to perform it.”24 And, finally, on the 31st of August: “Sallustio left this morning.”25 While confirming what Scelsi said in his autobiography—that Sallustio was his first teacher—the correspondence suggests their relationship was not perhaps a typical one.
The orchestral version of Rotativa was Giacinto Scelsi’s first piece debuted internationally, premiering at the Salle Pleyel, Paris, on December 20, 1931, with the Orchestre Symphonique de Paris, conducted by Pierre Monteux.26

Like I said, Monteux conducted the first performance of my Rotativa, a piece that describes the power and rhythm of a great machine. As well as a driving rhythm, it also had a certain lyricism. The same can be said of “Pacific 231” by Honegger and “Fonderies d’acier” by Mosolov. When I composed “Rotativa” I did not know these two pieces, but the exaltation of the strength and of certain poetry of the great machines and their power was in the air. “Rotativa” was composed in 1929 and conducted by Monteux in 1930. To tell the truth, I should say that the rhythm also clearly described a very human rhythm, and I would have liked to have given it an erotic title; but I never had the opportunity, and the great machine remained the pretext and the alibi. I was often asked up front to play this piece at the piano, and I would do so to a reasonable standard. I realized, too, that the body of the orchestra was too big for a piece that lasts only six minutes. This was why I later created a new version. A French painter, whose name escapes me, once said “Il faut peindre avec peu de couleurs,” and indeed there were too many colours. Also, the use of many tools is a folly of youth. I now think almost the opposite: it is best to use few tools and few notes, or even a single note.… The piece was performed again at Cannes with the orchestra of the Montecarlo Casino, which was not particularly wonderful; however I remember hearing it conducted by Toscanini, who transformed it, and Monteux drew on this to create sounds that were totally unexpected.27

This piece was, therefore, to be revisited over the years: Scelsi worked on Rotativa in stages, eventually creating five different versions. There are two versions for orchestra, and both remain unpublished.
An early version—subtitled Movimento sinfonico—includes a section for string instruments (first and second violins, violas, violoncellos, and double basses), as well as for woodwind, percussion, and piano. The score and parts are still available.28 We do not know the name of the copyist who collaborated with Scelsi in the drafting of the score—the handwriting has not yet been identified with certainty—but we can say that the same hand returns with considerable frequency in the orchestral works of this period. As for the parts, the handwriting varies, but in this case the hand reoccurs on other pages by Scelsi. See examples 9.1, 9.2, and 9.3.



The second version of Rotativa for orchestra does not include a section for string instruments, instead using woodwind, brass, percussion, harps, an organ, and three pianos. Only the score remains of this version, and the handwriting of the copyist seems to vary. See examples 9.4a, 9.4b, 9.5a, and 9.5b. Table 9.1 lists all the instruments used in each orchestral version. For the purpose of comparison, the differences are highlighted in boldface.
Another version version of Rotativa, also dated 1930, calls for “two pianos and thirteen percussion instruments” and is held in the Scelsi archive. It premiered in Geneva at the Archipel Festival on March, 27, 2007,29 making use of the score published by Editions Salabert, Paris, for the very same occasion.30 The handwriting on the score is the same as that on the version for orchestra without a string section. See example 9.6. The orchestral instruments listed on the first few pages are also in the same handwriting. See example 9.7.



A fourth version of “Rotativa,” for solo piano—published by Ricordi in 1933 and now out of print—was premiered in Rome on March 12, 1934, by Ornella Puliti Santoliquido.31 The published score has an epitaph as interesting as it is significant, with a quotation from the poem “The Great Lover” (1914) by Rupert Brooke (1887–1915): “The keen unpassioned beauty of a great machine.”32 See example 9.8.
The final recorded version of Rotativa is for two pianos; it is a transcription by Pietro Scarpini, testified as attested by the invitation to the concert organized by the Italian Cultural Institute of Lausanne on June 11, 1942, with a music program consisting of Scelsi and Piero Coppola, featuring pianists Nikita Magaloff and Maurice Perrin. There is a significant comment (in brackets) on the score edited by Salabert (1986, EAS 18396): subtitle mechanical lovemaking.

Each of the five versions of Rotativa is each unique in timbre. They all, however, share certain characteristics that emphasize the same musical substance: first, the polyrhythmic and polymetrics, with constant alternation between binary, ternary, and quaternary metrics; and, second, the use of irregular rhythmic patterns, with frequent shifts in emphasis. There is evident denial concerning the concept of development of the material; we notice, instead, the juxtaposition of repeated structures throughout the entire composition. Scelsi characterizes each rhythmic structure with a precise rhythmic pattern.
In light of the above, Rotativa is evidently a debut piece, however the many versions in existence leave no room for doubt that the author did not consider it a simple youthful exercise but a composition into which to channel much thought and work.
Rotativa is a piece that both assimilates and metabolizes the cultural and, in particular, the musical scene of the 1920s; it refers to the teachers and models but essentially leaves us to understand that Scelsi’s approach was—already at the time—fully independent and “transversal.”

If, as Scelsi wrote in Évolution de l’harmonie, “the artist is influenced by nothing other than the direct or indirect answers to his questions, either conscious or unconscious,” we can agree with him that “it is not the ‘influences’ on artists that should be studied but the evolution of the questions that prompt the answers,” because “the importance of ‘influences’ is always in relation to the degree of accuracy of the answer given to the question posed.”33
We can conclude that by studying the “evolution of questions” covered above, Scelsi turned out answers that were perfectly “exact” and to his satisfaction.

En effet, si dans Stravinskij l’harmonie est essentielle et significative, le rythme est déterminant.” Giacinto Scelsi, Évolution du rythme, in his Les anges sont ailleurs, 109, 111.

Le futurisme n’était d’ailleurs que le désir impérieux de la manifestation rythmique avant toute chose et pour elle-même.” Scelsi, Évolution du rythme, in his Les anges sont ailleurs, 120.
9. Il faut donc distinguer dans l’exaltation du machinisme ou du dynamisme deux catégories. La première prend la machine ou l’objet mécanique en opposition et comme substitution au sujet paysage, nature morte, etc. Les oeuvres de cette catégorie sont fondées sur des rythmes et harmonies imitatifs, et proviennent d’un désir de réaction ou de l’esthétique anti-sentimentale, anti-affective, tout autant que du besoin d’expression rythmique pure. La preuve en est que le langage rythmique, comme l’harmonie, étant imitatif, n’est point par ce fait exprimé selon ses lois propres, mais est soumis à la conception descriptive et extérieur du modèle. … Par contre, certains artistes ont réussi, grâce à leur talent véritable et à leur virtuosité, des tableaux remarquables dont quelques-uns sont saisissants. Parmi les plus connus, Pacific 231 de Honegger et Fonderie d’accier de Mossolov. Scelsi, Évolution du rythme, in his Les anges sont ailleurs, 121.
10. “La deuxième catégorie est celle où le rythme extérieur d’une machine, d’un jeu ou d’un travail humain est développé ou exprimé selon sa loi propre et ressentie subjectivement, les artistes ne s’inquiétant nullement dans ce processus où l’origine première ou matérielle du rythme disparaît totalement.” Scelsi, Évolution du rythme, in his Les anges sont ailleurs, 122.

14. Giacinto Sallustio (1879–1938) was an Italian composer and friend of Ottorino Respighi and Riccardo Zandonai. Influenced by the French school and Debussy in particular, Sallustio’s compositional style was clearly in antithesis to the Italian stylistic trends emerging during the 1930s. Among his most memorable compositions are La solitude for voice and piano (with words by Franz Toussaint, published in 1930 by Ricordi), dedicated “to the Marchesa Dorothy Imperiali,” future wife of Giacinto Scelsi; and Spleen for voice and piano (with words by Paul Verlaine, published in 1933 by Ricordi), dedicated “to the Contessa Isabella de Zogheb,” Giacinto Scelsi’s sister. Among scores by other composers, the Scelsi archive also contains copies of La solitude and Fiorella for voice and piano (with words by the same Giacinto Sallustio, published in 1935 by Ricordi). This is clear evidence of the strong collaborative ties and mutual respect that existed between Scelsi and Sallustio.

The foundation—awarded the Decree of the President of the Italian Republic in 1990 by Italy’s Ministero per i Beni e le Attività Culturali—was established on January 21, 1987, by Giacinto Scelsi, who wanted it to be named after his sister, Isabella. The foundation is located at Via di San Teodoro 8, the house where Scelsi lived during the last twenty years of his life.

The concert program also included the overture to Weber’s Oberon, Bach’s Concerto in E Major for violin and orchestra, the Nocturne and Scherzo from Mendelssohn’s Midsummer Night’s Dream, Rudolf Mengelberg’s Concerto for violin and orchestra (also premiered), and Suite no. 2 from Ravel’s Daphnis et Chloe. Regarding the composition of this concerto, the recitals, its success, and the press release (Le Figaro, Le Menestrel, L’Intransigeant, Le Monde Musical, Le Courier Musical), it is worth noting the exhaustive essay by Luciano Martini published in 2001: “Linotype, coitus mechanicus, ‘Rotativa’: Il singolare debutto di Giacinto Scelsi.” In Castanet and Cisternino, eds., Giacinto Scelsi: Viaggio al centro del suono, 297–320. Also in regard to this composition see Adriano Cremonese, “Giacinto Scelsi: Prassi compositiva e riflessione teorica fino alla metà degli anni ’40,” Quaderni Perugini di Musica Contemporanea 1 (or in book form, Palermo: L’epos società editrice, 1992, 9–17); Gregory N. Reish, The Transformation of Giacinto Scelsi’s Musical Style and Aesthetic, 1929–1959, Ph.D. diss., University of Georgia, 2001, 125–31; Alessandra Carlotta Pellegrini, “‘Rotativa’: Le début de Scelsi sur la scène musicale internationale,” Archipel: Festival des musiques d’aujourd’hui, Geneva, March 23–April 1, 2007, available online at http://www.archipel.org/archive/site07/index.php?top=2&sub=3&item=4&main=ScelsiPellegrini; Nicola Verzina, “Alcune categorie del futurismo in ‘Rotativa,’” in Tortora, ed., Giacinto Scelsi nel centenario della nascita, 37–54; Franco Sciannameo, La musica per pianoforte, Scelsi Collection vol. 4, booklet accompanying the Stradivarius CD, STR33804, 2008, 4–9.
27. Giacinto Scelsi, Il sogno 101, 240–41.
28. The archive at the Archivio Fondazione Isabella Scelsi, corda 125.1.
29. The Italian premiere took place in 2007 at Montepulciano as part of the Cantiere d’Arte, with the Ars Ludi Ensemble, conducted by Marco Angius, then published in the second volume of the Scelsi Collection of the Italian music label Stradivarius (CD STR 33802).
30. Giacinto Scelsi, “Rotativa” pour 2 pianos et 13 percussions (Paris: Editions Salabert, 007), EAS 18396.
31. Available recordings are by Daniele Lombardi (col legno, 1994-97, WWE 20076) and Donna Amato (Scelsi Collection, vol. 4, Stradivarius, 2008, STR 33804).
32. In this regard, refer to the exhaustive coverage by Verzina’s “Alcune categorie del Futurismo,” 37–54. See also Sciannameo’s, “La musica giovanile,” 4–9.
33. “L’artiste n’est jamais ‘influencé’ par autre chose que par des réponses directes ou indirecte à ses questions, celle-ci soient-elle conscientes ou incoscientes”, “ce n’est pas donc les ‘influences’ qu’il faut étudier chez les artistes, mais l’évolution des questions qui appellent les réponses”, “l’importance des ‘influences’ est toujours en rapport avec le degré d’exactitude de la réponse donnée à une question posée.” Giacinto Scelsi, Évolution de l’harmonie, in his Les anges sont ailleurs, 103.








10

Funziona? O non funziona? An Excursion through the Scelsi Archives
Friedrich Jaecker
The opening of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi’s archives can be considered the zero hour for Scelsi research. While he was still alive Giacinto Scelsi had established his foundation to which he bequeathed his musical estate. Since his death in 1988, all sound documents, scores, and writings have been kept there under lock and key. The flimsy explanations that have been used as an excuse for this tight security are revealed in an interview Stefania Gianni granted Christine Anderson. Gianni was, at the time, in charge of the archives.1 Since May 6, 2009, the archivio storico has finally been made accessible for scientific research. Composer Nicola Sani, foundation president since 2004, energetically sped up its opening, pushing it through against all odds. The director of the archives is now musicologist Alessandra Carlotta Pellegrini. As a national cultural establishment the foundation is supervised by Mauro Tosti-Croce, who is in charge of the scientific development of archives and libraries in Italy’s Ministry of Culture.
The archives are located in Scelsi’s last place of residence, in Rome, at Via di San Teodoro 8, directly opposite the Roman Forum. The house’s parlor, where Scelsi’s beloved sister, Isabella, lived—and after whom the foundation was named—contains a work area with writing desks, computers, stereo equipment, and a reference library. An in-house computer network allows access to the catalog, in which scores, phonograph records, and audiotapes are kept on record. Another section of the catalog is dedicated to Scelsi’s writings, letters, reviews of his works, photos, and miscellaneous personal documents. The catalog contains an extensive bibliography.
The holdings are said to contain more than sixteen thousand documents. The written documents are located in spacious archival cabinets. The audiotapes are the heart of the Scelsi estate. In the Istituto Centrale per i Beni Sonori e Audiovisivi (the Central Institute for Sound and Audiovisual Documents), which is located in the heart of Rome, more than six hundred audiotapes are being digitized under the supervision of Nicola Bernardini, professor of electronic composition and sound engineering at the Conservatorio “Cesare Pollini” in Padua. Bernardini, in addition to converting every track of the recorded audiotapes into sound files, generated the corresponding supplementary files when doubts arose concerning the playback speed or direction. All the tracks can be listened to via the computer network installed at the Scelsi archives. The features of the digital copies and the original tapes are documented in the catalog, as is the condition of the cardboard boxes in which many of the audiotapes are preserved.
So far, 273 audiotapes have been digitized. I have made it my duty to listen to them in their entirety and to record the results in detail. Under the title I nastri di Giacinto Scelsi (The Audiotapes of Giacinto Scelsi), they are available at the foundation and can be used by all interested researchers. Efforts continue in the digitizing of additional audiotapes and, some day, phonograph records. Of particular interest are, of course, Scelsi’s improvisations, particularly those he had transcribed into score format. So far, 52 works with 153 individual movements could be identified. Among them are compositions as important as Anagamin, Hurqualia, Quartet no. 4, Quattro pezzi per orchestra, Tre canti sacri, and Yamaon. The list of identified works records the work’s title, archive number of the audiotape, number of the track, start and finish of the recording, and, as necessary, comments about whether the recording is complete, whether it is a phonograph recording, and so forth. In addition, all other recordings that were not transcribed are listed in the listening logs of the individual audiotapes and described by identifying keywords.
Many audiotapes feature a wide-ranging mix of improvisations on piano, ondio-line, guitar, or other instruments, voice recordings, sound experiments, recordings of radio broadcasts, and phonograph records. Since Scelsi made new recordings on old tapes again and again, one can often hear traces of old recordings, which, at times, are only a few seconds long.
But Scelsi also compiled and arranged a group of works on a series of tapes. For example, on audiotape no. 119, the nine movements of Action Music, 3 preludi from the First Series, Cinque incantesimi, and 3 preludi from the Fourth Series can be heard on the left sound track, while the right track features the Suite no. 9, Ttai, as well as an additional recording of Cinque incantesimi.2 Action Music and Incantesimi are improvisations by Scelsi, while the preludes are phonograph transfers of recordings made by an unknown pianist. Another “collective sound tape” combines ten works for wind ensemble, another one comprises works for viola, and so on.3 Hence, Scelsi transferred the original recordings of the improvisations onto another tape using a second tape recorder. Many pieces feature several copies. The leading specimen is Cinque incantesimi for piano with at least eight versions. For the collective audiotapes, Scelsi generally wrote the titles of the pieces on the cardboard boxes in which the audiotapes were stored.
But it is not always so simple. At times titles of works are located on boxes but cannot be found on the audiotapes. Either the pieces have subsequently been deleted, or the boxes have been swapped. Frequently there are also early forms of titles, such as “Spagnoli” for Hispania, “Ixion” instead of Ixor, “Nomos” instead of Hymnos, “Tanmatras” instead of Quattro pezzi per orchestra, and other unknown titles. Sometimes, Scelsi also labeled the boxes at a later point in time when he still intended to do something else with his improvisations. Thus the box of audiotape marked as no. 22 reads as follows: Four Pieces for Guitar (amplified with micro), © Copyright/Scelsi, Viale Mazzini 9, Roma. Later, Scelsi adopted two of these pieces in his work for guitar Ko-Tha, while three movements were transcribed as TKRDG for six male voices, amplified guitar, and three percussionists. In numerous cases there is no reference whatsoever to the content of the tapes, such as in the case of Xnoybis or the Canti sacri, and for some of the tapes the boxes have been lost.
Most of Scelsi’s works are relatively short pieces that he later combined into multimovement cycles. The original improvisations of the individual pieces can often be found at different positions in the length of a tape or even on different audiotapes. An exception is Quattro illustrazioni for piano. Scelsi obviously improvised all four movements of this work immediately following each other in a single session, because no end and restart of the audiotape can be heard between movements.4
While the individual movements are generally faded in and out for the collective sound tapes by using the volume control, the recordings of the original improvisations start abruptly. By tapping the microphone, Scelsi often checks whether everything is working well, or one can hear his voice uttering the question “Funziona? O non funziona?” (Does it work? Or does it not work?). At the end of the improvisation, he abruptly switches the device off again.
Sometimes only segments from longer improvisations are transcribed in the scores. The improvisation then continues, even though the transcription is already over, whereas the beginnings of improvisation and transcription generally correspond with each other.5 Scelsi documented the pieces to be transcribed by marking the boxes with label such as pezzi ottimi (very good pieces), buono (good), vale (acceptable or valid), da sentire (to listen to), da fare (to do), and similar and, in the process, often indicated the numerical display of the tape recorder’s counter. It also happens that he discards his own improvisations as mediocre (mediocre), stufa (boring) or cattivo (bad). Hence Scelsi retrospectively listened to the results of his improvisatory technique with a critical ear and carefully selected the items to be transcribed in score format.
EARLY WORKS?
Several years ago I ventured to claim that a large part of Scelsi’s piano works, which were supposedly written between the years 1930 and 1941, could actually have been created significantly later.6 The works in question are Suite nos. 2, 5, 6, and 7, Sonata nos. 2, 3, and 4, as well as the triptych Hispania. All scores show the writing of Vieri Tosatti, who started working for Scelsi in 1947. Since home tape recorders have only been in production since 1950, I concluded at the time that these transcriptions could not have come from audiotapes that would have been recorded before Tosatti’s activity as a transcriber. Meanwhile, a device was discovered in one of Scelsi’s early residences that allowed self-recordings of phonograph records as well as a series of recorded phonograph records. Phonograph recorders had been commercially available since the early thirties. So far it is not known when Scelsi bought his equipment. He could have been capable of recording his own improvisations before 1950, but the listed works differ significantly from the works printed by Ricordi at the beginning of the thirties and by De Santis at the end of the forties, though they do show a certain similarity to the piano works of the years 1952 to 1954. The phonograph records have not been digitized as of yet, but Scelsi did transfer many of them to audiotape later on. They can be recognized, without difficulty, by the noise of the needle hitting down onto the plate and the typical crackling and noise of old phonograph records.
What are the new findings about the “early works” gained from listening to the tapes? Only Suite no. 6, presumably from the years 1938 to 1939, and the third movement of the Piano Sonata no. 2, dated 1939, have been transferred from phonograph records. No improvisations have yet been found for Suite no. 7 and the Piano Sonata no. 4. All other works have been improvised not on phonograph records but on audiotapes instead. Furthermore Scelsi appears to have used the phonograph recorder as well as a tape recorder for a long time because the first movement of Piano Sonata no. 3 shows traces of a phonograph transfer with—but also without—tape copies not bearing the usual noises.7 Hence Scelsi also transferred tape recordings to phonograph records and, in this case as well as subsequent cases, later transferred them back to audiotape.8 Scelsi wrote “Da fare il disco” (make it into a phonograph record) on some of the tape-recording boxes. Hence it is not possible to assume an early time of creation simply because a phonograph transfer existed!9
The box of the aforementioned audiotape containing Piano Sonata no. 3 also carries the label “Cafaro.”10 In my conversation with Sergio Cafaro, who worked as transcriber for Scelsi for several years, he only ever mentioned dischi—that is, phonograph records—and never audiotapes.11 It is possible that Scelsi transferred his tape improvisations onto phonograph records exclusively for Cafaro, since he did not have a tape recorder. Until now it has not been possible to determine which pieces Sergio Cafaro transcribed for Scelsi. The Scelsi archives contain a manuscript of Cinque incantesimi that features the following note at the beginning of the fourth movement: “Cafaro (8a grave alla fine)” (According to Cafaro keep lower octave until the end).12 This allows us to conclude that Tosatti’s score of Cinque incantesimi was based on a transcription by Cafaro. It cannot be ruled out that Cafaro had done preparatory work for the transcription of many additional piano improvisations.
SCELSI AS TRANSCRIBER
One of the central questions in the Scelsi c’est moi scandal initiated by Tosatti was whether and, if so, to what extent Scelsi had participated in the transcription of his improvisations. In this case, however, general answers do not lead us anywhere. Instead it is rather helpful to examine the question from different perspectives. An initial aspect, namely the selection and limitation of the pieces to be transposed, has already been discussed. It is followed by the question whether—and how—Scelsi determined the arrangement of his works. The piano improvisations can be skipped here since all of them have also been transcribed for piano. The improvisations on the ondioline allow for a greater latitude. While the octave and sound registers suggest certain instruments or exclude others, the ondioline sound is too “artificial” to truly suggest traditional instruments.
For his solo works Scelsi often provides precise annotations on the tape-recording boxes about the arrangement, such as per tromba (for trumpet), per violoncello (for cello), or per voce (for voice). He often also notates different possibilities, such as v.no o flauto (violin or flute)13 and eventualmente il 1º per corno e cb. (possibly the 1st [piece] for French horn and double bass),14 or he writes down yet-unclear ideas, such as da fare per strumenti strani (to be scored for unusual instruments).15 For chamber music works for two instruments, one can also find conclusive instrumental information, such as for the Duo for violin and cello,16 although they do deviate slightly at times. Hence Scelsi had initially intended Kshara to be for viola and double bass,17 whereas the transcription was then done for two double basses. In the case of Rucke di Guck for piccolo and oboe, the boxes always show the label ottavino (piccolo).18 In this case, it appears that the idea to reapportion this highly virtuosic music to two instruments to make it easier to play and design it more colorfully at the same time was initiated by the transcriber.
The Trio à cordes is not mentioned on two boxes of tape recordings;19 on a third one, Scelsi writes “Trio” with counter information for the four movements.20 Of particular relevance is the third movement, which can be found twice on the same audiotape. It is immediately preceded by the ondioline version for the second movement of Xnoybis for unaccompanied violin for which any annotations on the box are missing. Upon closer observation it becomes evident that the version of Trio is nothing else but the audiotape of Xnoybis played backward! The scores of the companion works were created in 1958 (Trio) and 1964 (Xnoybis). The conclusiveness of this information is based on the fact that a new notation technique was used in Xnoybis, in which each of the four strings was assigned its own system of notation, whereas the Trio was still conventionally notated. The interval of at least six years shows how far apart improvisation and transcription can be.
The designations of string quartets, pieces for string orchestra, and works with large numbers of instruments located on the cardboard boxes do not allow any inferences to Scelsi’s influences on the selection of the arrangement. The instrumentalists listed in each case either precisely match the transcribed works, such as for String Quartet no. 4 (Quartetto 4º),21 or the orchestral ensembles are only designated in general, such as for Chukrum (“per archi,” [for strings],22 Quattro pezzi per orchestra (4 pezzi orchestra, [4 pieces [for] orchestra]),23 or for Anahit (“pezzo per vl e piccola orchestra” [piece for violin and small orchestra]).24 Whether this arrangement information was provided before or after the transcription cannot be determined.
THE INVISIBLE COMPOSER
The Scelsi archives’ folders containing the scores include primarily manuscripts, transparencies, blueprints, and, on rare occasion, photocopies. In most cases these are the direct models for the printed versions. Sketches or corrections that allow inferences about the creative process of the pieces are extremely rare. Sometimes the titles of the scores are changed. The piano piece Un adieu, for example, also exists as a photocopy under the tile “per Marina.” In the manuscript written in pencil this title has been erased, but it remains barely readable.
A treasure chest is the folder containing the Cinque incantesimi, which not only includes the blueprints with the print version but also an earlier version with numerous corrections. The term designation deciso imperioso (decisively peremptory) of the first movement, which is also circled in red, is without a doubt Scelsi’s handwriting. The same holds true for performing directions: The word perensivo is crossed out and replaced with percussivo (percussion-like). Also circled in red are modified metronome markings, dynamic particulars such as sempre f or crescendo forks, stress marks, and pedal indications. A red pen was also used to supplement a few missing pauses as well as to overwrite the piece with “I.” Without a red circle, but clearly from Scelsi’s hand, is also the question mano destra? (right hand?) at the beginning of the piece. In this case Tosatti had transferred the entire first line of the left hand, whereas the subsequent print version showed the manuscript distributed to both hands.
Numerous additional corrections are either definitely or probably in Tosatti’s hand. These include numerous accidentals or orthographic corrections.25 Particularly significant are the modifications in the first movement of Hispania, whose manuscript—unidentified thus far—is situated in the Incantesimi score. In the later print version, numerous rhythms, single sounds, and chords are changed, but these corrections do not show any visible traces of Scelsi’s participation. As the comparison with other manuscripts shows, he seems to have limited himself in most cases to verbal characterizations and the sound surface of the works.
A composer who has not written a single note should be rather unique. And yet that is exactly true: Until now, not a single score has been found in the entire archives that, with absolute certainty, was written on paper by Scelsi. If at all, exceptions can be expected only in the early works. As such it cannot be ruled out for one untitled piece for piano and orchestra that Scelsi himself was at least partially involved. Apart from that he consistently left the tedious work of writing notes to others, while he for his part wanted to step behind the inspired work.
TKRDG, CKCKC
A special characteristic of Scelsi’s vocal works is their phonetic language. Information about how these sounds come about without semantic context is provided by the archives folder for TKRDG.26 This folder contains a sheet of paper on which phonemes from the second movement of CKCKC have been written in larger block letters with a blue ballpoint pen for voice and mandolin (to be performed by one player). The folder also contains the manuscript version of this movement notated in pencil by Tosatti, on which the phonemes have been entered—in the same writing as on the sheet and also with a blue ballpoint pen.27 It looks very much as if Scelsi wrote them himself, but they have certainly been entered only after the musical transcription. This also stands to reason because Scelsi improvised CKCKC on a mandolin and the phonemes cannot be inferred from the tape recording of the improvisation.28 They were added subsequently and, as such, were neither a component of the improvisation nor of the transcription.
CONSTRUCTIONS
Of special interest is the genesis of the works with constructive elements as described by Wolfgang Thein in his article “Botschaft und Konstruktion: Nichtimprovisierte Momente in der Musik Giacinto Scelsis.”29 In Dithome, the second part of the trilogy for unaccompanied cello, the manuscript runs symmetrically back to the start, beginning at the middle. It is admirable how Tosatti succeeded in appropriately notating Scelsi’s wild and enormously virtuosic ondioline improvisation. Until now the scope and complexity of this piece definitely ruled out Scelsi’s having also improvised the retrograded movement and, for this reason, its having been an involuntary construction. But the question whether Scelsi instructed his collaborator only verbally to notate the rewinding of the tape or whether he had already remastered the retrograded movement—using two tape recorders—has remained open until now. Listening to the audiotape shows that the latter may be the case in point. Dithome exists in several audiotape versions. In two of them the rewind is only incomplete,30 and in a last version matching the score it has been perfectly implemented.31
Scelsi selected a different constructive approach in his work for orchestra, Hymnos. The orchestra is divided into two similar groups of instruments (A and B), percussion and organ placed at the center of the score. In the first section of Hymnos, the two orchestra groups follow each other canonically, spaced at six measures and a quarter note apart. But it can soon be seen that not all the details of group A are literally transferred to group B. For example, the start of the French horn in measure 9 is heard three-sixteenths earlier than expected. In measure 17, the canon interval is reduced to exactly six measures. The longer the first section lasts, the more freedom the orchestra group B exercises in copying the sound textures of group A.
For this work, until now, it has also not been known what the state of the audiotape sample was. One could assume that it could hardly have been transcribed exclusively through listening. The complexity of the movement, the inevitably poor sound quality of the recording, and, last but not least, the fact that a mono recording does not allow for the spatial localization of both groups spoke against it. Initially the audiotape contains only the layer that was transcribed as orchestra group A.32 But Hymnos follows as a canon immediately. Hence Scelsi not only used the canonical construction speculatively, but he also implemented the sound result before the transcription process using his tape recorders, which allowed him to control them. The preceding version with only one layer enabled Tosatti to create a largely faithful transfer of the acoustic model.
IMPROVISATION AND TRANSCRIPTION
To create such complex movements as those we hear in Hymnos Scelsi used two tape recorders with which he could create dual or multilayered recordings using the playback method. The more complex the textures are, the more operations must have been required for their electroacoustic implementation. The term improvisation is no longer appropriate here. Combining several layers into a movement is “composing” in its literal meaning, even if the combined parts have been created by way of improvisation. Scelsi reveals himself as a composer in particular when applying traditional composition methods, such as the retrograde movement or the canon. The methods of transcription are still largely unknown. How were the sound textures acquired with respect to time and framed within a measure structure?
On the audiotapes for In nomine lucis for organ and Kshara for two double basses, one can hear the voice of Riccardo Filippini, Tosatti’s pupil, who recorded the clicking of a metronome and enunciated the beats of a 4/4 measure.33 The other track of the audiotape contains the piece to be transcribed. Then, if both tracks are then played in parallel fashion, we always have a certain control over the time sequence. But one can easily imagine how many design possibilities such a method would allow the transcriber in detail—or demand of him. Hence this method does not seem to have been the rule, otherwise more traces of a metronome should be detectable. Moreover, since Filippini claims to have invented this technique,34 we can assume that a metronome was generally ticking on the writing desk to accompany the sounds from the audiotape or phonograph record.
The more multilayered the audiotape sample and the greater the number of instruments of a work, the more it is called into question how the sound was transferred onto paper. Similar to Hymnos, we can also detect in the violin concerto Anahit that many details of the meticulously differentiated score can barely—if at all—be heard on the audiotape. As an example, we’ll examine a section of four measures (58–61) in greater detail. When listening to it on tape,35 two prominent events stand out: a wave-like glissando at the beginning, the changeover to a lower register at the end, and the use of wide vibrato. A pulsating soundscape that hardly changes with respect to its structure and intensity extends in between. In the score (see example 10.1) the sound at the beginning of measure 60 is extraordinarily differentiated by five different movement forms: calm tones in the flute, trombones, solo violin (second string) and double basses, wide vibrato in the saxophone, quintuplet sixteenth notes in the solo violin (first and third string), a notated trill in the first, and staccati in sextuplet sixteenth notes with appoggiature in the second viola. A color change takes place on the third count with the entrance of the French horns, on the fourth of the English horn and the trumpet, and one eighth later of the second flute. In measure 61 the two double basses start an interplay of eighths and triplet eighth notes. Finally, on the third count of the same measure, the cello enters playing fortissimo and al ponticello, with trills a quarter tone apart.

It is unmistakable that the tones used, their duration, and the “granular” sound character of the tape recording were the samples for writing the score. But all the details—the differentiated movement forms, the color change, the double bass rhythmic patterns, and the cello entrance—cannot be taken from the rather sweeping sound impression of Scelsi’s recording. They are due to the fancy and the skilled expertise of Vieri Tosatti. The term transcription is definitely no longer appropriate here. Does the “transcriber” subsequently have to be considered as the cocreator of the works? That will depend on the importance to be attributed to the compositional completion, the compositional detail for the quality of a piece. For works featuring a dense texture and an extensive number of instruments, the development performed at the writing desk plays a greater role than for a piece for solo cello. On the other hand, it should not be forgotten that Scelsi predetermined the idea, the dramaturgy, and the sound atmosphere even in his most complex works and—as far as it was possible with the technical means at his disposal—also implemented them electroacoustically. Masterpieces like Hymnos, String Quartet no. 4, Anahit, or Konx-Om-Pax would not exist without Scelsi’s visionary inventiveness and without Tosatti’s professional development.
Even if Scelsi composed with the help of electronic devices such as the ondioline and the tape recorder, his tape recordings are not yet finished compositions because he always attempted to transform and transfer them into instrumental or vocal works. His only electroacoustic release is the playback tape for Pranam I for voice, audiotape, and twelve instruments from the year 1972. Technically it corresponds to the professional standards of the time, but experienced collaborators may also have helped him in its creation. All other tape recordings sound more or less incomplete, beginning with the stiffness of the ondioline sounds due to poor level control, clearly audible switching on and off, and to the ever-present ambient noises. With all their defects, they act like sketches for a tonal picture still to be fully pursued. On the other hand, the traditional musical instruments, the human voice, or orchestras, like a string quartet or a symphony orchestra, always carry with them a certain aura that reshapes and enhances the electroacoustic sounds according to Western aesthetics. On the other hand, the gentle oscillations and differential tones can be heard more clearly and differentiated in the numerous pieces for two ondiolines, which operate with the finest graduations in pitch, than do those in the instrumental versions. Even Scelsi himself seems to have regarded the tape-recorded versions only as prestages to the works rendered in score format. With all the originality of the musical content and the production method, it reveals a strict orientation to the traditional Western idea of the musical work concept.
FINDS
The reasons for the rejection that the old Vieri Tosatti brought against the work of Giacinto Scelsi are still not known. Among the numerous letters to Scelsi collected in the archives there is only one from Tosatti and his wife Valeria from a summer vacation in 1961. Vieri Tosatti starts out with the salutation Carissimo (My dearest), reports on his own opera projects, inquires in a concerned way about Scelsi’s well-being, and asks “whether you have your ondioline with you or another possibility of getting something done.” He then reports about the progress in building the special cello mutes that Scelsi had wanted to employ in Triphon and continues, “We are often thinking of you, and in the evening we read: Valeria the letters of Wu Wei36 and I those of Aurobindo.”37 Vieri Tosatti’s lines are followed by a section written by his wife. The letter radiates a cordial warmth. It leaves the impression not only of two friends but of two composers meeting on equal footing. They also share interests in Eastern philosophy: Scelsi concerned himself intensively with Taoism and the teachings of Sri Aurobindo.38 Viewed against this background, Tosatti’s later statements that Scelsi had been “arrogant and presumptuous”39 and that the value of his music was “equal to zero”40 are the more surprising. Was the reason for this turnaround rooted only in bitterness as Tosatti was forgotten as a composer while Scelsi’s international fame was growing?
Besides additional letters—among them several from the French spectral composers Gérard Grisey and Tristan Murail—and the miscellaneous writings by Scelsi, the unpublished works of Scelsi are of particular interest. A series of early works, including those for large orchestra like Rapsodia romantic (1931) and Poema romano (1933), are completely unknown so far. Among the works from the later period, there are, first of all, many smaller solo pieces for piano (2 blues and Toccata, as well as numerous untitled pieces), viola, and double bass and duos for viola and cello (Bhuni), French horn, and double bass, as well as a piece for four male voices. The scores were partly written by Tosatti, partly by Filippini, and, as a result, they date from the time after 1950 and 1970, respectively.
But Scelsi’s estate also contains two larger works. The first one is an untitled instrumentation of four movements of the piano work Cinque incantesimi.41 Scelsi left out the only placid movement; the four others are arranged for winds, percussion, piano, choir, and electroacoustically amplified instruments, which are to be positioned apart from the orchestra. The beginning of the score (pages 1–8) was written by Tosatti, the rest (until page 83) by Filippini. In one catalog of works Scelsi designates this version as Incantesimi, in another one as 4 incantesimi per coro e orchestra.42 We can look forward with anticipation to the world premiere of this tempestuous and very virtuosic music, which the Westdeutsche Rundfunk has planned as part of Wittener Tage für Neue Kammermusik 2012.
The second work for a large orchestra is Kamakala.43 The Sanskrit term designates a Hindu love goddess who is said to personify desire. The headings of the three movements are Agitato, Sostenuto, and Molto mosso. The orchestra arrangement consists primarily of lower winds, four percussionists, and strings (without violins). This piece also appears in a handwritten list of works as well as a typescript that is based on it.44 The eighty-seven-page-long score written by Tosatti is based on improvisations on an ondioline that stylistically show a certain closeness to the cello works Triphon (1956) and Dithome (1957).45
Whether Scelsi intended to publish the posthumous works is uncertain. One could take the view that he authorized only those scores as “works” that he published with Ricordi, De Santis, Schirmer, and Salabert or that he self-published under “Edizioni dell’autore.” However, it is entirely possible that the Salabert publishing house did not want to or was not able to publish everything Scelsi offered. Ultimately the quality of the music, which has to manifest itself through performance, will be reserved for posterity.
