11

The Art of “Writing” Sound
Sandro Marrocu

He who thinks, then, that he has left behind him any art in writing and he who receives it in the belief that anything in writing will be clear and certain, would be an utterly simple person.
—Plato

In many ways, approaching the human and intellectual example of Giacinto Scelsi means, even in this day and age, to compare legend with reality—regarding both his poetic universe and his everyday life. It is undeniable that the Scelsian myth as l’homme du son, fascinating and rich in content, is essential for understanding Scelsi’s music. However, it is insufficient for formulating a proper exegesis. Instead, scientific research utilizing relevant documents is necessary to best dissolve preconceptions, thus separating fact and legend and placing myth into a logical historical context. In this sense the accessibility of the archives of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi in Rome represent the “zero hour” of research, which, over the past two years, has generated a great deal of attention to such documents, particularly those relating to sound, which can be finally consulted.1 These vital documents create a new understanding of Scelsi’s life and work, affording researchers the opportunity to completely reconstruct the development of some of the greatest musical masterpieces of the late twentieth century.
The examination of audiotapes and scores in these archives, with specific reference to the so-called “fase dell’ondiola,”2 reveals the parameters of Scelsi’s unique and much-discussed creative process that was divided into at least two, often three, essential phases. The first phase consisted of gathering recorded improvisations, a true poietic moment in which the composer would literally create the material to be molded. The second phase was a real postproduction endeavor, during which Scelsi reelaborated, with the help of two Revox tape recorders, the material he had previously collected and developed, editing it on tape as if it were electroacoustic prototypes of his compositions. Lastly, these prototypes were entrusted to professionals who arranged them for various types of instrumental or vocal ensembles.3 Such an ongoing process was executed in consultation with Scelsi, who, according to Riccardo Filippini’s recollection, selected those scores destined to become part of his oeuvre with great care. Sometimes he selected none, rarely all of them.4
After many years of controversy,5 which in reality has never entirely dissipated, Scelsi’s unique modus operandi—his creative synergy with his collaborators—still leaves many questions unanswered by the conscience of the Western world. A careful analysis of the sources is of utmost importance, recognizing the written scores, and not the audiotapes, as the medium through which Scelsi chose to commit to history the substance of his work. This is precisely the perspective I adopt in writing this essay. I will limit my focus to documents belonging to Scelsi’s creative period known as sola nota, and I will demonstrate, through the analysis of selected examples, how composing “sound” assumes multiple roles in Scelsi’s music.
AUDIOTAPES OR WRITTEN SCORES?
The relationship with notation was for Scelsi always problematic, to say the very least.6 For this reason, and given the singularity of his own aesthetic, it may sometimes seem necessary to doubt the fact that composing added value to his music. It is therefore possible to reconsider the audiotapes not so much as a mixed bag of notes to be developed with traditional criteria and instruments but, rather, as potential versions of the works. This is the case even though the composer never, in fact, claimed an audiotape to be his own independent work.7
On the other hand, writing is a phenomenon typical of Western classical tradition that has been refined in its musical semiography over the centuries in order to develop true sonic architectures rather than to explore the inner potential of sound. Moreover there are many individuals who have worked alongside Scelsi who remember how he regarded the written score as being totally inadequate to expressing the dictates of his creativity.8 It seems that Scelsi very often used recorded audiotapes, images, or verbal descriptions to convey the deep meaning of his music, as well as to explain ideas meant to guide an instrumentalist or singer in his or her search for the “right” sound.9
In addition, various factors within Scelsi’s compositions often intimate that he thought it was almost a mistake to have written down his works.10
This, however, is not surprising. It is ancient wisdom that writing is only a tool to make one remember something, which for those who “already know” has always been clear. The Platonic Prophecy of Ammon is a very poignant reminder that music is an allographic art,11 regardless of age or inscription. In fact, even if a concept is precise, it may never in itself reveal the atemporal substance inherent in art unless it is mediated by a tradition.12
Scelsi was most likely aware of these realities, but he employed writing as a means to giving his work an immanent format. And he was fully aware of what he was doing. Otherwise it would not be possible to explain his constant concern for the recording and preservation of his written scores or his strong desire to listen to his orchestral music “at least once, just once”13 in order to correct the errors and leave the paperwork “in order, which should be the duty of everyone.”14
The written score was for Scelsi a means to shedding light on his musical ideas and to becoming, as composer, part of a tradition he wanted to belong to, thus creating his own legacy. In addition to his “statue under the sand,”15 Scelsi will be remembered for his written scores, despite his predicament that his music had to remain hidden. However, one may argue that in order to achieve this he should never have written it down in the first place.16 Nevertheless writing down the music, or getting someone to do it—as we can say now without trepidation—was a necessary process.17 In any case, apart from the fruitless arguments closely related to the “Scelsi Case,” the essential thing to note is that Scelsi openly claimed ownership of his works, including more than one hundred titles of vocal, chamber, and orchestral music, which were all “written” down on paper.18 This, if you will, is logical. In fact, Scelsi found himself working in a cultural context that could hardly accept the validity of his unique compositional practices. Writing, therefore, assured the ethical dimension of his being a Western musician and gave his ideas a status of legitimacy.
In addition, for Scelsi writing was a precise aesthetic decision. He expressly pursued the written score as a format to record his output through means dictated by the “tradition of the sign” and the acoustic instrument at a time when he could have employed technology ab integro with a concentration on experimentation. In fact, it was during those years that, for example, electronics equipped composers with a new universe of undiscovered sounds, offering them the ideal tools with which to create extremely flexible combinatorial sounds derived from a tabula rasa perspective. Such was the new Koine so desired by the postwar avant-garde.19 However, Scelsi was not interested in mere experimentation.20 On the contrary, he poured a lot of content into his music, relating it to a specific imaginary sphere, nourished by mystical knowledge that was both Eastern and esoteric.21 This in turn led to an expressive intensity and “the feeling of great warmth” that pervaded his work.22 Through writing, Scelsi fully appropriated, from a Western point of view, the mysterious semantic area that reveals the deeper meaning of many of his most important compositions.
Finally, let us not forget that Scelsi was interested in “perceiving, receiving, and then demonstrating, with instruments or vocals,” a part of that “power of sound that is the basis of everything, that creates and often transforms people.”23 In this sense he approached composing in a mystical manner,24 more than as a musician, taking a perspective that makes express reference to the ritual roots of art, to writing as revelation of the Evam mayā śrutam.25 The well-known image of the “postman” to whom his music was entrusted26 reminds us, in fact, of the role of priests, who in ancient civilizations were responsible for the art of representing sacred things through all kinds of signs and symbols. It also suggests that, for Scelsi, musical semiography was the ideal way to encrypt the “message that has been received,” utilizing musical instruments, a choir, or an orchestra to express the spiritual messages that, in ancient times, mankind received with instinctive foresight.27
From this point of view there is no doubt that his music tells us about something that eludes nonexperts. Scelsi clearly states and warns us, for example, that the “true meaning of Xnoybis, or his string quartets, is little understood,”28 and, above all, that something should be searched for not in sound experimentation but in the evocation of what “is always the same story, if it can be called a story.”29 And who—in relation to his music—“will limit himself or herself to the acoustic issue or what can be considered to be pure instrumental research [has] not understood a damn thing.”30
IDEAS, TEXTS, ACTION
Scelsi’s most important scores are masterpieces of extraordinary complexity. The written reworking of ideas during their developmental stages was certainly a critical moment. In fact, the composer’s insights had to become a text that could be understood, performed, and then turned into action. By using audiotapes, Scelsi proposed a variety of models for developing the same idea; after such procedure had taken place, he would personally select those works he deemed appropriate to enter his official catalogue of compositions.31 Riccardo Filippini, for example, recalls how an audiotape could contain numerous components: improvisations at the ondioline, different arrays of sounds, and sounds obtained through the use of two model G Revox tape recorders. It was through these pastiches that Scelsi would “announce something new”:

After that, we (Vieri or I, or both) would write down on paper something understandable and playable whose result would be as close as possible to the recorded sound. Ultimately, it was Giacinto who decided what would become part of the repertoire. Sometimes nothing, rarely everything. Only in the case of large-scale compositions would the audiotape contain just one piece… and so we would work on that alone.32

Undoubtedly, the professional transcribers made a very significant contribution, which was inevitably greater the more complex the composition.33 In fact, the development of a work for voices, individual instruments, or small ensembles could be directly controlled by Scelsi and, perhaps, improved by him through a direct comparison with the sound effect, such as during the development of performances recorded with the players.34
However, the process would be different for those pieces whose complexity made it essentially impossible to conceive their development, notwithstanding a well-planned theoretical design of the instrumental textures, without any immediate acoustic feedback. For example, combinatorial sounds, sophisticated amalgam, the resonances contemplated in scores such as Konx-Om-Pax or Uaxuctum are the result of skillful sound structuring that shows an unquestionable mastery of orchestration.
It is precisely the most complex cases that raise the most questions. Indeed it is quite difficult to understand how Scelsi influenced the writing, and it is almost impossible to objectively reconstruct the multiplicity of steps that led to the final written score.
However, the evidence that has been gathered, as well as the analysis of the documents, leads, in essence, to the emergence of two possibilities:35
First, the audiotapes guided the transcribers during the ongoing process in consultation with Scelsi. In general terms, everything had to be transformed into something that was written down that, according to Riccardo Filippini’s recollections,

included background noises and the clicks of the tape recorder. In fact, when Vieri (or I) used to “explain” to Giacinto what was happening, for example at bar 48 (at this point there were violin tremolos while a singer was holding the note G and the bass tuba played something else…), he could say, “No, instead of the tuba I would prefer the notes to be played by a double bass.” So, we changed the score. Obviously the end result was always a virtual one. There has never been an orchestra to perform Scelsi’s scores when they were being developed; everything would run through his mind and/ or that of Vieri and we would… explain it in words. You should know, however, that for Scelsi it was fundamental when an audiotape was being used for the end result—i.e. the score—to be completed in a manner that ensured that the effect was as identical as possible to the original. This was so in general terms.… In these cases, we would not decide anything, unless we were faced with a “nonsensical” request, such as a violin that “must” play a C an octave below middle C. In this case there would be a discussion, because if he wanted this, it was not easy to convince him that it was not possible.36

Second, the contents of the audiotape and the requirements were a mere trace and the idea, at least technically, had to be developed ab integro. Again, Riccardo Filippini recalled that

sometimes the large pieces were developed starting from seemingly insignificant audiotapes. It was the imagination of the “transcribers” that turned these into sounds of cosmic entity.… The more Scelsi’s pieces are “grand” and “important,” the more the minds of those who “collaborated” with him were involved up to 99.9 percent. This was always the case for Giacinto’s entire musical production.37

This second point is hardly surprising. Simply, with the proper proportions Scelsi felt it was a logical extension of the role of the transcriber—the same method often used by performers. In fact, the ideas presented by Scelsi through different suggestions were very often literally developed by the players based on their respective and specific expressive potential. Consider, in this sense, the case study of Canti del Capricorno.38 What mattered was how much an idea worked out in the end. It is therefore possible to speculate that, at least in the case of important orchestral compositions, the musicians doing the arrangements would have to follow suit: to develop an idea on the basis of the suggestions they received using their technical skills to the utmost while fully respecting a precise expressive design.
EXAMPLES
To get a better picture of the development of Scelsi’s works, we will bring to the fore some examples specifically pertaining to two important compositions: “Pezzo sulla nota fa” from the cycle Quattro pezzi per orchestra (ciascuno su una nota), and the triptych Xnoybis for unaccompanied violin. In a nutshell, “Pezzo sulla nota fa” demonstrates the first of the above-mentioned known cases: “EVERYTHING” had to be transformed into writing, including the “background noise and the click of the audiotape.” This piece is also a wonderful example of writing as an aesthetic choice, “a forming architecture”39 able to direct the same sound project conceived on audiotape to an instrumental ensemble. The example of Xnoybis is attributable to the second scenario we previously discussed: in fact, it demonstrates how, in some cases, writing does not always mean transcribing or arranging an audiotape but, rather, developing the idea on paper following a defined expression. This example reveals how the “true meaning of Xnoybis”40 evolves, due to the similarity of sound determined by writing for instruments that can outline theoretical paths of meaning through the symbolic implications related to the notes and instruments in certain cultural systems.
Some of the examples that follow would be best understood after listening to the audiotapes. In addition, despite the value of writing in Scelsi’s music that we discuss in this essay, a keen understanding would also involve verbal discussion, because the sounds we are referring to are difficult to imagine without listening to them. Comparisons would only be truly meaningful if explained. In essence, in order to properly understand what we mean would require at this point careful listening to an example on CD or audio file. However, in the absence of adequate support in terms of sound, we will simply make a few key observations that illustrate the arguments set out above.41
SCRUPULOUS ADHERENCE AND IMAGINATIVE REELABORATION
We refer now to the sound collage that resulted in “Pezzo sulla nota fa” from Quattro pezzi per orchestra (ciascuno su una nota), composed in 1959.42 The level of adherence to the orchestral version arranged by Vieri Tosatti is really impressive, and, despite the rather overall sterility of the ondioline’s sound, listening to the audiotape gives us the impression of contemplating a true sketch of the coloristic masterpiece completed in 1959. In order to grasp the essential similarities, let us focus briefly on the structure of the piece using the enlightened analytical proposal of Giulio Castagnoli.43
The structure of the piece, as it generally occurs in Quattro pezzi per orchestra (ciascuno su una nota), stems from the dialectic between areas of movement and stasis.44 Each parameter of the sound creates movement and is subject to continuous melodic, rhythmic, and tonal variations that constantly negate a state of rest: for example, altering the frequency of the perpetual dynamic oscillation of the single sound is the embryonic stage of melodic movement. More generally, it is the extent of the variations in the sound parameters that determine the two possible movement stages: latent or explicit.
Example 11.1 summarizes the macrostructure of “Pezzo sulla nota fa”:45 there are four areas of latent movement marked I, II, III, and IV, interposed by three sections of explicit movement marked A, B, and C. Castagnoli46 created a semblance of symmetry for the piece based on the number of tactus, which in a certain way describe the distribution of the latent/explicit movement over time (see example 11.2). This way there are three different areas of alternating movement:

The first section, represented in example 11.3a, groups together the first cycle of latent/explicit movement.
Example 11.3b groups together the middle area of latent/explicit/latent movement characterized by a slight rhythmic and melodic movement, with the exceptions of bongos and octave of the melody on the bass clarinet.
Example 11.3c groups together the final cycle of explicit/latent movement. It takes up the same orchestral material and treatment method used in the α area.



The same articulation can be found in the ondioline collage. It was therefore possible to make an extensive comparison between the audiotape and written score for each of the three sections represented here in examples 11.3a–c. Regarding sections in examples 11.3a and 11.3b, there is a formal and specific adherence, which is quite majestic, in the reelaboration for orchestra of the ondioline sound. In these areas any details on the audiotape are exactly the same as in the written score. The details of the collage were certainly the inspiration for developing the structural sound characteristics of the piece, able to describe the transformation of the sound and draw the evocative third dimension from it in color.47 In particular, the technical solutions adopted to translate area α, first cycle of latent/explicit movement, became material to be decomposed and recomposed in order to create cross-references between different sections of the piece.

For the section in example 11.3c, adherence was, instead, quite partial. In fact, here the audiotape is a much more generic reference point. More than a faithful transcription, the written score appears to have been developed on a theoretical basis—starting from the material that was previously produced—to make the music formally consistent. In particular, it includes much of the material from the α section, which is missing on the γ area of the audiotape, redistributed among various phases of recording.

In short, when an audiotape was used the reworking could be formal, but the tone/sound result had to produce an effect as far as possible identical to the original.48 Indeed, by extending the same type of comparison outlined in example 11.3 to other ondioline traces for “Pezzo sulla nota fa,” we can obtain results similar to those described above.

For example, during his presentation at the Suono nel Suono conference (Rome 2010) Jaecker illustrated the case of Dithome for unaccompanied cello, stating that it was “admirable how Vieri Tosatti was able to write this fierce and virtuous piece.” Moreover, during our research we were able to find the same adherence shown in regard to “Pezzo sulla nota fa” by making a similar comparison for Trio à cordes.
SOUND DISCREPANCIES
However, in the course of this study a rather interesting exception was found on Trio’s audiotape: in fact, it contains a track of ondioline belonging to the second movement of Xnoybis,49 a composition whose particular sound differs radically from that of the “pastiche.” It was therefore a case of quite partial sound adherence.
It is curious to observe how this track is the reverse of that used by Scelsi in the Revox, which led to the extremely faithful transcription of the third movement of Trio à cordes mapped out in example 11.4. The two ondioline collages are therefore palindromes, as indeed the two scores are perfectly symmetrical,50 suggesting that the reverse was made starting from Trio’s written score and not from the audiotape. In fact, the writing for Trio à cordes was reworked for solo violin by keeping the three-staves scoring solution, which, of course, allowed a precise understanding of the sound of each string but led to a substantial amplified inhomogeneity in the tone, above all by the use of scordatura and metal mutes. The result was the sharp sound that is characteristic of the Xnoybis triptych, which is so distant from the sweet and mellow tone of the ondioline tape recordings in its medium- to high-register sounds. If we consider the proverbial attention to detail that Scelsi applied when searching for the “right” sound,51 this cannot be considered to be mere chance but rather the result of the composer’s specific intent. We can be certain of one thing, though: it was not a mere experiment as Scelsi revealed to us.52 More generally, he often underlined how, in all his music, the search for the right sound was always related to an expression of intent.53 For example, the metal mutes, which so heavily affect the tone of the violin in Xnoybis, are not “a refinement of the music but a necessity,”54 because the right sound is never “just an acoustic phenomenon but much more than that!”55
We have reason to suppose that all of the Xnoybis triptych is a particularly good example of how Scelsi could rely on a precise instrument, as well as the written composition/de-composition of sound, to signify something and, above all, to demonstrate the “power of sound that is at the basis of everything, which creates and often transforms people.”56

XNOYBIS : THE SONIC WRITING OF A SYMBOL
Scelsi considered Xnoybis to be a “small bomb… very small but very powerful,” a piece “more explosive than Quattro pezzi per orchestra and many others put together.”57 This triptych was presented by Alain Danielou at the Semaines musicales internationales de Paris, and the Concert Orient-Occident, on November 11, 1964. In that context, a meeting between East and West, the “true meaning of Xnoybis”—clarified by Scelsi as being related to the knowledge of initiation—became clear to all.58 It is likely that the mysterious “sense” the composer referred to was captured by the fine writing for violin. Therefore, let us explain first of all certain essential characteristics of this music.
Xnoybis is formed by three short pieces, consisting of seventy-nine, seventy-seven, and sixty-one measures, respectively, that are very difficult to grasp at first hearing, despite their profound evocativeness. From a technical point of view,59 we see the very elaboration of sound: in all three pieces, the nota sola is almost never a pure entity. It is, in fact, systematically juxtaposed to its upper or lower quarter tones, or to the adjacent halftones. Thus it identifies not so much a specific frequency but a real frequency domain, an area of sound capable of changing in a flexible manner, to contract and expand continuously, to interact with different high levels of frequencies. The search for the “right” sound, and its expansion/contraction between the various domains, is conducted through the use of a number of notation staves. This allows us to pinpoint a certain sound from a given string, also making it possible to fade and diversifying the playing, tone, dynamics, and type of vibrato, as well as the intonation, in a way that had never been tried before. In order to explain this more clearly I have used certain elements from the work’s structure that are particularly exemplary.
First of all, let me clarify the concept of domain. Using terminology borrowed from mathematics, we consider what is “near” a note, the very apparent position of the frequencies adjacent to it and which have a “tendency” toward it. Therefore let us observe the opening notes of the first part of Xnoybis (see example 11.5): The first domain is that of the note E, the first pure note from which the movement originates. It expands or contracts, reaching the upper and lower adjacent frequencies. Then we focus on how the movement of the sound is inserted within this domain. The single note, vibrated from the outset, instantly begins to slip “within” itself: in fact, the vibrated E immediately overlaps with D-sharp. The two juxtaposed sounds “react” by emanating harmonics and sound flutters before merging to create a continuum of frequencies in constant metamorphosis, as a result of the continually varying parameters that affect the individual frequencies.
Let us observe, again in example 11.5, at measure 8, how the domain of the note E is explored in its depth through the return of D-sharp to E4 on string II, while the vibrated E sounds on string III. This way, it produces, as a sensation of the physical crossing of the sound it holds, the vibrated E and an allusion to the lifting within the note E (represented by D-sharp) toward its surface and a subsequent redescent into its depths (see example 11.5, measure 9). The subsequent measures (see example 11. 6) show the rhythmic and melodic pulsations that prepare the sound to migrate toward another tone of the domain, in other words the E4-/E4+ overlaps to measure 19.


This development is announced by measures 11 to 12, animated by many combined melodic and rhythmic motions. The melodic motion culminates in the expansion of the sound of the first harmonic of D-sharp to measure 14 on string I, within which subsequent micromelody contractions resonate, carried out on the II and III strings. The sound will once again condense back in a tone in the domain: the mix of E4–/E4+of measure 19.
The “sound domains” behave like patches of color on a palette. They are colors to be mixed in order to obtain all possible tones and scales from a similar “pure” frequency. Bear in mind that, in this way, the sound never crystallizes: no domain is ever at a state of rest. Rather, it is the intermediate stage of a continuous evolution. This development is affected by the interchange of the domains and defines the overall structure of the piece.
Therefore let us observe the first real settlement of the sound on a new level of purity, occurring at measure 28 on E4 for the duration of two measures, seen in example 11.7. This is a moment of true relaxation, and yet what we have stated above can be immediately observed: a state of stillness is in any case denied. In fact, the sound is characterized by an ample vibrato, and the only two nonvibrato beats, at measure 30, on string III, increase in dynamism, an embryonic stage of melodic movement.
The entire piece proceeds in a similar manner in subsequent stages of evolving sound: at measures 38 to 39 the domain moves to F4–, which will contract and expand again through overlaps with the E and F4+, until it reaches F-sharp on measure 50. This will be the new domain, from which the piece will proceed toward its conclusion, the F-natural. Example 11.8 summarizes the total interchange, reduced to a macrostructure, animated by the tension of the domain of the E towards that of F. As far as the level of microstructural composition is concerned, we have already been able to observe how the continuous parametric variations determine the perpetual movement of the Klang. Every single note, or microtonal cluster, is a sound-transition area to be explored in depth.


In this regard, there was a curious anecdote, mentioned by Vieri Tosatti and confirmed by Riccardo Filippini, about Scelsi’s demand that they create a glissando upon—or within—a single note. It is impossible to reconstruct exactly what the composer intended with this, if you will, absurd request. However, it is a fact that a microstructural solution is systematically found across the Xnoybis triptych. Such a solution is able to at least allude to a glissando executed within the surface of a note. This is the same solution already observed with regard to measure 8 and that, in any case, deserves a brief detailed analysis.
Let us consider another example taken again from the first piece, this time example 11.9, measures 35 through 38. We can see how, at measure 35, on string II, the sound settles on the note F4–. The sound, set in movement by the use of wide vibrato, undergoes an immediate tone change, as highlighted at measure 36, passing to string III and undergoing dynamic discontinuity, from piano to mezzopiano. The continuity of the flow is in any case guaranteed by the continuation of F4– on string II, while the micromelody movement continues on E-nature on string III: here the dynamic sound changes once more and begins to vibrate for the duration of the measure. In conjunction with this vibrato, the playing passage from F4 onto D-sharp4+ occurs on string II. This way, what occurs is a true “transfer” of the E vibrato caused by F4–, which slides onto D-sharp4+. The tension created by the resonation of the three adjacent frequencies (D-sharp4+/E/F4–) is solved precisely by sliding back to D-sharp4+ from the inside up to the “surface” of E to measure 37, always on string II. The result consists of a sinuous, very evocative, sound effect that clearly gives the idea of the spatial depth that characterizes the sound and that, at this point, allows a physical crossing through an allusion to the glissando on a single note, executed within the third dimension of the sound.60

More generally, the above issue could be extended to the whole triptych. In fact, in pieces II and III we find the same arrangements in the sound’s motion, as well as the same way of structuring the form through the continuous migration of the frequency domains. Therefore, we propose a simple summary of the macrostructures of the three pieces. We have seen how the first piece appears diminishable by an alteration of the sound from the domain of E to that of F, as seen in example 11.8. In the same way, the second piece in example 11.10 is expressed as a tension that brings the domain of D-sharp/D4+ to that of B. The third piece, like the first, once again has the tension at the domain of F, but this time starting from the domain of D, as illustrated in example 11.11.


THE TRUE MEANING OF XNOYBIS
If indeed the “true meaning of Xnoybis” had been hidden by this complex writing structure, it should be possible to find a sound in the overall structure that refers, for example, to its “progressive release,”61 to catharsis, to the ascension of energy toward the spirit, since this seems to be the theme evoked by the title/subtitle combination.62
It is certainly very difficult to find evidence of objective musical references that demonstrate how this piece can be viewed, for example, because it evokes the “right” sound for extolling a catharsis. This is especially the case if we consider the amount of knowledge of initiation that Scelsi was referring to,63 which multiply the potential symbolic interpretations, moreover creating a very high risk for overinterpretation. However, let us try to reason about Xnoybis, commencing from certain elementary considerations related to Scelsi’s way of thinking.
First, it is likely that “the true meaning of Xnoybis”64 is somehow related to what the mystical symbol bearing the same name evokes;65 therefore, in this music we should be able to find a reference to the “transcendental, supernatural, or spiritual value of sacrifice.”66 This is because the idea of rebirth, evoked by the domain of the zodiac sign of Leo,67 has always meant a reference to sacrifice in all cultural systems. In particular, in the Vedanta philosophical system “the term that technically means sacrifice is friction,”68 because everything originates, transmutes, and is then reborn from friction. For this reason, the arches are among the primary symbols of sound that create.69 Not only: in mystery cults, in order to evoke transmutation, sacrifices were offered to the deities, as well as initiation sounds to evoke the “ritual friction with which fire would be reborn in the early spring, being the fire that is locked inside the… wood, stone, or metal.”70 Thus, bearing in mind Scelsi’s mystical-music thinking,71 the meaning of Xnoybis might be the search for a sound analogy of that sacrifice/friction that “allows the universe to continue to exist”72 and that is the basis of the transmutation evoked by the domain.73 This is simply a hypothetical guess. However, if indeed this is the substance of Xnoybis, it is only the written score that reveals and keeps it intact for at least two reasons.
First, it is the writing that determines and prescribes the type of sound. It works because it is an alchemical formula, a cypher needed to re-create the “right” sound: opaque, bitter, metallic, and, as such, capable of evoking “the sound that produced life,” represented by ancient symbols of sound “like a high-pitched noise,… a metallic sound.”74 Moreover, the writing of this sound is for the violin, prepared ad hoc by Scelsi to express the right “vibration of a bowstring,”75 which is among the “most ancient symbols of materials of the sound that creates.”76 Above all, in mystery cults a musical bow that is rubbed is a “symbol of the amalgam life/death.”77
The second reason is that writing, as we have seen previously, traces the evolution of the sound continuum through domains, thus creating a further analogy with the myth of the primal sound: shrill and “metallic, which, little by little, was developed by the creative process into well-defined sound syllables and musical notes.”78 In addition, the path traced by this writing of sound can take on meanings that are very risky to decipher but that can be hypothesized on the basis of the symbolic implications of the notes in certain cultural systems. Therefore, it could be a matter of coincidence: it is at the very least extraordinary to observe how the notes F and B, conclusive sound domains for the three pieces of Xnoybis, are the sounds that, in Sangita Ratnakara, correspond to the zodiac signs of Leo and Pisces, respectively,79 associated in many cultural systems with the Spirit, including the Jewish and early Christian faiths.80
The hypothesis of seeing true semantic units in the sound nuclei of the triptych becomes even more striking if we consider that the metamorphosis of sound begins, for each of the three pieces, respectively, from the notes E, D-sharp, and D. These notes are located in that octave area in which, according to the Science of the Spirit, “in human beings the most physical aspect comes into play before anything else.”81
According to occult knowledge, the human body expresses itself between the notes C and E: the physical body, the etheric body, and the astral body.82 This mysterious statement refers to knowledge that is impossible to penetrate through traditional musicology. For these reasons it may appear overly imaginative to regard Xnoybis in these terms. However, according to a study conducted by Schweizer in 2008, “the initiatory experience is a foundation of Scelsi’s style of writing,”83 something the composer himself revealed on many occasions, such as in relation to Xnoybis or the four quartets.84 In this sense, Schweizer’s study of the third quartet85 is revealing in its attempt to “interpret the compositional scheme, thanks to the semantic implications related to it,”86 founding, above all, its considerations on the basis of esoteric knowledge and the “development of Scelsi under Steiner’s influence.”87
SCORE WRITINGS: FOUR FUNCTIONS IN RELATION TO ETHICS AND AESTHETICS
The examples produced thus far, the latter one in particular, allow us to conclude that score writing is a quintessential aspect of Scelsi’s music. It performs multiple functions and works on several levels concurrently. A first function, strictly technical and linguistic, consisted of score writing carried out through sound design, the development of ideas, and the identification of solutions to translate the audiotapes into possible notation for performing content. At this level, the writing worked, in a certain sense, through a “forming architecture”88 that consisted of creating an instrument for ordering the sign and identifying the essential units of a grammar and a very personal language.89
A second function is of an aesthetic nature: in fact, the concept mediates a result that could be called “acoustic,” allowing it to be experienced through traditional sound solutions interpreted by working on the audiotape.
Then there is a third function, which could be defined as ethical, and which integrates the previous two: score writing guaranteed legitimacy to Scelsi’s music within a specific culture. In this way the composer was able to fulfill his mission by placing the urges of his inspiration within society through musical instruments and a shared format: the written score.
Finally, as we have seen in the case of Xnoybis—or, for example, as noted by Schweizer90—with regard to String Quartet no. 3, it is very likely that there is another function in which musical notation acts as a symbol of some sort of alchemical formula, able to “re-create the arcane sound in the material, the sound in itself.”91 This final function could be defined as analogical/symbolic. In fact, the score not only describes the movement of the sound and prescribes its tonal qualities—thereby creating analogies and highly evocative atmospheres and sensations—but probably also uses the notes as cultural stratifications of the sound and, as such, as potential vehicles of extramusical meanings.
ARTIFEX IN WORKS: COOPERATIVE, DIRECTING, AND FABRICATION
I believe that score writing, despite the many limitations associated with it, is the necessary format to express Scelsi’s music. Thus we can consider his written scores as his method for writing sound, his means of capturing a creative process. In other words, the relationship of his audiotapes to their arrangement does not completely define his music. This proposition requires an entirely new way of assessing the role of “cooperation” in music,92 since it is certain that Scelsi developed his ideas independently and it is true that, in order for them to be developed, they required a substantial contribution from others, at least on a purely technical and notational level.93
In this sense, Vieri Tosatti was Scelsi’s craftsman of choice, the demiurge who, better than anyone else, knew how to give his “ideas” a format that made sense. Their relationship was a sort of “Faustian bargain”94 or, perhaps, just as Scelsi stated, a karmic bond.95 What is certain, though, is that theirs was an extremely fruitful artistic partnership. That the special synergy was due precisely to this bond96 is a theory that is both evocative and easily proven. It would, in fact, allow us to understand Vieri Tosatti’s positive role in an entirely new way, as well as his unique ability to understand Scelsi’s “mystical sound” and to turn his trade into a sort of maieutics.
Over and above the sterile disputes generated by the “Scelsi Case,” which is totally irrelevant from an aesthetic point of view,97 the statements made by persons who attest to the friendship between the two are very significant, as well as the strong personal involvement of Tosatti in Scelsi’s work, especially in the 1960s.98 The relationship was certainly in large part financial. However, a letter found during my research at Tosatti’s home99 suggests that, at least for a time, there was actually a different bond on another level between the two musicians.100
Vieri Tosatti, the desecrator, the enfant terrible, participated with Scelsi in meetings held by Lanza del Vasto and probably belonged, along with the latter, to some mystical group. The form and content of a letter dated June 18, 1963,101 leaves little room for doubt and is a very poignant testimony of what was strictly a friendship and professional relationship: “today I will certainly complete the sketch of the second piece, and I will start the first one.” But that is not all. In short, as Tosatti’s widow stated, “there was a plot,”102 and the result was the extraordinary score writing that, even today, preserves the unique mystique of the Sound of Giacinto Scelsi.
In the century in which the radical division of labor determined the status quo of society and the division of expertise characterized much of the best artistic production, such as in cinema or theater, there is now nothing unusual in legitimizing this creative synergy, this cooperation, as potential evolution, even in music, of Western individualism.
What matters is the legacy, the work. In this sense, Scelsi worked as a great director of “sound.” He consciously devised music that was sometimes very complex and always innovative. In coordinating its development through the management of different skill levels, as shown both by the transcribers and players, he poured all his personal beliefs and his very personal vision of reality into it, giving his work a style that is unmistakable, to say the least.
Scelsi’s music is, at all levels, a summary of many things, an extraordinary directorial copyright. We must not be frightened of considering it just as he gave it to us as allographic art.103 Scelsi’s music was not the product of pure experimentation, the result of laboratory research, or—even less—an autograph on a recording medium. On the contrary, it was often through this medium that it was developed in formats representing a cyclical return to life, entrusted to the voice or musical instruments, full of history and meanings.
In this sense, l’homme du son, who was a precursor, a de-composer,104 a great director, or just a “postman,” fully inserts himself in a dialectic ever capable of perpetuating a cycle that places all innovations within a tradition. And if his modus operandi may appear controversial, perhaps it is because in music, as always, “tradition dwells in a state of contradiction.”105

21. I refer particularly to theosophy and anthroposophy. Indeed, it was Scelsi himself who recounted how Rudolph Steiner generally influenced his way of thinking (see Scelsi, Il Sogno 101, 69–71, 265). However, it was probably Kohler who introduced him to theosophy and anthroposophy (see Christine Anderson, “Immaginare l’Oriente: Elementi tibetani nella musica di Giacinto Scelsi degli anni Sessanta,” in Tortora, ed., Giacinto Scelsi nel centenario della nascita, 145). This theme is important enough to deserve in-depth investigation.
That said, there are some almost immediate similarities between the anthroposophical thought and Scelsi’s aesthetic, particularly with regard to the conception of music and sound. In fact, the links between Scelsi’s ideas and the cycles of lectures delivered by Steiner in Cologne, Berlin, Leipzig, and Aberystwyth in 1906, 1920, and 1921 were more explicit than ever. The Austrian thinker theorized about certain musical problems in the light of science and spirit, especially in the Dornach lecture of 1920 (see Rudolph Steiner, L’iniziazione: Come si conseguono conoscenze dei mondi superiori? [Milan: Antroposofica, 2003], 45–57). Steiner clearly outlined the entire Scelsi nota sola aesthetic. In this lecture Steiner expressed the need to examine sound in depth (Ibid., 47–48).
In Scelsi we also find the idea, again proposed by Steiner, that the musicians of the past were timid precursors of a future in which the highest possible spiritual dimensions would be reached through sound and the rapport with transcendental issues would be mediated (Scelsi, Il sogno 101). In fact, musicians would be responsible for linking human and divine plans and would participate in the evolution of humanity (Ibid., 19). The Cologne and Berlin lectures (Ibid., 8) also touched on the theory that musicians would draw from the Devachanic dimension—that is, the dream dimension. With specific reference to Scelsi, in this sense perhaps nothing can be more explanatory than the visionary dimension that partly characterizes all of Sogno 101: Il ritorno—in particular the pages where Scelsi communicates in drawings his idea of music as a pure expression of substance, sound in its vital power as atemporal synergy of the music of the world (Ibid., 454).

62. Xnoybis, the ability of energy to ascend to the spirit. “Xnoybis” is an ancient alchemical symbol, often depicted on Gnostic gems (see Attilio Mastrocinque, ed., Bollettino di Numismatica, Monografia, 8.2.I [Rome: Istituto Poligrafico e Zecca dello Stato, 2003]), which became a symbol of the transmutation of a physical principle into spiritual entity, as well as enlightenment. Its origin is to be found in the magical culture of ancient Egypt (see Paolo Vitellozzi, Lo Zodiaco, Sopraintendenza per i Beni Archeologici dell’Umbria, 2009, online at www.archeopg.arti.beniculturali.it/getFile.php?id=414; and Jack Lindsay, The Origins of Alchemy in Graeco-Roman Egypt [London: Muller, 1970], and its Italian translation, Le origini dell’alchimia nell’Egitto greco-romano [Rome: Mediterranee, 2001]).
It was so dear to Scelsi (Scelsi, Il sogno 101) that Xnoybis was the image of the first zodiac domain of the Leo star sign: a snake’s body with a lion’s head, sometimes inscribed in a solar circle. It was initially associated with the flooding of the Nile that had started under his own astral domain. The symbol was later assimilated by the solar deity, thereby becoming a mystical symbol, the main part of the theological reflection of various doctrines (Vitellozzi, Lo Zodiaco) and, consequently, a transcultural image (Attilio Mastrocinque, “Un’altra immagine transculturale: Chnoubis,” 391–98, in Images et religion dans l’antiquité gréco-romaine: Actes du colloque de Rome, ed. S. Estienne et al. [Naples: Centre Jean Bérard, 2008], 391–98).
For example, the Gnostics saw the Father in the lion’s head and the Word made flesh in the body of the snake. In addition, “this symbol is identical to another that, according to Iamblichus and Champollion, was referred to as the ‘First of the Celestial gods,’ the god Hermes—or Mercury to the Greeks—the God to whom Hermes Trismegistus attributed the invention of magic and the first initiation of mankind to this art. Furthermore, Mercury is Buddha, Wisdom, Enlightenment, or the ‘Awakening’ in the Divine Science” (see Helena Petrovna Blavatsky, The Secret Doctrine: The Synthesis of Science, Religion and Philosophy [London: The Theosophical Publishing Society, 1888], and its Italian translation, La dottrina segreta: Sintesi della scienza, della religione, della filosofia, vol. 1 of Cosmogenesi, 3rd ed. [Rome: Istituto Cintamani, 2007]).
More generally, in the Judeo-Christian culture the Lion “was, like the Sun, a fragment of divine fire” (see Mario Bacchiega, I mostri dell’Apocalisse [Rome: Mediterranee, 1982]). And the same iconography associated Christ, who had the power to rise to his Father, with the solar image of the lion (Apocalypse 5:5), which is “light and knowledge” (Bacchiega, I mostri dell’Apocalisse). For such a reason, because it is a symbol of the Revelation, the task of opening the Book and its seven seals was entrusted to him (Genesis 49:9; Isaiah 11:1–10). It is certainly very difficult to even attempt a reconstruction of syncretism, which has led to the assimilation of the idea of transmutation of spiritual matter into the body of the snake, the head of the lion, and the solar circle. There are supposed to be many possible paths, although they are almost impossible to decipher in precise terms, since they lose themselves in the meanderings of popular imagination (Lindsay, The Origins of Alchemy).

97. In overall terms, this issue has led to a distortion of Tosatti’s real intentions; despite highly polemical tones, he never claimed authorship of Scelsi’s works. He had even less of a desire to attack Scelsi as a man. This point deserves specific debate. In any case, to summarize, it is worth remembering that Tosatti’s statements were about the more general mystification, which he believed was represented by the label “new music,” as well as experimental approaches (see Zaccagnini, ed., “Il compositore è una cooperativa?”; see also Tosatti, “Giacinto Scelsi: C’est moi”).
As confirmation, there was also a RAI program Tosatti recorded on January 10, 1989, the Radio Svizzera Italiana program recorded on February 6, 1989, and the radio interview on the “Scelsi Case” conducted by A. Vannucchi, again in February 1989. More generally, Tosatti saw in avant-garde aesthetics an emblem of the decay of musical language. This clearly emerges from his epistolary and from some of his significant writings (see Vieri Tosatti, Capitoli scompagni [Monte Caminetto and Rome: E.O.T, 1981]).
98. The friendship between the two is proven by the tone of certain letters and dedications Scelsi wrote found in Tosatti’s archive (see note 35) but in particular by the testimony of Tosatti’s widow, Valeria Ravot, who defined the relationship between Scelsi and Tosatti in the early 1960s as “above all friendship. I can’t remember how it started, but I know I have always known Giacinto.” On her husband’s involvement in that work, Ravot stated that her husband

took on the task very seriously and in a professional manner. But had he been asked why he did it, he would have dismissed as “all games, nonsense.” Never would he have admitted that there was something attractive to him in that music. For him it was literally “nonmusic.” It was repugnant to him, and yet I think he was attracted to it, and he put his heart and soul in it, all his skills, burning the midnight oil as if it [were] his own work. It was intriguing. The contacts were very intense, a number of times a week, sometimes in Via Mazzini, sometimes in Via S. Teodoro, but normally in Via S. Teodoro.… Vieri coped with it on a rational level, saying that he wanted to provoke, to demonstrate the mystification of the avant-garde before the institutions, but it was a mask. If it was only about that, with his character he would have mocked it all from the beginning.… It may seem incredible, but Vieri respected Scelsi a lot. I never saw him throw papers onto the floor as I have sometimes heard. He would dispute it in private, of course, he would talk badly about it as he always did, he would say that those ideas disgusted him, were a hoax… but then… when conducting works by Scelsi he would do it scrupulously, and would pay attention to the slightest detail. He cared about it. I was often present at rehearsals, and he was extremely demanding.

99. Here is the text of the letter:

Rome, Tuesday, June 18, 1963
My dear friend,
The meeting the other day was held unexpectedly with order and attention. There were no persons from a certain group present… doubt, but I am not sure that this is the reason for the betterment of others. We must now proceed with extreme caution, especially as Marisa has learned of the intention, by undesirable external, to intervene in force with the group, for unclear purposes, which are not in line with ours. Marisa has found this out, and not only in a subtle way, but from direct news on a physical level. If this alarm turns out to be founded we will proclaim a state of emergency: the door will be closed to all outsiders, and we will interrupt all our relationships with the group, and inform Lanza of it. However, let’s hope it doesn’t reach that stage. Apart from this alarm, the visit yesterday to Marisa was extremely satisfactory. Today I will certainly finish the trace of the second piece, and I’ll start the trace of the first one. Valerie and I think about you a lot, and we hope your trip will be beneficial in terms of peace and energy. We would be very happy to hear from you.
Warm greetings to Francoise, and a strong embrace from your (Vieri)

Foreword
Giacinto Scelsi (1905–1988) assumed a proper place in the musical scene of his country of birth only after his death, a circumstance that sometimes marks the destiny of those great utopians and artists far ahead of their time.
Notwithstanding his deep Italian roots, his interests, friendships, and frequent travels projected him onto a culturally international panorama where he was able to establish the foundations of his own peculiar creative journey.
Since his youth Scelsi had been animated by an intellectual curiosity that prompted him to explore deeply the traditions of the “Old World,” comparing them to those of “other” cultures—from Mediterranean culture to African rhythms, from the study and practice of yoga to the chants of Tibetan monks and the stories of Japanese folklore.
Among his many interests, standing front and center was his attachment to the New World experience. The “Italian Ives”—as Morton Feldman dubbed him—was passionate about the figurative arts. In fact, between the years 1956 and 1961 Scelsi became a strong promoter of American artists like Jackson Pollock, who at that time was virtually unknown in Rome.
The exhibits organized by the Rome–New York Art Foundation—an association Scelsi created with his companion, the American Frances McCann—became in those years a fervid meeting place. It was around that time his friendship emerged with John Cage, who went on to become his and McCann’s frequent foundation guest. It was hardly coincidence that led Cage to compare the sometimes-extreme character of Scelsi’s music and sonic experiments to the abstract pictorial experience of Mark Tobey’s “white writing.”
With this publication, for the first time the Western side of the Atlantic is presented some of the research conducted on the figure and works of Giacinto Scelsi in a systematic and organic fashion. The volume’s range spans the early debates on Scelsi’s authorship of his compositions (see, for instance, the roundtable discussion organized by Guido Zaccagnini) to the latest research based on primary sources now available in the historical archives at the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi, as represented in the chapters written by Friedrich Jaecker, Alessandra Carlotta Pellegrini, and Sandro Marrocu.
This volume constitutes the first significant introduction of the figure of Giacinto Scelsi in the cultural context from which he emerged and with which he showed such profound affinity. Music as Dream begins to fill an expansive gap in English-language literature on this remarkable composer, and we hope that this book is only the first of many such publications.
Nicola Sani, composer and president
  of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi








Introduction
Luminous brow. Penetrating gaze. Serious, slightly inquisitive expression. Such are the attributes radiating from an old photograph of Giacinto Scelsi (1905–1988). That photo, reproduced inside the pocket score of Scelsi’s Quartetto per archi (1944),1 has become the composer’s “official” portrait, often adorning published volumes, periodicals, concert programs, Web pages… and this very book. Scelsi’s photograph is of a gentleman in his early forties who looks enigmatically mature for all his youthfulness, and yet youthful in his maturity.
For various reasons, Scelsi forbade the public release of other images of himself, perhaps with the intention of preserving an impression of a never-fading, smiling, dignified, savvy younger person. Yet Scelsi did age, his life unfolding through the twentieth century, occupying an extended chronological arch running parallel to his creative season.
The art of sound was a constant in Scelsi’s life, from the notes obsessively pounded on the piano during his infancy to the most daring experimentations with the ondio-line in his old age, “painting” a global soundscape alongside the artistic experiences of his time, which Scelsi always observed with curiosity, interest, and detachment. Ultimately that soundscape became the composer’s peculiar artistic itinerary that turned into a journey of disconcerting coherence.
In Art and Knowledge—a transcription of an impromptu conversation that took place over the course of 1953 and 1954, but which didn’t appear in print until 1982—Scelsi pondered the meaning of the artistic creative faculty. He wrote, “it is the faculty of arresting motion, crystallizing an instant of durée, to extract it oneself—and of projecting this instant by an effort of the entire being in a verbal, sonorous, or plastic medium.”2 It is to this conceptual universe that his entire musical, literary, and artistic oeuvre can be traced. In contemplating that “instant of durée” one understands Scelsi’s reluctance to call himself a composer, preferring instead the epithet “mailman” or “decomposer,” as Tristan Murail suggested years later.3
But even for a Scelsi eternally young, mature, and old there existed an inexorable sequencing of before-during-after, perhaps a simple consequence of cause and effect, or a tediously recurrent question of fact-checking, scattered things, work, and encounters. One thing is clear, though—that Scelsi was meticulous in preserving vast arrays of information, as evidenced by the five hundred–plus pages of his autobiography, a formidable undertaking, notwithstanding its lack of accuracy regarding chronological references, people, consequences, and correspondences.
Scelsi the utopist, the visionary, the provocateur, the pioneer, the rebel to any kind of systematization, ultimately played a prank on posterity, as it were, a further coup of his proverbial irony in leaving behind a huge mound of tangled-up audiotapes, used to the last fraction of an inch and at the brink of disintegration, which he kept in mismatched containers. To further complicate the puzzle, Scelsi also left an avalanche of papers: bound scores never published, loose sheets that found their way between the leaves of well-known works, countless postcards, piles of literary texts, masses of photographs, and myriad drawings.
According to testimonials provided by intimate friends, Scelsi attached no importance to dating his written compositions in an orderly fashion, this because he attributed a different function to “writing” than one would normally. This, thus, prompts a flurry of questions: But why play such a prank at the expense of future generations? Why is there so much material left in unmarked closets and drawers despite so many voyages around the world and the inevitable loss of personal belongings, relocations, scores given to friends as gifts, audiotapes entrusted to performers? Such vexing questions have been asked many times, and convincing answers continue to be sought. But the truth is that the key to understanding resides in knowledge and that in order to acquire knowledge one must rely on perseverance. So, if one is interested in finding out the truth, why not try? Ultimately Scelsi himself offered a possible solution to the problem when in the year before his death he established a foundation in memory of his sister—the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi—whose scope is to put order to chaos through the creation of a scientific archive for the preservation and sharing of his oeuvre.
Let us contemplate once again this young-mature-old effigy of Scelsi and imagine whether that twine of tapes and those mounds of folders can reveal the who, what, when, where, why, and how of his existence. (But perhaps it would be best for us to skip over the when, because answering such temporal questions, as we well know, is always a delicate matter in the case of musicians.)
Count Giacinto Scelsi d’Ayala Valva (1905–1988) was a wealthy aristocrat endowed with a profound and ever-daring musical intuition. He received no formal schooling or traditional training. According to the little Scelsi shared about his personal life, from childhood he was tutored in the sciences, humanities, and, as he reported, chess and fencing. His father, Guido Scelsi, was a naval officer from a distinguished Sicilian family, his mother, Donna Giovanna d’Ayala Valva, a noble-woman from an old and well-endowed southern Italian dynasty—from whom Giacinto inherited the title of count and a fortune that allowed him to live comfortably.
The young Scelsi manifested a musical intuition from an early age, as he apparently spent hours pounding notes and chords on the piano. Eventually he acquired considerable skills as a piano player and as a budding socialite was in demand among the bel mondo characters lingering between Capri and the French Riviera.
Was Giacinto Scelsi a self-taught amateur musician? Yes, he was, but not in the derogatory sense generally associated with such qualifications. He was a sort of nobile musico dilettante, a poet, an artist, and man of letters of a bygone era, a little like Don Carlo Gesualdo, Prince of Venosa (c. 1560–1613), to name one among those fortunate (or unfortunate?) musicians not obliged to make a living in the practice of their art.
Scelsi’s intuition led him to explore not only the music of others but also how others managed their talents; and those he admired the most were sought for advice. He discussed his ideas with them, showing them his compositional sketches, which they then corrected, edited, sometimes performed, and, occasionally, made ready for publication. Count Scelsi was known to have taken “lessons” from certain well-known professionals, whom he reimbursed for their time.
Some of these musicians’ names often appear among the dedicatees of Scelsi’s works. The influence of others could, perhaps, with punctilious fixation, be identified by their stylistic peculiarities. Others have come to the fore, especially after the composer’s death, to claim outright authorship of some of his compositions. From the mid-1940s Vieri Tosatti (1920–1999), a hugely talented and respected Roman composer, acted as Scelsi’s principle diaskeuast—editor, researcher, reviser. Ironically, Scelsi himself declared that he never composed in the traditional sense but only jotted down “messages” he’d received from an “elsewhere.” Therefore, others had to do the rest!
Such a declaration was snobbish if not outright irresponsible. It opened the door, especially after Scelsi’s death, to speculation about the identity of these “others” and the extent of their input into his music.
The title of this book, Music as Dream: Essays on Giacinto Scelsi, pays homage to Il sogno 101, the title Scelsi gave his autobiography, a chimeric, much-talked-about volume only published in recent years.4 This necessarily means that the contributors of the earlier essays in this collection had no direct knowledge of Il sogno 101’s content, while the others had to sort verifiable information from among Scelsi’s rhapsodic memories and oneiric digressions.
Music as Dream is a collection of writings tracing the development of scholarly criticism elicited by Scelsi’s music and philosophical thinking. Originally written in Italian, French, and German, these essays are made available here for the first time in English translation. The volume’s editors have selected contributions whose chronological gestation, influence, and eventual order of publication chronicle the evolution of critical thinking about Scelsi’s works since his death in 1988.
Chapter 1, “Is Composing a Cooperative Effort? A Historical Roundtable Discussion,” ignites the book’s critical discourse on Scelsi by evoking the gist of a vociferous querelle that erupted in the Italian press in January 1989, less than six months after Scelsi’s death, which came to be known as Il caso Scelsi (the Scelsi case). Composer and longtime Scelsi collaborator Vieri Tosatti published a blistering article in Il Giornale della Musica, claiming outright authorship of Scelsi’s works. The ensuing controversy reached its apogee in the pages of Piano Time’s March 1989 issue, in which musicologist Guido Zaccagnini (b. 1952) invited a group of noted composers, writers, and arts operators to discuss three questions: (1) “Could the practice of improvisation that in our century reached its golden moment in the 1960s be (if it ever was) still considered a valid compositional system?” (2) Is it “possible, just, and justified to speak of workshops, perhaps in the Renaissance meaning, when next to the master there is an apprentice? More concretely, is the hidden handwork within either serious music or commercial music an undisputed fact, plausible, if not efficient from a didactic standpoint?” (3) “The argument has moved a little toward the implications between the hierarchic positions of master and pupil. Are there instances during the past thirty years in which a pupil writes music in the name of the master or… for the master?”
This historical roundtable discussion included Heinz-Klaus Metzger (1932–2009), Salvatore Sciarrino (b. 1947), Mario Bortolotto (b. 1927), Sylvano Bussotti (b. 1931), Guido Turchi (1916–2010), Paolo Castaldi (b. 1930), Camillo Togni (1922–1993), Aldo Clementi (1925–2011), Franco Donatoni (1927–2000), Giorgio Vidusso (b. 1926), Gino Marinuzzi (1920–1996), Michele dall’Ongaro (b. 1957), Vieri Tosatti (1920–1999), and none other than John Cage (1912–1992).5
As the reader will soon discover, they engaged in a lively discussion that, if it did not settle the Scelsi-Tosatti controversy or swing opinions from one direction to another, touched on very important questions pertinent to creativity in general terms and provided the necessary impetus for scientific research on Scelsi’s prismatic personality and his cutting-edge ideas.
Born of just such research was Giacinto Scelsi: Viaggio al centro del suono, a collection of essays on the composer, published in 1993 under the editorship of Pierre Albert Castanet and Nicola Cisternino.6 From this volume we have borrowed an eloquently analytical study written in 1990 by Michelle Biget-Mainfroy, a French musicologist specializing in “gestural” piano writing. In her essay she offered an in-depth look at Scelsi’s complex piano output, which, at the time of writing, had just become available in print through the efforts of G. Schirmer in New York and Editions Salabert in Paris. While Biget-Mainfroy says that today she would adopt a different methodology to treat the subject, she offers in chapter 2, “Giacinto Scelsi: An Approach to Piano Writing,” the English translation of her original, unaltered, and unpublished French essay, Giacinto Scelsi: Une approche du piano, which was later published in Italian translation as Giacinto Scelsi: Un approccio al pianoforte.7
Immediately following, in chapter 3, is Gianmario Borio’s influential contribution, “Sound as Process: Scelsi and the Composers of Nuova Consonanza.” First appearing in Klaus Angermann’s edited collection Giacinto Scelsi: Im Innem des Tons, this essay was subsequently published in Italian as “Il suono come processo: Scelsi e i compositori di Nuova Consonanza.”8
Gianmario Borio prefaces this English edition of his twenty-year-old essay by noting that “this article was first published in 1992. In the following years, the field of research on Scelsi has made… significant progress, especially under the impulse of the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi. In order to avoid discontinuities in the argumentation, I decided to make no change to the content of my article for this publication in English. However, as a useful addition I wish to provide the reader with some advice regarding further reading on the topics under discussion. For instance, see Friedrich Jaecker’s ‘Giacinto Scelsi e Walther Klein’9 for the influence of Walther Klein, while for the relationship to the contemporary painters I suggest Alessandro Mastropietro’s ‘Action music (1955)… action painting: Su un nodo della produzione pianistica di Scelsi e su alcune ipotesi definitorie della sua tecnica compositiva.’10 Regarding the improvisation group Nuova Consonanza, see Thorsten Wagner’s Franco Evangelisti und die Improvisationsgruppe Nuova Consonanza; for Mario Bertoncini, see Mario Bertoncini’s Arpe Eolie and Other Useless Things; and, finally, for the structural functions of timbre see Gianmario Borio’s ‘Zur Vorgeschichte der Klangkomposition.’”11
Chapter 4, “Conceptual Reflections and Literary Evolution in Giacinto Scelsi’s Works,” was first written and published in 1999 by Alessandra Montali as “Rifles-sione concettuale e percorso letterario in Giacinto Scelsi.”12 It examines in detail Giacinto Scelsi’s theoretical and literary production, which can be divided into three periods: (1) the conceptual reflection of Sens de la musique and the theoretical reflection of Évolution du rythme and Évolution de l’harmonie in the 1940s; (2) the definition of his human and artistic identity and the approach to spirituality of Son et musique and Art et connaissance, along with the three collections of poems, Le poids net, L’archipel nocturne, and La conscience aigüe, in the 1950s; and, finally, (3) the hermetic stage of the production of Il sogno 101, Cercles, and Octologo in the 1980s.
The first period is characterized by Scelsi’s desire to define his artistic identity in relation to the contemporary musical panorama. His reflections concern both the proposition of a method of analysis based on the theory of fundamental elements—that is, the scrutiny of the rapport between the elements of music and the categories of images that the composer casts on the sound material—and its application to the study of the development of music between the end of the nineteenth century and the first thirty years of the twentieth. Thus the rhythmic and psychic elements come into the foreground, superseding the nineteenth-century predominance of the intellect and the emotions.
Scelsi examines the rhythmic element, inherent in music through both pulsation and temporal perception, as far as its depth is concerned. This will be a fundamental achievement for the development of Scelsi’s musical language along the path of infra-sonic research. The emancipation of this element is attributed to Liszt, Debussy, and Stravinsky, to jazz, and to the futurist experimentation. The psychic element, which is expressed through harmony (as Klang), is the projection in music of the deepest parts of conscience; it emerges in the work of Wagner, Debussy, Richard Strauss, and especially Scriabin. In this first stage Scelsi emerges as one who had great knowledge of the musical and cultural tendencies of his time and was seeking to establish a system of music criticism based on innovative categories of analysis.
Chapters 5, 7, and 8 (Martinis) and chapter 6 (Sciannameo) focus on Scelsi’s relationships with “forgotten” composers Giacinto Sallustio (1879–1938), Walther Klein (1882–1961), and Richard Falk (1878–1949), each of whom so greatly influenced Scelsi’s creative process as to be considered his early mentors. In chapter 5, “Who Is Walt(h)er Klein?,” and chapter 7, “In the New World,” Scelsi’s personal friend and confidant Luciano Martinis offers a string of commentaries that follow and enlighten the tortuous and intermittent Scelsi/Klein correspondence that took place between the early 1930s and the late 1940s. Personally entrusted by Scelsi to Martinis and preserved now in Martini’s personal archive, Le parole gelate, these letters reveal the profoundly human side to Scelsi’s personality at a crucial juncture in world history when millions of lives were disposed of amid the greatest of indifference. Scelsi’s prodigality toward his less-fortunate friends and colleagues, such as Klein and Falk during their struggle to escape political and racial persecutions and to rebuild their lives in America, is a story that merits being told and appreciated. This Luciano Martinis elegantly accomplishes through his uniquely intimate knowledge of the subject.13
In chapter 6, “Expressive Atonality,” Franco Sciannameo discusses another group of letters written in French by Scelsi in 1941 and addressed to Klein. They have been found among The Walther Klein Papers at the Jean Gray Hargrove Music Library of the University of California at Berkeley.14 This exchange is particularly relevant insofar as it documents the working method and the “per service” financial arrangement established between the two musicians. Furthermore, it reproduces in its entirety a piece for violin and piano sketched by Scelsi and completed by Klein that, according to Scelsi, was intented as a demonstration of a new compositional style he termed “expressive atonality.” The reader will be able to draw his or her own conclusion about Scelsi’s original thoughts and about his understanding of the levels and functions of one’s expertise involved in the creative process.
In chapter 7, “In the New World,” Luciano Martinis concludes his previous narrative on the Scelsi/Klein correspondence by presenting the reader with letters written in 1948 pertaining to a possible invitation extended to Giacinto Scelsi to lecture on Italian contemporary music at various American institutions.
Finally, in chapter 8, “A Forgotten Personage: Richard Falk,” Martinis introduces a truly important force in Scelsi’s lore. A German composer of Jewish ancestry, Falk lived and worked in Italy for many years as a Pergolesi specialist before enforcement of the Italian Racial Laws forced his emigration to the United States in 1938. On November 9–12, 2010, the Scelsi-based conference Suono nel Suono convened in Rome, where Martinis delivered a lecture on Falk and Scelsi, which has been translated here from the original Italian.
Chapter 9, “About and Around Rotativa: A Look at Young Scelsi” (Pellegrini), chapter 10, “Funziona? O non funziona? An Excursion through the Scelsi Archives” (Jaecker), and chapter 11, “The Art of ‘Writing’ Sound” (Marrocu), are analytical discussions based on the most recent findings in the now fully operating Scelsi archives at the Fondazione Isabella Scelsi. Pellegrini, Jaecker, and Marrocu reveal how twenty-first-century research methodologies and scholarship can untangle the mysteries Scelsi left behind amid those mazes of audiotapes and mounds of papers. The three scholars’ tours de force in genetic criticism apply investigative and interdisciplinary skills that espouse musicology and aesthetics to forensics and cultural analyses.
Pellegrini’s fascinating chapter focuses on the genesis, evolution, and metamorphosis of Rotativa (1933), Scelsi’s first work of importance. She emphasizes Giacinto Sallustio’s influence on a young Scelsi preparing for his debut on the international music scene and chronicles how Rotativa, Scelsi’s sort of idée fixe, was submitted for advice, performance, and revision(s) to various artists, including pianists Ornella Puliti Santoliquido (who premiered the piano solo version) and Pietro Scarpini (who helped arrange the two-piano version). In light of documents newly discovered in the Scelsi archive, Pellegrini specifically focuses on the two orchestral versions of this work.
German musicologist Friedrich Jaecker has been the catalyst in establishing systems for decoding, encoding, and cataloguing Scelsi’s audiotapes containing his original improvisations on the ondioline of all his works composed from 1960 onward. Jaecker’s sibylline chapter title, “Funziona? Non funziona?” (does it work or not?), refers to a Scelsi utterance captured on tape as he and a technician tested the functionality of a recording device. It is interesting how Scelsi’s “Muss es sein? Es muss sein!” makes no reference to a particular composition but to the mechanical (electronic) means that had become so essential to his creative process. Jaecker devotes a great portion of his chapter to a comparative analysis of the sources for “Anahit,” from the original fragments he was able to identify on tape to Vieri Tosatti’s finished score. Jaecker’s research work at the newly created Scelsi archives has served many younger scholars who have embarked on the truly scientific task of identifying myriad recorded improvisatory fragments recognizable in Scelsi’s most important works of his mature period. For instance, Sandro Marrocu’s chapter, “The Art of ‘Writing’ Sound,” is totally devoted to analyzing the practical approaches Tosatti, Filippini, and other collaborators had to devise in order to achieve the highest possible level of approximation in writing down in traditional fashion, according to Scelsi’s wishes, the composer’s conceptual sound sculptures. Thus we believe that Jaecker’s and Marrocu’s studies will serve as models for all scholars embarking on a scientific examination of the highly idiosyncratic creative process that is at the core of Scelsi’s music.
Music as Dream: Essays on Giacinto Scelsi concludes with a comprehensive bibliography (Pellegrini), discography (Caputo), and index of subjects and names.
